Local forms for the double A n A_{n} quiver
Hao Zhang

Abstract
. This paper studies the noncommutative singularity theory of the double A n A_{n} quiver Q n Q_{n} (with a single loop at each vertex), with applications to algebraic geometry and representation theory. We give various intrinsic definitions of a Type A A potential on Q n Q_{n} , then via coordinate changes we (1) prove a monomialization result that expresses these potentials in a particularly nice form, (2) prove that Type A A potentials precisely correspond to crepant resolutions of c ​ A n cA_{n} singularities, (3) solve the Realisation Conjecture of Brown–Wemyss in this setting. For n ≤ 3 n\leq 3 , we furthermore give a full classification of Type A A potentials (without loops) up to isomorphism, and those with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebras up to derived equivalence. There are various algebraic corollaries, including to certain tame algebras of quaternion type due to Erdmann, where we describe all basic algebras in the derived equivalence class.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

This paper is motivated by classification problems arising in the Minimal Model Program (MMP). Given a reasonable variety, the MMP seeks a nice representatives within its birational class. Such representatives are in general not unique; rather, they are related by codimension–two surgeries known as
flops

[
BCHM
]
.

Let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be a crepant resolution, where
\mathcal{R}
has compound Du Val (cDV) singularities.
Associated to
\uppi
is a noncommutative algebra
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
, called the
contraction algebra
, which represents the noncommutative deformation theory of the exceptional curves
[
DW1
]
.
The contraction algebra
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
is isomorphic to the Jacobi algebra of a quiver with some potential
[
V2
]
, and it classifies
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
complete locally if
\mathcal{R}
is furthermore isolated
[
JKM
]
.

This motivates classifying Jacobi algebras (equivalently, their potentials) on various quivers, as this immediately then classifies certain crepant resolutions. In this paper, we give various intrinsic algebraic definitions of a Type
A
potential on the double
A_{n}
quiver
Q_{n}
(with a single loop at each vertex). Then via coordinate changes, we prove a monomialization result that expresses these potentials in a particularly nice form, and prove that these potentials precisely correspond to
cA_{n}
crepant resolutions, which solves the Realisation Conjecture of Brown–Wemyss in Type A cases
[
BW
]
. There are further applications to representation theory.

Taken together, our results may be viewed as a noncommutative analogue of the classical classification of simple singularities by commutative polynomials
[
A1
]
, as well as a generalisation of the fact that the germ of a complex analytic hypersurface with an isolated singularity is determined by its Tjurina algebra
[
MY
]
.

1.2.
Main Results

We now summarise the main results of the paper.
For any fixed
n\geq 1
, consider the following quiver
Q_{n}
, which is the double of the usual
A_{n}
quiver, with a single loop at each vertex. Label the arrows of
Q_{n}
left to right, as illustrated below.

From this, define elements
\mathsf{x}_{i}
and
\mathsf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
as follows: first, set
b_{2i-1}
to be the trivial path at vertex
i
, for any
1\leq i\leq n
. Then for any
1\leq i\leq 2n-1
, set
\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a_{i}b_{i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{i}a_{i}
.

For example, in the case
n=3
,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{x}_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime}=a_{1}\\
\mathsf{x}_{3}=\mathsf{x}_{3}^{\prime}=a_{3}\\
\mathsf{x}_{5}=\mathsf{x}_{5}^{\prime}=a_{5}\end{array}\end{array}

whereas
\mathsf{x}_{2}=a_{2}b_{2}
,
\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime}=b_{2}a_{2}
, and
\mathsf{x}_{4}=a_{4}b_{4}
,
\mathsf{x}_{4}^{\prime}=b_{4}a_{4}
.

Given the above
\mathsf{x}_{i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}
, we first define a
reduced Type
A
potential
on
Q_{n}
to be any reduced potential
f
that contains the terms
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq 2n-2
. A
Type
A
potential
on
Q_{n}
is then defined in
4.4
. But for the purposes of this introduction, it suffices to work with the notion of a
monomialized Type
A
potential
on
Q_{n}
, defined to be any potential of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}

for some
\upkappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
. We prove in
4.20
and
4.23
that any Type
A
potential is isomorphic to a monomialized Type
A
potential, and so the above monomialized version suffices.

The first main result is that the complete Jacobi algebra (denoted
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}
) of any Type
A
potential on
Q_{n}
can be realized as the contraction algebra of a crepant resolution of some
cA_{n}
singularity.

Theorem 1.1

(
5.12
)
.

For any Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
where
n\geq 1
, there exists a crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
where
\mathcal{R}
is
cA_{n}
, such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
.

The Brown–Wemyss Realisation Conjecture
[
BW
]
predicts that if
f
is a potential whose Jacobi algebra
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is either finite-dimensional, or infinite-dimensional with at most linear growth in the successive quotients by powers of its Jacobi ideal, then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
arises as the contraction algebra of some crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is cDV.
Theorem
1.1
verifies this conjecture for all Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
, for any
n\geq 1
.

We then obtain the converse to
1.1
(see
5.15
), which shows that our definition of Type A potential is intrinsic. The definition of the quiver
Q_{n,I}

and Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolutions are given in §
4
and
5.11
.

Corollary 1.2

(
5.16
)
.

Let
f
be a reduced potential on
Q_{n,I}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is Type A.

(2)

There exists a Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
.

(3)

e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Moreover, there is a correspondence between crepant resolutions of
cA_{n}
singularities and our intrinsic noncommutative monomialized Type
A
potentials, as follows.

Corollary 1.3

(
5.19
)
.

For any
n
, the set of isomorphism classes of contraction algebras associated to crepant resolutions of
cA_{n}
singularities is equal to the set of isomorphism classes of Jacobi algebras of monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
.

Then, after restricting to those
cA_{n}
singularities which are isolated, we obtain the following consequence.

Theorem 1.4

(
5.21
)
.

For any
n
, there exists a one-to-one correspondence

derived equivalence classes of monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra.
isomorphism classes of isolated
cA_{n}
singularities
which admit a crepant resolution

1.3.
Special cases:
A_{3}

In the case of the double
A_{3}
quiver without loops, it is possible to describe the full isomorphism classes of Type
A
potentials, and the derived equivalence classes of those with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebras. This generalises
[
DWZ
,
E2
,
H2
]
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{x}=b_{1}a_{1},\ \mathsf{y}=a_{2}b_{2}\end{array}\end{array}

Given two potentials
f
and
g
on
Q
, we say that
f
is
isomorphic
to
g
, written
f\cong g
, if the corresponding Jacobi algebras are isomorphic (see
2.8
). Similarly, we say that
f
is
derived equivalent
to
g
, written
f\simeq g
, if the corresponding Jacobi algebras are derived equivalent (see
5.20
).

Theorem 1.5

(
6.19
)
.

Any Type A potential on
Q
must be isomorphic to an algebra in one of the following isomorphism classes of potentials:

(1)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
for any
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
.

(2)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{r}
for any
r\geq 3
.

(3)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\cong\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
for any
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

(4)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

(5)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\cong\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
for any
p\geq 2
.

(6)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
.

The Jacobi algebras of these potentials are all mutually non-isomorphic (except those isomorphisms stated), and in particular the Jacobi algebras with different parameters in the same item are non-isomorphic.

The Jacobi algebras in
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
are realized by crepant resolutions of isolated
cA_{3}
singularities, and those in
(4)
,
(5)
,
(6)
are realized by crepant resolutions of non-isolated
cA_{3}
singularities.

Theorem 1.6

(
6.28
)
.

The following groups the Type
A
potentials on
Q
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra into sets, where all the Jacobi algebras in a given set are derived equivalent.

(1)

\{\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda^{\prime}\mathsf{y}^{2}

\mid

\uplambda^{\prime}=\uplambda,\frac{1-4\uplambda}{4},\frac{1}{4(1-4\uplambda)},\frac{\uplambda}{4\uplambda-1},\frac{4\uplambda-1}{16\uplambda},\frac{1}{16\uplambda}\}
for any
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
.

(2)

\{\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{2}
,
\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
,
\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}\}
for
p\geq 3
.

(3)

\{\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}
,
\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}\}
for
p\geq 3
and
q\geq 3
.

Moreover, the Jacobi algebras of the sets in
(1)–(3)
are all mutually not derived equivalent, and in particular the Jacobi algebras of different sets in the same item are not derived equivalent.
In
(1)
there are no further basic algebras in the derived equivalence class, whereas in
(2)–(3)
there are an additional finite number of basic algebras in the derived equivalence class.

Next, recall the definition of the quaternion type quiver algebra
A_{p,q}(\upmu)
in
[
E2
,
H2
]
, which is the completion of the path algebra of the quiver
Q
modulo the relations

a_{1}a_{2}b_{2}-(a_{1}b_{1})^{p-1}a_{1},b_{2}b_{1}a_{1}-\upmu(b_{2}a_{2})^{q-1}b_{2},a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}-(b_{1}a_{1})^{p-1}b_{1},b_{1}a_{1}a_{2}-\upmu(a_{2}b_{2})^{q-1}a_{2},

where
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}
and
p,q\geq 2
. Note we have fewer relations than in
[
E2
,
H2
]
since we are working with the completion. In fact
A_{p,q}(\upmu)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)
, where

f=\frac{1}{p}\mathsf{x}^{p}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{\upmu}{q}\mathsf{y}^{q}\cong\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+(-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\upmu\mathsf{y}^{q}.

The following improves various results of Erdmann and Holm
[
E2
,
H2
]
.

Corollary 1.7

(
6.31
)
.

The following groups those algebras
A_{p,q}(\upmu)
which are finite-dimensional into sets, where all the algebras in a given set are derived equivalent.

(1)

\{A_{2,2}(\upmu^{\prime})

\mid

\upmu^{\prime}=\upmu,1-\upmu,\frac{1}{1-\upmu},\frac{\upmu}{\upmu-1},\frac{\upmu-1}{\upmu},\frac{1}{\upmu}\}
for
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

(2)

\{A_{p,q}(1)
,
A_{q,p}(1)\}
for
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

Moreover, the algebras in different sets in
(1)–(2)
are all mutually not derived equivalent.
In
(1)
there are no further basic algebras in the derived equivalence class, whereas in
(2)
there are an additional finite number of basic algebras in the derived equivalence class.

Among the families of potentials in
1.6
, the quadratic case in (1) exhibits a particularly rich structure, with derived equivalence classes given by finite sets of parameters related by explicit transformations.
Corollary
1.7
presents the same family via quaternion type algebras
A_{2,2}(\upmu)
, making the underlying
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-symmetry explicit.
In §
6.2
, we give a geometric explanation of this symmetry in terms of flops of crepant resolutions of
cA_{3}
singularities, and relate it to the classical
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-action on the Legendre parameter of elliptic curves.

Conventions

Throughout this paper, we work over the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, which is necessary for various geometric statements in §
3
.
The definitions of
Q_{n,I}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}
are fundamental, and are repeated in §
4
.
We also adopt the following notation.

(1)

Throughout,
n
is the number of vertices in the quiver
Q_{n,I}
, and
I\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is the set of vertices without loops in
Q_{n,I}
.

(2)

Set
m\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2n-1-|I|
, which equals the number of
\mathsf{x}_{i}
in
Q_{n,I}
.

(3)

Vector space dimension will be written
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}V
.

(4)

We write
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for the multiplicative group of nonzero complex numbers.

2.
Algebraic Preliminaries

To set notation, let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},t,h)
be a
quiver
, where
Q_{0}
is a finite set of vertices,
Q_{1}
is a finite set of arrows, and
t,h\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
are the tail and head maps.
A
loop

a
is an arrow satisfying
h(a)=t(a)
, and
a
path
is a formal expression
a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}
where
h(a_{i})=t(a_{i+1})
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
. A path
a
is
cyclic
if
h(a)=t(a)
.

Let
k
be a field. The
complete path algebra

k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
is the completion of the usual path algebra
kQ
with respect to the arrow ideal. That is, the elements of
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
are possibly infinite
k
-linear combinations of paths in
Q
.

Write
\mathfrak{m}_{Q}
, or simply
\mathfrak{m}
, for the two-sided ideal of
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
generated by the elements of
Q_{1}
, and write
A_{Q}
, or simply
A
, for the
k
-span of the elements of
Q_{1}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose that Q is a quiver with arrow span
A
.

(1)

A
relation
of
Q
is a
k
-linear combination of paths in
Q
, each with the same head and tail.

(2)

Given finitely many relations
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
, let
R
be the closure in
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
of the two-sided ideal
kQR_{1}kQ+\cdots+kQR_{n}kQ
.
We call
(Q,R)
a
quiver with relations
, and
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R
its complete path algebra.

(3)

A
quiver with potential

(
QP
for short
)
is a pair
(Q,W)
, where
W
is a
k
-linear combination of cyclic paths in
Q
.

(4)

For any
n\geq 1
, set
W_{n}
to be the
nth
homogeneous component of
W
with respect to the path length.

(5)

For each
a\in Q_{1}
and cyclic path
a_{1}\dots a_{d}
in
Q
, define the
cyclic derivative
as

\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}\left(a_{1}\ldots a_{d}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{a,a_{i}}a_{i+1}\ldots a_{d}a_{1}\ldots a_{i-1}

(where
\delta_{a,a_{i}}
is the Kronecker delta), and then extend
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}
by linearity.

(6)

The
Jacobi ideal

J(W)
is the closure of the two-sided ideal of
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
generated by
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}W
for all
a\in Q_{1}
.

(7)

The
Jacobi algebra

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,W)
or
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(A,W)
is the quotient
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/J(W)
. We write
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(W)
when the quiver
Q
is obvious.

(8)

For every potential
W
, write
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}W
for the
k
-span of
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}W
for all
a\in Q_{1}
.

(9)

We call a QP
(Q,W)

reduced
if
W_{2}=0
. It is called
trivial
if
W_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 3
, and further
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}W=A
.

Example 2.2
.

Consider the one loop quiver
Q
with potential
W=a^{2}
,

\scriptstyle a

The complete path algebra
k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
is
k[\kern-1.0pt[a]\kern-1.0pt]
. In this case,
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,W)\cong k[\kern-1.0pt[a]\kern-1.0pt]/(a)\cong k
since
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(a^{2})=2a
. Since
W_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 3
and
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}W=ka=A_{Q}
, this
QP

(Q,W)
is trivial.

Notation 2.3
.

For
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, let
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\}
denote the commutator subspace of
\mathcal{A}
, consisting of finite sums

\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}(p_{i}q_{i}-q_{i}p_{i}),

with
p_{i},q_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
k_{i}\in k
.
Write
\{\kern-3.0pt\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\}\kern-3.0pt\}
for the closure of the commutator vector space
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Two potentials
W
and
W^{\prime}
are
cyclically equivalent
(written
W\sim W^{\prime}
) if
W-W^{\prime}\in\{\kern-3.0pt\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}\}\kern-3.0pt\}
. We write
W\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}W^{\prime}
if
W\sim W^{\prime}
and
W-W^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{m}^{i}
.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that if two potentials
W
and
W^{\prime}
are cyclically equivalent, then
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}W=\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}W^{\prime}
for all
a\in Q_{1}
, and hence
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,W)=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,W^{\prime})

[
DWZ
, 3.3]
. Since we aim to classify the Jacobi algebras up to isomorphism, we always consider the potentials up to cyclic equivalence.

Given an algebra homomorphism
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q^{\prime}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
such that
\varphi|_{k}=id
which sends
\mathfrak{m}_{Q}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{Q^{\prime}}
, write
\left.\varphi\right|_{A_{Q}}=\left(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\right)
where
\varphi_{1}\colon A_{Q}\rightarrow A_{Q^{\prime}}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon A_{Q}\rightarrow\mathfrak{m}_{Q^{\prime}}^{2}
are
k
-module homomorphisms.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
DWZ
, 2.4]

Given two quivers
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, any pair
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
of k-module homomorphisms
\varphi_{1}\colon A_{Q}\rightarrow A_{Q^{\prime}}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon A_{Q}\rightarrow\mathfrak{m}_{Q^{\prime}}^{2}
gives rise to a unique homomorphism of algebras
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q^{\prime}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
such that
\varphi|_{k}=id
and
\left.\varphi\right|_{A_{Q}}=\left(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\right)
. Furthermore,
\varphi
is an isomorphism if and only if
\varphi_{1}
is a
k
-module isomorphism
A_{Q}\rightarrow A_{Q^{\prime}}
.

From the above result, whenever we construct an automorphism
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
in §
4
and §
6
, it will always be the case that
\varphi|_{k}=id
, so we will only describe
\varphi|_{A_{Q}}
.

Definition 2.7
.

An algebra homomorphism
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
is called a
unitriangular automorphism
if
\varphi|_{k}=id
and
\varphi_{1}=id
. For
i\geq 1
, we say that
\varphi
has
depth
i
provided that
\varphi_{2}(a)\in\mathfrak{m}_{Q}^{i+1}
for all
a\in Q_{1}
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
f
and
g
be potentials on a quiver
Q
.

(1)

We say that
f
is
isomorphic
to
g
(written
f\cong g
) if their Jacobi algebras
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)
as algebras.

(2)

If there exists an algebra isomorphism
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
such that
\varphi|_{k}=id
and
\varphi(f)=g
, then
we write
\varphi\colon f\mapsto g
and say that
f
is
equivalent
to
g
.

(3)

If there exists an algebra isomorphism
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
such that
\varphi|_{k}=id
and
\varphi(f)\sim g
, then we write
\varphi\colon f\rightsquigarrow g
and say that
f
is
right-equivalent
to
g
.

(4)

For
i\geq 1
, if there exists a unitriangular
\varphi\colon k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\rightarrow k\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
such that
\varphi
has depth greater than or equal to
i
, and further
\varphi(f)\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}g
, then we write
\varphi\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}g
and say that
f
is
path degree
i
right-equivalent
to
g
.

We follow the definition of right-equivalence in
[
DWZ
, 4.2]
. Moreover, from
[
DWZ
, p12]
,
f\rightsquigarrow g
induces
f\cong g
, and further a finite sequence of right-equivalences is still a right-equivalence. By
2.6
,
f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}g
induces
f\rightsquigarrow g
.
Thus, together with the above definition, we obtain

f\sim g\quad\text{or}\quad f\mapsto g\quad\text{or}\quad f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}g\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;f\rightsquigarrow g\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;f\cong g.

The Jacobi algebra isomorphism
\cong
is the equivalence relation that we aim to classify the potentials up to.
The strategy is to start with a potential
f
, then transform it by a sequence of automorphisms which chase terms into higher and higher degrees. Composing this sequence of automorphisms then gives a single automorphism which takes
f
to the desired form (see §
4
and §
6.1
).

The subtle point is that at each stage, the automorphism only gives the desired potential up to cyclic equivalence (e.g.
\rightsquigarrow
,
\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}
).
Given an infinite sequence of path degree
i
right-equivalences
\varphi_{i}\colon f_{i}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{i+1}
for
i\geq 1
, then the following asserts that
\lim f_{i}
exists, and further there exists a right-equivalence
F\colon f_{1}\rightsquigarrow\lim f_{i}
.

Theorem 2.9
.

[
BW
, 2.9]

Let
f
be a potential, and set
f_{1}=f
. Suppose that there exist elements
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
, …and automorphisms
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
, …, such that

(1)

Every
\varphi_{i}
is unitriangular of depth of
\geq i
, and

(2)

\varphi_{i}(f_{i})\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f_{i+1}
, for all
i\geq 1
.

Then
\lim f_{i}
exists, and there exists an automorphism
F
such that
F(f)\sim\lim f_{i}
.

3.
Geometric Preliminaries

In this section we recall basic concepts, including NCCRs and contraction algebras, and then summarise some facts about
cA_{n}
singularities and their NCCRs from
[
IW1
]
.

Throughout the remainder of the paper, we reserve the notation
\mathcal{R}
for complete local
\mathbb{C}
-algebras of the following form.

Definition 3.1
.

A complete local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{R}
is called a
compound Du Val (cDV) singularity
if

\mathcal{R}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,t]\kern-1.0pt]}{f+tg}

where
f\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x]\kern-1.0pt]
defines a Du Val, or equivalently Kleinian, surface singularity and
g\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,t]\kern-1.0pt]
is arbitrary.

Definition 3.2
.

A projective birational morphism
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
is called a
crepant partial resolution
if
\omega_{\mathcal{X}}\cong\uppi^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{R}}
. When
\mathcal{X}
is furthermore smooth, we call
\uppi
a
crepant resolution
.
If
\mathcal{R}
is isolated, crepant partial resolutions and crepant resolutions are equivalently called
flopping contractions
and
smooth flopping contractions
, respectively
(
see e.g.
[
R
, §1]
)
.

3.1.
NCCRs and Contraction Algebras

In this subsection, we will introduce NCCRs and contraction algebras of cDV singularities and then recall some associated theorems.

We will write
\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}
for the category of maximal Cohen–Macaulay
\mathcal{R}
-modules and
\underline{\mathrm{CM}}\,\mathcal{R}
for the stable category of
\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 3.3
.

A
noncommutative crepant resolution (NCCR)
of
\mathcal{R}
is a ring of the form
\Lambda(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
for some finitely generated reflexive
\mathcal{R}
-module M, such that
\Lambda(M)\in\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}
and has finite global dimension.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is cDV, there is a bijection between NCCRs of
\mathcal{R}
and crepant resolutions of

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
; see
[
W2
]
:

\left\{\,M\in\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}\ \middle|\ \End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\ \text{is an NCCR}\,\right\}\ \longleftrightarrow\ \left\{\,\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}\ \text{a crepant resolution}\,\right\}.

The passage from left to right takes a given
M
and associates a certain moduli space of representations of
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
. In particular, passing from a crepant resolution to the corresponding NCCR retains the geometric
information relevant for our purposes.

We next explain the passage from right to left in detail.
Let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be a crepant resolution with exceptional curves
\mathrm{C}_{1},\dots,\mathrm{C}_{n}
. For any
1\leq i\leq n
, there is a unique bundle
\mathcal{M}_{i}
on
\mathcal{X}

[
V1
, 3.5.4]
,
and

\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{i}

is a tilting bundle on
\mathcal{X}

[
V1
, 3.5.5]
. Pushing forward via
\uppi
gives
\uppi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathcal{R}
and
\uppi_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{i})=M_{i}
for some
\mathcal{R}
-module
M_{i}
. Set
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}
. Then
M\in\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}
and
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
is an NCCR. Thus, there is an equivalent definition of NCCR associated to the crepant resolution
\uppi
,

\Lambda(\uppi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\End_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{M})\cong\End_{\mathcal{R}}\left(M\right)=\Lambda(M),

where the isomorphism follows from the crepancy of
\uppi
; see
[
V1
, 3.2.10]
.

The contraction algebra can be defined as a quotient of the NCCR as follows.

Definition 3.4
.

Define the
contraction algebra
associated to a crepant resolution
\uppi
to be the stable endomorphism algebra

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)=\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)/\langle\mathcal{R}\rangle,

where
\langle\mathcal{R}\rangle
denotes the two-sided ideal consisting of all morphisms which factor through
\add\mathcal{R}
. We also write
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(M)
for
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
.

The difference between flopping contractions and divisor-to-curve contractions can be detected by the finite dimensionality (or otherwise) of the contraction algebra as follows.

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose that
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
is a crepant partial resolution, and write
Z
for the locus in
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
for which
\uppi
is not an isomorphism. Then
\operatorname{Supp}_{\mathcal{R}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)=Z
, and

\uppi\text{ is a flopping contraction}\iff\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)<\infty.

If moreover
\mathcal{X}
is smooth, then these conditions are equivalent to
\mathcal{R}
being an isolated singularity.

Proof.

The equivalences preceding the final assertion are established in
[
DW2
, 4.8]
, so we only justify the case where
\mathcal{X}
is smooth.
If
\mathcal{X}
is smooth, then the locus over which
\uppi
fails to be an isomorphism coincides with the singular locus of
\mathcal{R}
, that is,
Z=\Sing\mathcal{R}
. Hence
\Supp_{\mathcal{R}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)=\Sing\mathcal{R}
.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is complete local and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{R}
, it follows from
[
E1
, 2.13, 2.15, 2.17]
that

\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)<\infty\iff\Supp_{\mathcal{R}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)=V(\mathfrak{m}),

where
\mathfrak{m}
denotes the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{R}
and
V(\mathfrak{m})\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
is the corresponding Zariski closed point.
Therefore,
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)<\infty\iff\Sing\mathcal{R}=V(\mathfrak{m})
, which is equivalent to
\mathcal{R}
having an isolated singularity.
∎

Donovan and Wemyss conjectured that the contraction algebra distinguishes the analytic type of the flop
[
DW1
, 1.4]
, which has been proved as follows.

Theorem 3.6
.

[
JKM
, A.2]

Let
\uppi_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
be smooth flopping contractions of isolated
cDV

\mathcal{R}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Then
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
are derived equivalent if and only if the singularities
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are isomorphic.

The following result connects the derived equivalence of contraction algebras with the flops in geometry.

Theorem 3.7
.

[
A2
, 5.2.2]

Given a flopping contraction
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
where
\mathcal{R}
is isolated
cDV
, then the basic algebras derived equivalent to
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
are precisely those
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{\prime})
where
\uppi^{\prime}
is obtained by a sequence of iterated flops from
\uppi
. In particular, there are finitely many such algebras.

3.2.
cA_{n}
singularities

This subsection summarises several facts about
cA_{n}
singularities and their NCCRs in
[
IW2
]
.

Every
cA_{t-1}
singularity
\mathcal{R}
has the following form

\mathcal{R}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-g_{0}g_{1}\dots g_{n}},

where
t
is the order of the power series
g_{0}g_{1}\dots g_{n}
and each
g_{i}
is a prime element of
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
. Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
admits a crepant resolution if and only if each
g_{i}
has a linear term by e.g.
[
IW2
, 5.1]
.

In this subsection we restrict to those
\mathcal{R}
that admit a crepant resolution. In this case each
g_{i}
has a linear term, and hence the order
t
equals the number of factors, so
t=n+1
and
\mathcal{R}
is a
cA_{n}
singularity.
Consider the
\mathrm{CM}

\mathcal{R}
-module

M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,g_{0})\oplus(u,g_{0}g_{1})\oplus\ldots\oplus(u,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}g_{i}),

and let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be the associated crepant resolution in
3.9
below.
We fix
\mathcal{R}
,
M
and
\uppi
throughout this subsection.

Notation 3.8
.

We adopt the following notation.

(1)

Consider the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
. For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
, set

M^{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,g_{\sigma(0)})\oplus(u,g_{\sigma(0)}g_{\sigma(1)})\oplus\ldots\oplus(u,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}g_{\sigma(i)})\in\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}.

(2)

Write
\uppi^{\sigma}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
for the associated crepant resolution of
M^{\sigma}
in
3.9
below.

(3)

Now let
k\geq 1
and consider the
k
-tuple
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
with each
1\leq r_{i}\leq n
. Set

\sigma(\mathbf{r})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(r_{k},r_{k}+1)\cdots(r_{2},r_{2}+1)(r_{1},r_{1}+1)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1},

and
M^{\mathbf{r}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}M^{\sigma(\mathbf{r})}
. Write
\uppi^{\mathbf{r}}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
for
\uppi^{\sigma(\mathbf{r})}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\sigma(\mathbf{r})}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
.

(4)

For
1\leq i\leq n
, write
\uppi^{i}
,
\mathcal{X}^{i}
and
M^{i}
for
\uppi^{(i)}
,
\mathcal{X}^{(i)}
and
M^{(i)}
respectively.

The following two results describe NCCRs of crepant resolutions of
cA_{n}
singularities.

Proposition 3.9
.

[
IW2
, 5.1, 5.27]

The
\mathrm{CM}

\mathcal{R}
-modules that satisfy
3.3
are precisely
M^{\sigma}
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
. Moreover, there is a bijection satisfying
\Lambda(\uppi^{\sigma})\cong\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{\sigma})
,

\displaystyle\left\{M^{\sigma}\mid\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\text{ crepant resolutions of }\mathcal{R}\right\},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad M^{\sigma}\longleftrightarrow\uppi^{\sigma}:\mathcal{X}^{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R},

where
\mathcal{X}^{\sigma}
is given pictorially by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{X}^{\sigma}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to178.36pt{\vbox to29.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.10693pt\lower-12.963pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Proposition 3.10
.

[
IW2
,
W2
]

Given any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
, let
\uppi^{\sigma}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\sigma}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be the associated crepant resolution. Then the
NCCR

\Lambda(\uppi^{\sigma})
can be presented as the following quiver (with possible loops):

1
2
3
\ldots
n-1
n
0
\scriptstyle inc
\scriptstyle g_{\sigma(0)}
\scriptstyle inc
\scriptstyle g_{\sigma(1)}
\scriptstyle inc
\scriptstyle g_{\sigma(2)}
\scriptstyle inc
\scriptstyle g_{\sigma(n-1)}
\scriptstyle\frac{g_{\sigma(n)}}{u}
\scriptstyle u

where the vertex
0
represents
\mathcal{R}
and the vertex
i
represents
(u,\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}g_{\sigma(j)})
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

There is a loop labelled
t
at vertex
0
if and only if
(g_{\sigma(0)},g_{\sigma(n)})\subsetneq(x,y)
and there exists
t\in(x,y)
such that
(g_{\sigma(0)},g_{\sigma(n)},t)=(x,y)
in the ring
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
.
Further, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, the possible loops at vertex
i
are given by the following rules:

(1)

the normal bundle of curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)\iff

(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)})=(x,y)
in
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]

\iff
add no loop at vertex
i
.

(2)

the normal bundle of curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(-2)\oplus\mathcal{O}

\iff

(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)})\subsetneq(x,y)
and there exists
t\in(x,y)
such that
(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)},t)=(x,y)
in
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]

\iff
add a loop labelled
t
at vertex
i
.

Proof.

In general,
[
IW2
,
W2
]
shows that either (1), (2) or the following third case holds.

(3)

(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)})\subsetneq(x,y)
and there is no
t
such that (2)
\iff
add two loops labelled
x
and
y
at vertex
i
.

We now prove that (3) is impossible when
\mathcal{R}
is
cA_{n}
and admits a crepant resolution. If there exist two loops at some vertex
i
, then
(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)})\subsetneq(x,y)
and there exists no
t\in(x,y)
that satisfies
(g_{\sigma(i-1)},g_{\sigma(i)},t)=(x,y)
. Hence both
g_{\sigma(i-1)}
and
g_{\sigma(i)}
must belong to
(x,y)^{2}
. But this contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{R}
admits a crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

The following asserts that isomorphisms between contraction algebras of
cA_{n}
singularities can only map
e_{i}
to
e_{i}
or
e_{n+1-i}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, where
e_{i}
denotes the trivial path at the vertex
i
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\uppi_{k}\colon\mathcal{X}_{k}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{k}
be two crepant resolutions of
cA_{n}
singularities
\mathcal{R}_{k}
for
k=1,2
. If there exists an algebra isomorphism
\phi\colon\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, then
\phi
must belong to one of the following two cases:

(1)

\phi(e_{i})=e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
,

(2)

\phi(e_{i})=e_{n+1-i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

Write
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{k})
for the category of finitely generated right
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{k})
-modules for
k=1,2
. Write
\mathcal{S}_{i}
for the simple
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
-module corresponding to the vertex
i
in the quiver of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
(see
[
HW
, §5.2]
). Similarly, write
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
for the simple
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
-module corresponding to the vertex
i
in the quiver of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
.
By
[
W2
, 2.11]
, for each
1\leq i\leq n
the simple module
\mathcal{S}_{i}
corresponds to the
i
-th exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
in
\mathcal{X}_{1}
. The same holds for
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
and the exceptional curves in
\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

The algebra isomorphism
\phi
induces an equivalence
\varphi\colon\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
. By Morita theory,
\varphi
maps simple modules to simple modules, and furthermore there is a
\sigma
in the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{i})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}
.

We next use the intersection diagram of exceptional curves for a
cA_{n}
singularity—namely, a Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n}
, obtained from the diagram in
3.10
by removing the vertex
0
—together with the correspondence between
\mathcal{S}_{i}
,
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
and exceptional curves, to constrain the permutation
\sigma
.

Since
\uppi_{1}
is a crepant resolution of a
cA_{n}
singularity,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is the unique simple
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
-module other than
\mathcal{S}_{1}
that satisfies
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})}(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2})\neq 0
by
3.10
and the intersection theory of
[
W2
, 2.15]
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is equivalent to
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, there exists unique simple
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
-module
\mathcal{T}
other than
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(1)}
such that
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(1)},\mathcal{T})\neq 0
. Thus the exceptional curve
\sigma(1)
in
\uppi_{2}
must be a edge curve, by
3.10
and the intersection theory of
[
W2
, 2.15]
. Thus
\sigma(1)=1
or
n
. We split the proof into two cases.

(1)
\sigma(1)=1
.

Since
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})}(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2})\neq 0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is equivalent to
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, we have
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(1)},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(2)})\neq 0
, and so
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(2)})\neq 0
.
Thus the curve
\sigma(2)
in
\uppi_{2}
must be connected to the curve
\sigma(1)=1
, and so
\sigma(2)=2
by
3.10
and the intersection theory of
[
W2
, 2.15]
.
Repeating the same process, we can prove
\sigma(i)=i
, and so
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{i})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{i}
, and furthermore
\phi(e_{i})=e_{i}
for each
i
.

(2)
\sigma(1)=n
.

Since
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})}(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2})\neq 0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is equivalent to
\mathop{\mathrm{mod}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, we have
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(1)},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(2)})\neq 0
, and so
\Ext^{1}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\sigma(2)})\neq 0
.
Thus the curve
\sigma(2)
in
\uppi_{2}
must be connected to the curve
\sigma(1)=n
, and so
\sigma(2)=n-1
by
3.10
and the intersection theory of
[
W2
, 2.15]
.
Repeating the same process, we can prove
\sigma(i)=n+1-i
, and so
\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{i})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{n+1-i}
, and furthermore
\phi(e_{i})=e_{n+1-i}
for each
i
.
∎

4.
Monomialization

This section introduces the quiver
Q_{n,I}
and Type
A
potentials.
In §
4.1
we prove that every reduced Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
is right-equivalent to a reduced monomialised Type
A
potential (see
4.20
), which is the starting point for the geometric realisation in §
5
.
Finally, §
4.2
shows that any monomialised Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
is isomorphic to a (possibly non-reduced) monomialised Type
A
potential on
Q_{n}
(see
4.23
), so it suffices to work on
Q_{n}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a quiver
Q
, let
f
,
g
and
h
be potentials on
Q
. Write
f=\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}c_{i}
as a linear combination of cycles where each
\uplambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(1)

We write
V(f)
for the
\mathbb{C}
-span of all cycles
c
such that
c\sim c_{i}
for some
i
.

(2)

We say
f
is
orthogonal
to
g
if
V(f)\cap V(g)=\{0\}
.

(3)

We write
f=g\oplus h
if
f=g+h
and
g
is orthogonal to
h
.

(4)

We say
f

contains

g
if
f\sim\uplambda g\oplus h^{\prime}
for some
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and potential
h^{\prime}
.

Recall the definition of the quiver
Q_{n}
in
1.2
, which is the double of the usual
A_{n}
quiver, with a single loop at each vertex as follows.

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

For any
I\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, define the quiver
Q_{n,I}
by removing the loop in
Q_{n}
at each vertex
i\in I
, and then relabel
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
from left to right.
As before, we now set
b_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}e_{i}
whenever
a_{i}
is a loop in
Q_{n,I}
, and set
\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a_{i}b_{i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{i}a_{i}
for each
i
. In particular, the quiver
Q_{n,I}
contains
2n-1-|I|
paths of each type
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
,
\mathsf{x}_{i}
, and
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}
.

For example,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{25.16035pt}{23.49406pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 34.81 32.51)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
