{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-75.11528pt}{-0.13889pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -103.94 -0.19)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}b_{1}=e_{1},\ \mathsf{x}_{1}=\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime}=a_{1}\\
b_{4}=e_{4},\ \mathsf{x}_{4}=\mathsf{x}_{4}^{\prime}=a_{4}\\
\end{array}\end{array}

whereas
\mathsf{x}_{2}=a_{2}b_{2}
,
\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime}=b_{2}a_{2}
, and
\mathsf{x}_{3}=a_{3}b_{3}
,
\mathsf{x}_{3}^{\prime}=b_{3}a_{3}
.
In this example, the quiver
Q_{3,\{2\}}
is obtained by removing the loop at vertex
2
in
Q_{3}
.
More precisely, the quiver
Q_{3}
contains arrows
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 5
.
Since the loop at vertex
2
corresponds to the arrows
a_{3}
and
b_{3}
, these are removed.
The arrows
a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},
and
b_{2}
are left unchanged, while the indices of

a_{4},b_{4},a_{5},
and
b_{5}
in
Q_{3}
are shifted down by one, becoming

a_{3},b_{3},a_{4},
and
b_{4}
in
Q_{3,\{2\}}
.

Notation 4.2
.

Throughout this paper,
n
denotes the number of vertices of
Q_{n,I}
, and
I\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is the set of vertices
without
loop in
Q_{n,I}
. Note that
Q_{n,\emptyset}
is just
Q_{n}
. Furthermore, set
m\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2n-1-|I|
, which equals the number of
\mathsf{x}_{i}
in
Q_{n,I}

We now give several definitions and notations with respect to
Q_{n,I}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Given a cycle
c
on
Q_{n,I}
, write
c
as a composition of arrows.
For
1\leq i\leq m
, let
q_{i}
be the number of times
a_{i}
appears in this composition. Then set
\mathbf{T}(c)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(q_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{m})
, and define the
degree
of
c
to be
\mathrm{deg}(c)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}q_{i}
.

Definition 4.4
.

We say that a potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
is
reduced Type
A
if
f
is reduced in the sense of
2.1
and
f
contains
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
in the sense of
4.1
for each
1\leq i\leq m-1
. Further, we say that a (possibly non-reduced) potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
is
Type
A

if

(1)

All terms of
f
have degrees greater than or equal to two in the sense of
4.3
.

(2)

The reduced part
f_{\textnormal{red}}
is Type
A
on
Q_{n,I^{\prime}}
for some
I\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

The Splitting Theorem
[
DWZ
, 4.6]
gives the existence and uniqueness of
f_{\textnormal{red}}
, so
4.4
is well defined.

Lemma 4.5
.

Given any potential
f\sim\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\uplambda_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h
where each
\uplambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on
Q_{n,I}
, there exists a right-equivalence
f\rightsquigarrow f^{\prime}
such that

f^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+g

for some potential
g
.

Proof.

Applying
a_{i}\mapsto k_{i}a_{i}
where
k_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
for each
1\leq i\leq m
gives

f\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}k_{i}k_{i+1}\uplambda_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+g,

for some potential
g
. Since each
\uplambda_{i}\neq 0
, we can always find some
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{m})
that ensures
k_{i}k_{i+1}\uplambda_{i}=1
holds for
1\leq i\leq m-1
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The above lemma shows that any reduced Type
A
potential
f
can be transformed to the form of
\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g
for some potential
g
. Thus, in this paper, for any reduced Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, we always assume that
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g
.

Definition 4.7
.

We call a potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}

monomialized Type
A
if
f\sim\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}
for some
\upkappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Given any monomialized Type
A
potential
f
, it is clear that
f
is Type A. Moreover,
f
is reduced if and only if
\upkappa_{s2}=0
whenever
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop.

Definition 4.8
.

Given a cycle
c
on
Q_{n,I}
, consider
\mathbf{T}(c)
from
4.3
.
Define
\textnormal{left}(c)
to be the smallest
i
such that
q_{i}>0
, and
\textnormal{right}(c)
to be the largest
i
such that
q_{i}>0
. We then define the
length
of
c
by
\mathrm{len}(c)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{right}(c)-\textnormal{left}(c)+1
.

From the above definition, if
\mathrm{len}(c)=1
then
c\sim\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}
for some
1\leq i\leq m
and
j\geq 1
.

Notation 4.9
.

We adopt the following notation regarding cycles on
Q_{n,I}
.

(1)

Write
F
for the
\mathbb{C}
-span of
\{c\mid c\text{ is a cycle with }\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 1\}
where the degree is defined in
4.3
.

(2)

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, write
D_{i}
for the
\mathbb{C}
-span of
\{c\mid\text{c is a cycle with }\mathrm{deg}(c)=i\}
.

(3)

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, write
L_{i}
for the
\mathbb{C}
-span of
\{c\mid\text{c is a cycle with }\mathrm{len}(c)=i\}
where length is defined in
4.8
.

(4)

For any
i
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
1\leq i\leq j\leq m
, write
V_{ij}
for the
\mathbb{C}
-span of
\{c\mid\text{c is a cycle with }\textnormal{left}(c)=i\text{ and }\textnormal{right}(c)=j\}
.

It is clear that
F=\bigoplus_{i}D_{i}
,
F=\bigoplus_{i}L_{i}
and
F=\bigoplus_{i\leq j}V_{ij}
.

Notation 4.10
.

Let
f
be a potential on
Q_{n,I}
.

(1)

Write
\mathrm{deg}(f)=i
if
f\in D_{i}
. Similarly write
\mathrm{deg}(f)\geq i
if
f\in\oplus_{j\geq i}D_{j}
, with natural self-documenting variations such as
\mathrm{deg}(f)\leq i
.

(2)

Write
\mathrm{len}(f)=i
if
f\in L_{i}
. Similarly write
\mathrm{len}(f)\geq i
if
f\in\oplus_{j\geq i}L_{j}
, with natural self-documenting variations such as
\mathrm{len}(f)\leq i
.

The above degree and length notations will be important, and they will replace the common notations such as path length.

Notation 4.11
.

Let
f
and
g
be potentials on
Q_{n,I}
. With the notation in
4.9
, since
f,g\in F
,
F=\bigoplus_{i}D_{i}
and
F=\bigoplus_{i\leq j}V_{ij}
, we will adopt the following notation.

(1)

Define
f_{d}
by decomposing
f=\sum_{d}f_{d}
where each
f_{d}\in D_{d}
.

(2)

Define
f_{<d}=\sum_{i<d}f_{i}
and
f_{>d}=\sum_{i>d}f_{i}
, with natural self-documenting variations such as
f_{\leq d}
and
f_{\geq d}
. Thus, if
\mathrm{deg}(f)\geq 2
then
f=f_{2}+f_{3}+f_{>3}
.

(3)

Define
f_{ij}
by decomposing

f=\sum_{i,j:1\leq i\leq j\leq m}f_{ij},

where each
f_{ij}\in V_{ij}
. Variations such as
f_{ij,d}
,
f_{ij,<d}
,
f_{ij,\leq d}
,
f_{ij,>d}
and
f_{ij,\geq d}
are obtained by applying (1) and (2) to
f_{ij}
.

(4)

Given
s
such that
1\leq s\leq m
, set

f_{[s]}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i,j:1\leq i\leq s\leq j\leq m}f_{ij}.

Variations such as
f_{[s],d}
,
f_{[s],<d}
,
f_{[s],\leq d}
,
f_{[s],>d}
and
f_{[s],\geq d}
are obtained by applying (1) and (2) to
f_{[s]}
.

(5)

Write
f=g+\mathcal{O}_{d}
if
f-g\in\bigoplus_{k\geq d}D_{k}
, and
f=g+\mathcal{O}_{ij,d}
if
f-g\in V_{ij}\bigcap\bigoplus_{k\geq d}D_{k}
.

Remark 4.12
.

We will frequently work with sequences of potentials
(\mathsf{f}_{d})_{d\geq 1}
on
Q_{n,I}
, and write
f_{d}
for the degree
d
pieces of
f
(see
4.11
). To avoid confusion, we will systematically use Greek font
\mathsf{f}_{d}
to denote the
d
-th elements in a sequence, and not the
d
-th degree piece.

4.1.
Monomialization

This subsection will prove that any reduced Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
is right-equivalent to some reduced monomialized Type
A
potential (see
4.20
).

Notation 4.13
.

To ease notation, in this subsection
f
will always refer to a reduced Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g
(see
4.6
).
In the statements below, to ease notation, the
c
and
c_{k}
will refer to a cycle on
Q_{n,I}
, possibly with a coefficient.

The following lemma allows us to monomialize the degree
2
terms in
f
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose that
g=h+c
where
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 3
and
\mathrm{deg}(c)=2
. Then there exists a path degree
1
right-equivalence (in the sense of
2.8
),

\rho_{c}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+c_{1})+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(c_{1})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(c_{1})=2
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{deg}(c)=2
and
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 3
,
c
must have the form of
c\sim\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
for some
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, where
s
is such that
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop.
Since
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop and
f
is reduced,
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{s}^{2}
, and so
f_{[s],2}=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s}+\mathsf{x}_{s}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
.

Rewrite
f=f_{[s],2}\oplus f_{[s],\geq 3}\oplus r
. Being a loop,
\mathsf{x}_{s}=a_{s}
, so applying the depth one unitriangular automorphism
\rho_{c}\colon a_{s}\mapsto a_{s}-\uplambda b_{s-1}a_{s-1}
(in other words,
\mathsf{x}_{s}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}
) gives

\displaystyle\rho_{c}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}(\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime})+(\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime})\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r+\mathcal{O}_{3}

\displaystyle=f-\uplambda(\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime})^{2}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+\mathcal{O}_{3}

(
f=f_{[s],2}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h+c-\uplambda(\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime})^{2}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+\mathcal{O}_{3}

(
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h+c
)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{3}

(
c\sim\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1},\ (\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime})^{2}\sim\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{2}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{3}.

(
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+c),\ \mathrm{len}(c)\geq 3
)

Set
c_{1}=-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{2}
, which satisfies
\mathrm{len}(c_{1})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(c_{1})=2
, and we are done.
∎

The following lemmas allow us to monomialise the terms of degree at least three in
f
.
More precisely, given a cycle
c
with
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 2
occurring in
f
, we repeatedly apply
right-equivalences that decrease
\textnormal{right}(c)
(see
4.16
) until all resulting terms have length one
(see
4.17
).

Lemma 4.15
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{len}(f_{2})\leq 2
and
g=h+c
where
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 2
,
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 3
. Then there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\upvartheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+c_{1}+c_{2})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that each
c_{k}
is either zero or satisfies
\textnormal{right}(c_{k})\leq\textnormal{right}(c)
,
\mathrm{deg}(c_{k})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
and
\mathbf{T}(c_{k})_{\textnormal{right}(c)}=\mathbf{T}(c)_{\textnormal{right}(c)}-1
.

Proof.

Set
s=\textnormal{right}(c)-1
. The assumption
\mathrm{len}(f_{2})\leq 2
says that the degree two part of
f
(wrt.
\ \mathsf{x}_{i}
, as in
4.10
) must be spread over at most two variables.
Hence the only degree two cycles in
f
involving
\mathsf{x}_{s}
are
\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s}
,
\mathsf{x}_{s}^{2}
and
\mathsf{x}_{s}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
. In the notation of
4.11
, this gives
f_{[s],2}=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s}+\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}^{2}+\mathsf{x}_{s}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
for some
\upkappa\in\mathbb{C}
.
Decomposing
f
into terms that do and do not involve
\mathsf{x}_{s}
, we may write

f=f_{[s],2}\oplus f_{[s],\geq 3}\oplus r
. We treat two cases.

(1)
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is not a loop.

The assumptions that
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 2
and
\textnormal{right}(c)=s+1
imply that
a_{s}
,
b_{s}
,
a_{s+1}
and
b_{s+1}
all appear in
c
. Note that
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is not a loop, thus
\mathsf{x}_{s}=a_{s}b_{s}
. Locally
Q_{n,I}
looks like the following.

\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
is not a loop
\scriptstyle a_{s}
\scriptstyle b_{s}
\scriptstyle a_{s+1}
\scriptstyle b_{s+1}
\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
is a loop
\scriptstyle a_{s}
\scriptstyle b_{s}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{s+1}

Since
\textnormal{right}(c)=s+1
, we may assume that the cycle
c
starts with
\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
, up to cyclic equivalence. Then
c
begins with some power of
\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
, and the next arrow must be
b_{s}
.
Thus we may write

c\sim\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s+1}^{N}b_{s}pa_{s}r\sim\uplambda b_{s}pa_{s}r\mathsf{x}_{s+1}^{N}

for some
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, an integer
N\geq 1
, and paths
p
,
r
. Consider the path
q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}r\mathsf{x}_{s+1}^{N-1}
, and rewrite
c\sim\uplambda b_{s}pa_{s}q\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
.
Since
\mathrm{deg}(c)=d\geq 3
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{x}_{s})=\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{x}_{s+1})=1
, we have
\mathrm{deg}(p)+\mathrm{deg}(q)=d-2\geq 1
.

Then applying the depth
d-1
unitriangular automorphism
\upvartheta\colon a_{s}\mapsto a_{s}-\uplambda pa_{s}q
gives

\displaystyle\upvartheta(f)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}(a_{s}-\uplambda pa_{s}q)b_{s}+\upkappa[(a_{s}-\uplambda pa_{s}q)b_{s}]^{2}+b_{s}(a_{s}-\uplambda pa_{s}q)\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}pa_{s}qb_{s}-2\uplambda\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}pa_{s}qb_{s}-\uplambda b_{s}pa_{s}q\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f=f_{[s],2}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}pa_{s}qb_{s}-2\uplambda\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}pa_{s}qb_{s}-\uplambda b_{s}pa_{s}q\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+c+h+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h+c
)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}pa_{s}qb_{s}-2\uplambda\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}pa_{s}qb_{s}+h+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
c\sim\uplambda b_{s}pa_{s}q\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}pa_{s}qb_{s}-2\uplambda\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}pa_{s}qb_{s}+h)+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Since
\mathrm{deg}(p)+\mathrm{deg}(q)=d-2
, all terms not displayed explicitly lie in
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
.

Set
c_{1}=-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}pa_{s}qb_{s}
and
c_{2}=-2\uplambda\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}pa_{s}qb_{s}
. Since
\mathrm{deg}(p)+\mathrm{deg}(q)=d-2
, it follows that
\mathrm{deg}(c_{1})=d=\mathrm{deg}(c_{2})
.
The conclusions for
c_{1}
are clear. Either
c_{2}
is zero or
\upkappa\neq 0
. In that case, the conclusions for
c_{2}
are also clear.

(2)
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop.

Since
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop, from the shape of the quiver
Q_{n,I}
,
\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
is not a loop. Since
\textnormal{right}(c)=s+1
, we can assume that the cycle
c
ends with
\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
, up to cyclic equivalence. Thus
c\sim\uplambda p\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
for some path
p
and
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Since
\mathrm{deg}(c)=d\geq 3
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{x}_{s+1})=1
,
\mathrm{deg}(p)=d-1\geq 2
.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{x}_{s}
is a loop and
f
is reduced, the coefficient
\upkappa
in
f_{[s],2}=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s}+\upkappa\mathsf{x}_{s}^{2}+\mathsf{x}_{s}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
is zero. Locally
Q_{n,I}
looks like the following.

\scriptstyle a_{s+1}
\scriptstyle b_{s+1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{s}

Being a loop,
\mathsf{x}_{s}=a_{s}
, so applying the depth
d-1
unitriangular automorphism
\upvartheta\colon a_{s}\mapsto a_{s}-\uplambda p
(in other words,
\mathsf{x}_{s}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda p
) gives

\displaystyle\upvartheta(f)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}(\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda p)+(\mathsf{x}_{s}-\uplambda p)\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}p-\uplambda p\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f=f_{[s],2}+f_{[s],\geq 3}+r
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}p-\uplambda p\mathsf{x}_{s+1}+c+h+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+h+c
)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}p+h+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
c\sim\uplambda p\mathsf{x}_{s+1}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}p+h)+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Note that since
\mathrm{deg}(p)=d-1
, all terms other than those explicitly displayed above contribute only to the higher degree part
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
.

Set
c_{1}=-\uplambda\mathsf{x}_{s-1}^{\prime}p
and
c_{2}=0
. Since
\mathrm{deg}(p)=d-1
, it follows that
\mathrm{deg}(c_{1})=d
.
The conclusions for
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are clear.
∎

We next apply the previous lemma multiple times to decrease
\textnormal{right}(c)
.

Corollary 4.16
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{len}(f_{2})\leq 2
and
g=h+c
where
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 2
,
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 3
. Then there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence

\upvartheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{k}c_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\textnormal{right}(c_{k})\leq\textnormal{right}(c)-1
and
\mathrm{deg}(c_{k})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
for each
k
.

Proof.

Set
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{T}(c)
and
j=\textnormal{right}(c)
. By
4.15
, there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\upvartheta_{1}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{s=1}^{2}w_{s})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
w_{s}
is either zero, or satisfies
\textnormal{right}(w_{s})\leq\textnormal{right}(c)
,
\mathbf{T}(w_{s})_{j}=q_{j}-1
and
\mathrm{deg}(w_{s})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
for each
s
.

If all
w_{s}
equal zero, or all satisfy
\mathbf{T}(w_{s})_{j}=0
, we are done. Otherwise, we continue to apply
4.15
to decrease
\mathbf{T}(w_{s})_{j}
, as follows.

\upvartheta_{2}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{s=1}^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{2}w_{st})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that each
w_{st}
is either zero, or
\textnormal{right}(w_{st})\leq\textnormal{right}(w_{s})\leq\textnormal{right}(c)
,
\mathbf{T}(w_{st})_{j}=q_{j}-2
and
\mathrm{deg}(w_{st})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
.
The proof follows by induction.
∎

We iteratively apply
4.16
to reach the case where all resulting terms have length one, i.e. a monomial-type potential.

Corollary 4.17
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{len}(f_{2})\leq 2
and
g=h+c
where
\mathrm{len}(c)\geq 2
,
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 3
. Then there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence

\rho_{c}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{k}c_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(c_{k})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(c_{k})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
for each
k
.

Proof.

Set
j\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{right}(c)
.
By
4.16
, there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence

\upvartheta_{1}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f_{1}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{s}w_{s})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{deg}(w_{s})=d
and
\textnormal{right}(w_{s})\leq j-1
for each
s
.

If all
\mathrm{len}(w_{s})=1
, we are done. Otherwise, we continue to apply
4.16
to those
\mathrm{len}(w_{s})>1
to decrease
\textnormal{right}(w_{s})
, as follows.

\upvartheta_{2}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{s,t}w_{st})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{deg}(w_{st})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
and the
w_{st}
satisfies
\textnormal{right}(w_{st})\leq j-2
.

If all
\mathrm{len}(w_{st})=1
, we are done.
Otherwise, we can repeat this process at most
j-1
times, as follows.

\rho_{c}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{j-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(h+\sum_{k}c_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{deg}(c_{k})=\mathrm{deg}(c)
, and either each
\mathrm{len}(c_{k})=1
or
\textnormal{right}(c_{k})=1
. However if
\textnormal{right}(c_{k})=1
, then
\mathrm{len}(c_{k})=1
, we are done.
∎

We now monomialise
f
degree by degree, using the previous lemmas.
We begin with the degree two part.

Proposition 4.18
.

There exists a path degree
1
right-equivalence,

\rho_{2}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus h+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(h)=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(h)=2
.

Proof.

Decompose
g
(from
4.13
) by degree (wrt.
\ \mathsf{x}_{i}
, as in
4.10
) as
g=g_{2}\oplus g_{\geq 3}
, and write
g_{2}=\oplus_{k=1}^{s}c_{k}

as a
\mathbb{C}
-linear combination of degree-two cycles.
Since there are only a finite number of cycles with degree two on
Q_{n,I}
, necessarily
s
is finite.
Then we write

f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g_{2}\oplus g_{\geq 3}.

Since
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus g
and
g_{2}
is orthogonal to
\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
,
the degree two part
g_{2}
contains no length two terms. Hence
\mathrm{len}(c_{k})=1
or
\mathrm{len}(c_{k})\geq 3
for each
k
.

If
\mathrm{len}(c_{1})=1
, set
\rho_{c_{1}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Otherwise
\mathrm{len}(c_{1})\geq 3
, so by
4.14
there exists

\rho_{c_{1}}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(\sum_{k=2}^{s}c_{k}+\mathrm{h}_{1})+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{1})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{1})=2
.

If
\mathrm{len}(c_{2})=1
, set
\rho_{c_{2}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Otherwise
\mathrm{len}(c_{2})\geq 3
, so again by
4.14
there exists

\rho_{c_{2}}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(\sum_{k=3}^{s}c_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{2}\mathrm{h}_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{2})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{2})=2
.

We repeat this process
s
times and set
\rho_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rho_{c_{s}}\circ\dots\circ\rho_{c_{2}}\circ\rho_{c_{1}}
. It follows that,

\rho_{2}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\sum_{k=1}^{s}\mathrm{h}_{k}+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=1
,
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=2
for each
k
. Set
h=\sum_{k=1}^{s}\mathrm{h}_{k}
, we are done.
∎

The following will allow us to monomialize the higher degree terms.

Proposition 4.19
.

Suppose that
\mathrm{len}(f_{2})\leq 2
. For any
d\geq 3
, there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\rho_{d}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(g_{<d}+h)+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(h)=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(h)=d
.

Proof.

Decompose
g
(from
4.13
) by degree (wrt.
\mathsf{x}_{i}
, as in
4.10
) as

g=g_{<d}\oplus g_{d}\oplus g_{>d}
, and write

g_{d}=\oplus_{k=1}^{s}c_{k}

as a
\mathbb{C}
-linear combination of degree
d
cycles. Since there are only a finite number of cycles with degree
d
on
Q_{n,I}
,
s
is finite.

If
\mathrm{len}(c_{1})=1
, set
\rho_{c_{1}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Otherwise, by
4.17
there exists

\rho_{c_{1}}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(g_{<d}+\sum_{k=2}^{s}c_{k}+\mathrm{h}_{1})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{1})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{1})=d
.

If
\mathrm{len}(c_{2})=1
, set
\rho_{c_{2}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Otherwise, again by
4.17
there exists

\rho_{c_{2}}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(g_{<d}+\sum_{k=3}^{s}c_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{2}\mathrm{h}_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=d
.

We repeat this process
s
times and set
\rho_{d}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rho_{c_{s}}\circ\dots\circ\rho_{c_{2}}\circ\rho_{c_{1}}
. It follows that,

\rho_{d}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus(g_{<d}+\sum_{k=1}^{s}\mathrm{h}_{k})+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=1
,
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{k})=d
for each
k
. Set
h=\sum_{k=1}^{s}\mathrm{h}_{k}
, we are done.
∎

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 4.20
.

For any reduced Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, there exists a right-equivalence
\rho\colon f\rightsquigarrow f^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}
is a reduced monomialized Type A potential.

Proof.

We first apply the
\rho_{2}
in
4.18
,

\rho_{2}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\mathrm{h}_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{3},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{2})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{2})=2
.

Since
(\mathsf{f}_{1})_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\mathrm{h}_{2}
, it is clear that
\mathrm{len}((\mathsf{f}_{1})_{2})\leq 2
.
Thus by
4.19
applied to
\mathsf{f}_{1}
, there exists

\rho_{3}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\sum_{j=2}^{3}\mathrm{h}_{j}+\mathcal{O}_{4},

such that
\mathrm{len}(h_{3})=1
,
\mathrm{deg}(h_{3})=3
.
Iterating this procedure, for each
s\geq 3
we obtain

\rho_{s}\circ\dots\circ\rho_{3}\circ\rho_{2}\colon f\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{f}_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\sum_{j=2}^{s}\mathrm{h}_{j}+\mathcal{O}_{s+1},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{j})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{j})=j
for all
2\leq j\leq s
.

Since
\rho_{d}
is a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence for each
d\geq 2
by
4.18
and
4.19
, by
2.9

\rho\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\rho_{s}\circ\dots\circ\rho_{3}\circ\rho_{2}
exists, and further

\rho\colon f\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}\oplus\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathrm{h}_{j},

such that
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{j})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(\mathrm{h}_{j})=j
for each
j
.

Set
f^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\mathrm{h}_{j}
. Since
\mathrm{len}(\mathrm{h}_{j})=1
for each
j
,
f^{\prime}
is a monomialized Type A potential.
Moreover, since
f
is reduced,
f^{\prime}
is also reduced.
∎

4.2.
Transform monomialized Type A potentials on
Q_{n,I}
to
Q_{n}

To state unified results later, it will be convenient to show that any monomialized Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
is isomorphic to a (possibly non-reduced) monomialized Type
A
potential on
Q_{n}
.
This required the following results, which provide a precise construction for adding a loop to
Q_{n,I}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Given any
I\neq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and
i\in I^{c}
, let
\mathsf{x}_{t}
be the loop at vertex
i
of
Q_{n,I}
. Suppose that
h=\sum_{i=1}^{t-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}+\sum_{i=t+1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq t}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}
where all
\upkappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
.
There exists a right-equivalence

h\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j},

where
\upkappa_{ij}^{\prime}
are some scalars, and further
\upkappa^{\prime}_{t2}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{x}_{t}
is the loop at vertex
i
of
Q_{n,I}
, the quiver looks like the following locally,

\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle a_{t}
\scriptstyle b_{t}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{t}
\scriptstyle a_{t+1}
\scriptstyle b_{t+1}

Being a loop,
\mathsf{x}_{t}=a_{t}
, so applying the automorphism
a_{t}\mapsto a_{t}-b_{t-1}a_{t-1}-a_{t+1}b_{t+1}
(in other words,
\mathsf{x}_{t}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{t}-\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}-\mathsf{x}_{t+1}
) gives,

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{t-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}+\sum_{i=t+1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{x}_{t}-\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}-\mathsf{x}_{t+1})^{2}+\sum_{i\neq t}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq t}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}.

(4.A)

The last step uses the cyclic equivalences

\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{t}\sim\mathsf{x}_{t}\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{x}_{t}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}\sim\mathsf{x}_{t+1}\mathsf{x}_{t}
, and

\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}\sim\mathsf{x}_{t+1}\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}
.
Then define the scalars
\upkappa^{\prime}_{ij}
by the identity

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t-1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t+1}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq t}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}.

Since
\upkappa_{t2}^{\prime}
is the coefficient of
\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}
in (
4.A
),
\upkappa_{t2}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\neq 0
.
∎

Corollary 4.22
.

Given any
I\neq\emptyset
,
i\in I
and a monomialized Type A potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, then there exists a monomialized Type A potential
g
on
Q_{n,I/i}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I/i},g)
and
g
contains the square of the loop at vertex
i
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{x}_{t}
be the loop at vertex
i
of
Q_{n,I/i}
. Locally,
Q_{n,I}
and
Q_{n,I/i}
look like the following, respectively.

Q_{n,I}
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle a_{t-1}
\scriptstyle b_{t-1}
\scriptstyle a_{t}
\scriptstyle b_{t}
Q_{n,I/i}
\scriptstyle i
\scriptstyle a_{t}
\scriptstyle b_{t}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{t}
\scriptstyle a_{t+1}
\scriptstyle b_{t+1}

Relabeling the paths allows us to consider
f
as a potential on
Q_{n,I/i}
.
More precisely, we replace the
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
in
f
by
a_{k+1}
and
b_{k+1}
respectively for any
k\geq t
.
Then set
h\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}
. It is clear that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I/i},h)
.

By
4.21
, there exists a right-equivalence
h\rightsquigarrow g
such that
g
is a monomialized Type A potential on
Q_{n,I/i}
and
g
contains
\mathsf{x}_{t}^{2}
. Thus
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I/i},h)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I/i},g)
, and so
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I/i},g)
.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

Given any
I
and a monomialized Type
A
potential
f=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa^{\prime}_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}
on
Q_{n,I}
where all
\upkappa^{\prime}_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
, then there exists a monomialized Type
A
potential
g
on
Q_{n}
, namely

g=\sum_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}

for some
\upkappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
, such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},g)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
and
\upkappa_{2i-1,2}\neq 0
for each
i\in I
.

Proof.

If
I=\emptyset
, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, given any
i\in I
, by
4.22
there exist a monomialized Type
A
potential
\mathsf{g}_{1}
on
Q_{n,I\backslash i}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I\backslash i},\mathsf{g}_{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
, where
\mathsf{g}_{1}
contains the square of the loop at vertex
i
.

Similarly, by
4.22
we can repeat the same argument to
\mathsf{g}_{1}
on
Q_{n,I\backslash i}
and any
j\in I\backslash\{i\}
to construct a monomialized Type
A
potential
\mathsf{g}_{2}
on
Q_{n,I\backslash\{i,j\}}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I\backslash\{i,j\}},\mathsf{g}_{2})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I\backslash i},\mathsf{g}_{1})
, where
\mathsf{g}_{2}
contains the square of the loop at vertex
i
and vertex
j
.

Set
s=|I|
. Thus we can repeat this process
s
times to construct a monomialized Type
A
potential
\mathsf{g}_{s}
on
Q_{n,\emptyset}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,\emptyset},\mathsf{g}_{s})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
, and
\mathsf{g}_{s}
contains the square of all the loops at all vertices
i\in I
.

Set
g\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{g}_{s}
. Since
\upkappa_{2i-1,2}
are the coefficients of the square of the loops at the vertices
i\in I
, the statement follows.
∎

5.
Geometric Realisation

In §
5.1
we show that every Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
is realised by a crepant resolution of a
cA_{n}
singularity (see
5.12
), thereby proving the Realisation Conjecture of Brown–Wemyss
[
BW
]
for Type
A

potentials. Conversely, §
5.2
establishes the reverse direction (see
5.15
) and then proves a correspondence between crepant resolutions of
cA_{n}
singularities and our intrinsic Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}

(see
5.18
and
5.19
).

5.1.
Geometric Realisation

In this subsection we prove that, given any Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, there exists a crepant resolution

\uppi
of a
cA_{n}
singularity such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
(see
5.12
). This verifies
the Realisation Conjecture of Brown–Wemyss
[
BW
]
in the setting of Type
A
potentials.

Notation 5.1
.

Fix a monomialised Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
of the form

f=\sum_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j}.

(5.A)

Consider the system of equations (
5.B
) in unknowns
g_{0},\dots,g_{2n}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
:

\displaystyle g_{0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}g_{1}^{j-1}+g_{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle g_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2j}g_{2}^{j-1}+g_{3}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{{}={}}][c]{\vdots}

(5.B)

\displaystyle g_{2n-2}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2n-1,j}g_{2n-1}^{j-1}+g_{2n}

\displaystyle=0.

The following lemma allows us to construct the geometric realisation of
f
(
5.A
) in
5.3
(
1
) by the system of equations (
5.B
).

Lemma 5.2
.

With notation in
5.1
, fix some integer
t
satisfying
0\leq t\leq 2n-1
, and set
g_{t}=y
,
g_{t+1}=x
.
Then there exists a sequence
(g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{2n})
satisfying (
5.B
) such that each

g_{s}\in(x,y)\subseteq\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
is a prime element with a linear term.
Moreover,

(1)

For any
0\leq s\leq 2n-1
,
(g_{s},g_{s+1})=(x,y)
.

(2)

For any
1\leq s\leq 2n-1
,
(g_{s-1},g_{s+1})\subsetneq(x,y)
when
\upkappa_{s2}=0
, and
(g_{s-1},g_{s+1})=(x,y)
when
\upkappa_{s2}\neq 0
.

Proof.

We start with the equation
g_{t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{t+1,j}g_{t+1}^{j-1}+g_{t+2}=0
in (
5.B
) which defines
g_{t+2}=-y-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{t+1,j}x^{j-1}\in(x,y)
.
Then we consider
g_{t+1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{t+2,j}g_{t+2}^{j-1}+g_{t+3}=0
which also defines
g_{t+3}\in(x,y)
. Thus we can repeat this process to construct
g_{s}\in(x,y)
for
t+2\leq s\leq 2n
.
Similarly, the equation
g_{t-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{t,j}g_{t}^{j-1}+g_{t+1}=0
defines
g_{t-1}\in(x,y)
. We can repeat this process to construct
g_{s}\in(x,y)
for
0\leq s\leq t-1
.

(1) For any
0\leq s\leq 2n-2
, using
g_{s}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{s+1,j}g_{s+1}^{j-1}+g_{s+2}=0
in (
5.B
), we have
(g_{s},g_{s+1})=(g_{s+1},g_{s+2})
. Moving either to the left or right until we hit
t
, it follows that
(g_{s},g_{s+1})=(g_{t},g_{t+1})=(y,x)
.
Hence
(g_{s},g_{s+1})=(x,y)
for all
0\leq s\leq 2n-1
.

In particular, no
g_{s}
can lie in
(x,y)^{2}
,
so each
g_{s}
has a nonzero linear term. Moreover, since
(g_{s},g_{s+1})
is the maximal ideal of
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
,
each
g_{s}
is irreducible, hence prime.

(2) For any
1\leq s\leq 2n-1
, using
g_{s-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{sj}g_{s}^{j-1}+g_{s+1}=0
in (
5.B
), we have
(g_{s-1},g_{s+1})=(g_{s-1},\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{sj}g_{s}^{j-1})
. Thus, if
\upkappa_{s2}=0
then
(g_{s-1},g_{s+1})\subsetneq(x,y)
, and if
\upkappa_{s2}\neq 0
then
(g_{s-1},g_{s+1})=(g_{s-1},g_{s})
which equals
(x,y)
by (1).
∎

Notation 5.3
.

For any
t
with
0\leq t\leq 2n-1
,
5.2
calculates a solution of (
5.B
).
Fix any such solution, say
(g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{2n})
. From this, we adopt the following notation.

(1)

We first define the
cA_{n}
singularity

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-g_{0}g_{2}\dots g_{2n}}.

Note that each
g_{i}
is a prime element of
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
with a linear term by
5.2
, so
\mathcal{R}
is a
cA_{n}
singularity.
Then consider the
\mathrm{CM}

\mathcal{R}
-module

M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,g_{0})\oplus(u,g_{0}g_{2})\oplus\ldots\oplus(u,\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}g_{2j}).

(2)

We next define

\mathcal{S}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\dots,x_{2n-1},x_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-x_{0}x_{2}\dots x_{2n}}.

(3)

Define a sequence
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{2n-1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
to be

\displaystyle h_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x_{i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}x_{i}^{j-1}+x_{i+1},

and set
\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{1}/(h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{i-1})
for
2\leq i\leq 2n
.

(4)

For
1\leq i\leq 2n
, by abuse of notation we regard

(u,x_{0}),\ (u,x_{0}x_{2}),\ \ldots,\ \bigl(u,\prod_{j=0}^{\,n-1}x_{2j}\bigr)
as
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-modules.
Then we define the
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-module

N_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{i}\oplus(u,x_{0})\oplus(u,x_{0}x_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus\bigl(u,\prod_{j=0}^{\,n-1}x_{2j}\bigr).

(5)

Write
\uppi_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{1}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
for the universal flop of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
corresponding to
N_{1}
, as constructed in the complete local setting in
[
IW1
, §5]
.
For
2\leq i\leq 2n
, consider the morphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
, and the fiber product
\mathcal{X}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{X}_{1}\times_{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
. These morphisms fit into the following commutative diagram.

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{2}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{2n}
\mathcal{X}_{1}
\mathcal{X}_{2}
\mathcal{X}_{2n}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{1}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{2}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{2n}
\dots
\dots

(6)

Consider the following quiver
Q
.

1
2
3
\ldots\ldots
n-1
n
0
\scriptstyle b_{0}
\scriptstyle a_{0}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle a_{2}
\scriptstyle b_{4}
\scriptstyle a_{4}
\scriptstyle b_{2n-2}
\scriptstyle a_{2n-2}
\scriptstyle a_{2n}
\scriptstyle b_{2n}
\scriptstyle l_{0,0},l_{0,1},\dots,l_{0,2n}
\scriptstyle l_{1,0},l_{1,1},\dots,l_{1,2n}
\scriptstyle l_{2,0},l_{2,1},\dots,l_{2,2n}
\scriptstyle l_{3,0},l_{3,1},\dots,l_{3,2n}
\scriptstyle l_{n-1,0},l_{n-1,1},\dots,l_{n-1,2n}
\scriptstyle l_{n,0},l_{n,1},\dots,l_{n,2n}

Then define the relations
R_{1}
of
Q
as follows.

R_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}l_{t,i}a_{2t}=a_{2t}l_{t+1,i},\ l_{t+1,i}b_{2t}=b_{2t}l_{t,i},\ l_{t,i}l_{t,j}=l_{t,j}l_{t,i},\\
l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t},\ l_{t+1,2t}=b_{2t}a_{2t}\text{ for any }t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)\text{ and }0\leq i,j\leq 2n.\end{cases}

(5.C)

For
2\leq s\leq 2n
, define
R_{s}
to be
R_{1}
with the additional relations

l_{t,i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}l_{t,i}^{j-1}+l_{t,i+1}=0\text{ for any }0\leq t\leq n\text{ and }1\leq i\leq s-1.

(5.D)

To prepare for the main construction
5.9
, we now establish in
5.4
–
5.8
a quiver presentation of the NCCR
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
, where
\mathcal{R}
and
M
are as in
5.3
(
1
).

Lemma 5.4
.

With notation in
5.3
, for
2\leq k\leq 2n
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is an integral domain and normal. Furthermore, there exists a ring isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{S}_{2n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{R}
such that
\varphi(N_{2n})=M
.

Proof.

Fix some
k
with
2\leq k\leq 2n
.
By the definition in
5.3
(
2
) and
5.3
(
3
),

\mathcal{S}_{k}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt]}{(uv-x_{0}x_{2}\dots x_{2n},h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{k-1})},

where each
h_{i}=x_{i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}x_{i}^{j-1}+x_{i+1}
.

As in the proof of
5.2
, the relations
h_{1},\dots,h_{k-1}
allow us to eliminate
x_{2},\dots,x_{k}
recursively, expressing each
x_{i}
(
2\leq i\leq k
) as a formal power series in
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
.
Write this as
x_{i}=H_{i}(x_{0},x_{1})
.

Thus, when
k
is even,

\mathcal{S}_{k}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},x_{k+1},x_{k+2},\dots,x_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-x_{0}H_{2}H_{4}\dots H_{k}x_{k+2}\dots x_{2n}}.

When
k
is odd,

\mathcal{S}_{k}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},x_{k+1},x_{k+2},\dots,x_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-x_{0}H_{2}H_{4}\dots H_{k-1}x_{k+1}\dots x_{2n}}.

In both cases,
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is an integral domain and normal by e.g.
[
S
, 4.1.1]
.

Then we prove that
\mathcal{S}_{2n}\cong\mathcal{R}
. Recall from
5.2
that we start with
g_{t}=y
and
g_{t+1}=x
and then construct
(g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{2n})
where each
g_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]
using the equation system (
5.B
). Then, in
5.3
(
1
), these
g_{i}
were used to define
\mathcal{R}
.

On the other hand,

\mathcal{S}_{2n}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt]}{(uv-x_{0}x_{2}\dots x_{2n},h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{2n-1})}.

Similar to
5.2
, we can express each
x_{s}
as a formal power series of
x_{t}
and
x_{t+1}
using
h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{2n-1}
.
More precisely, in
\mathcal{S}_{2n}
we have
x_{s}=g_{s}(x_{t+1},x_{t})
for all
s
, where
g_{s}
is the power series in
variables
(x,y)
obtained from (
5.B
) with
g_{t}=y
and
g_{t+1}=x
. Hence

\mathcal{S}_{2n}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x_{t},x_{t+1}]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-g_{0}(x_{t+1},x_{t})g_{2}(x_{t+1},x_{t})\dots g_{2n}(x_{t+1},x_{t})}.

Define a ring homomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{S}_{2n}\to\mathcal{R}
by

u\mapsto u
,
v\mapsto v
,
x_{t+1}\mapsto x
, and
x_{t}\mapsto y
.
It is immediate that
\varphi
is an isomorphism, and moreover
\varphi(N_{2n})=M
.
∎

With notation as in
5.3
, let
\uppi_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{1}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
be the universal resolution with
\Lambda(\uppi_{1})\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(N_{1})

[
IW1
, §5]
. As shown in
Appendix
7.17
, there is an isomorphism

\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(N_{1})\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{1},

where
Q
and
R_{1}
are as in (
5.C
).

For notational convenience, set
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{1}
.
By
[
W2
, 6.2]
,
\mathcal{X}_{1}
is isomorphic to a moduli scheme of stable representations of
\Lambda
, of dimension vector
\updelta=(1,1,\dots,1)
and stability
\upvartheta=(-n,1,1,\dots,1)
where the
-n
sits at vertex
0
of
Q
.
In notation,
\mathcal{X}_{1}\cong\mathcal{M}_{\updelta}^{\upvartheta}(\Lambda)
, which is the moduli space of
\upvartheta
-stable representations of dimension vector
\updelta
.

Moreover, as in
[
B2
, §3]
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\updelta}^{\upvartheta}(\Lambda)
is covered by

n+1
affine charts
\mathcal{U}_{10},\dots,\mathcal{U}_{1n}
.
Accounting for the relations
R_{1}
(
5.C
), the first affine chart
\mathcal{U}_{10}
is parameterised by

\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\ldots\ldots
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{2}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{4}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{a}_{2n}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{2n}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{x}_{2n},

where
\mathsf{x}_{2n}=\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n}
and (since we work on the completed path algebra) all cycles are nilpotent. We claim that
\mathcal{U}_{10}\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{A}_{10}
where

\mathcal{A}_{10}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n})}.

(5.E)

Indeed, on
\mathcal{U}_{10}
we fix the clockwise arrows marked
1
(except
\mathsf{a}_{2n}
), and then use all the relations in

R_{1}
to express all remaining arrow-parameters in terms of the displayed generators.
So the question boils down to understanding nilpotent cycles. It is clear that
\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-3},\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}
are cycles, as is
\mathsf{a}_{2n}
(once composed with all clockwise arrows marked
1
), thus they are nilpotent. As is
\mathsf{b}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}\ldots\mathsf{b}_{0}
.

There is no condition on
\mathsf{b}_{2n}
, so it is a polynomial variable. Introducing a new completion variable
\mathsf{v}
to capture the nilpotency of
\mathsf{b}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}\ldots\mathsf{b}_{0}
, which has a mix of both polynomial and completion variables, the claim (
5.E
) follows.

Moreover,
\uppi_{1}|_{\mathcal{U}_{10}}\colon\mathcal{U}_{10}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
is induced by the ring homomorphism
\varphi_{10}\colon\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{10}

\displaystyle x_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{0},\quad x_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-2}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\quad x_{2n}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n},

\displaystyle x_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{1},\quad x_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{3},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\quad u\mapsto\mathsf{a}_{2n},\quad v\mapsto\mathsf{v}.

(5.F)

Similarly, the second affine chart
\mathcal{U}_{11}
is parameterised by

\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\ldots\ldots
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\mathbb{C}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{2}
\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle 1

\scriptstyle\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{a}_{2n}
\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},

\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},
\scriptstyle\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},

where
\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}=\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}
and (since we work on the completed path algebra) all cycles are nilpotent. We claim that
\mathcal{U}_{11}\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{A}_{11}
where

\mathcal{A}_{11}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{a}_{2n-2},\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-2})}.

(5.G)

Similarly, on
\mathcal{U}_{11}
we fix the clockwise arrows marked
1
(except
\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}
), and then use all the relations in

R_{1}
to express all remaining arrow-parameters in terms of the displayed generators. Clearly
\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-3},\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\mathsf{a}_{2n}
are cycles, as is
\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n}
(once composed with all clockwise arrows marked
1
), thus they are nilpotent. As is
\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-4}\ldots\mathsf{b}_{0}
.

There is no condition on
\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}
and
\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}
, so they are polynomial variables. Introducing new completion variables
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{v}
to capture the nilpotency of
\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n}
and
\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-4}\ldots\mathsf{b}_{0}
respectively, which have a mix of both polynomial and completion variables, the claim (
5.G
) follows.

Moreover,
\uppi_{1}|_{\mathcal{U}_{11}}\colon\mathcal{U}_{11}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
is induced by the ring homomorphism
\varphi_{11}\colon\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{11}

\displaystyle x_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{0},\quad x_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-4}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\quad x_{2n-2}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\quad x_{2n}\mapsto\mathsf{a}_{2n},

\displaystyle x_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{1},\quad x_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{3},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\quad u\mapsto\mathsf{u},\quad v\mapsto\mathsf{v}.

(5.H)

Each of the remaining affine charts
\mathcal{U}_{1j}
of
\mathcal{X}_{1}
admits a similar parametrisation, and the corresponding morphism
\uppi_{1}|_{\mathcal{U}_{1j}}\colon\mathcal{U}_{1j}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
is defined in the same way as above.

Notation 5.5
.

With the notation
\mathcal{U}_{1j}
above and in
5.3
, for each
2\leq i\leq 2n
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we set

(1)

\mathcal{U}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{U}_{1j}\times_{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
, the base change of
\mathcal{U}_{1j}
along
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
;

(2)

\mathcal{A}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Gamma(\mathcal{U}_{ij},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{U}_{ij}})
, the coordinate ring of
\mathcal{U}_{ij}
;

(3)

\varphi_{ij}\colon\mathcal{S}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{ij}
, the ring homomorphism associated to
\uppi_{i}|_{\mathcal{U}_{ij}}\colon\mathcal{U}_{ij}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

By definition
5.3
(
5
)
\mathcal{X}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{X}_{1}\times_{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
, hence
\mathcal{X}_{i}\cong\bigcup_{j=0}^{n}\mathcal{U}_{ij}
.

Since
\mathcal{X}_{1}
is the universal resolution of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
, it is connected and smooth. We now show that, for
2\leq i\leq 2n
, the base change
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is likewise connected and smooth.

The next result shows that the connectivity of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
comes from the overlap of adjacent affine charts along the exceptional curves.

Proposition 5.6
.

With notation in
5.3
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is connected for all
2\leq i\leq 2n
.

Proof.

For
1\leq j\leq n
, write
\mathrm{C}_{j}
for the
j
-th exceptional curve of the universal resolution
\uppi_{1}\colon\mathcal{X}_{1}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
over the origin.
By definition
5.3
(
3
), for
2\leq i\leq 2n

\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{1}/(h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{i-1}),

where
h_{k}
is a power series without a constant term for
1\leq k\leq i-1
.

Since each
h_{k}
has no constant term, the closed immersion
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
meets the origin of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Hence the base change
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\times_{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
contains the exceptional fibre

\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{C}_{j}
over the origin.
Moreover, for each
1\leq j\leq n
the affine charts of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
satisfy

\mathrm{C}_{j}\subset\mathcal{U}_{i,j-1}\cup\mathcal{U}_{ij}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{U}_{i,j-1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{ij}\neq\emptyset,

and so the affine charts of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
pairwise overlap along the exceptional curves. Hence
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is connected.
∎

We now prove that
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is smooth for
2\leq i\leq 2n
by analysing each affine chart
\mathcal{U}_{ij}
of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

With notation in
5.3
, for
2\leq i\leq 2n
,
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is smooth.

Proof.

Since by definition
5.3
(
5
)
\mathcal{X}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{X}_{1}\times_{\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq 2n
, we have the following pullback squares for the
j
-th affine chart
\mathcal{U}_{ij}
of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
:

\mathcal{U}_{ij}
\mathcal{U}_{1j}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
\uppi_{i}|_{\mathcal{U}_{ij}}
\uppi_{1}|_{\mathcal{U}_{1j}}

\mathcal{A}_{ij}
\mathcal{A}_{1j}
\mathcal{S}_{i}
\mathcal{S}_{1}
\varphi_{ij}
\varphi_{1j}

Recall from
5.3
(
3
) that for
2\leq i\leq 2n

\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{1}/(h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{i-1})\quad\text{with}\quad h_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}x_{i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}x_{i}^{\,j-1}+x_{i+1}.

Therefore, for
2\leq i\leq 2n
and
0\leq j\leq n
,

\mathcal{A}_{ij}\cong\mathcal{A}_{1j}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{i}\cong\mathcal{A}_{1j}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{1}/(h_{1},h_{2},\dots,h_{i-1})\cong\mathcal{A}_{1j}/(\mathcal{A}_{1j}h_{1},\mathcal{A}_{1j}h_{2},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{1j}h_{i-1}).

(5.I)

First chart
(j=0)
.

From (
5.E
) we have

\mathcal{A}_{10}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{0},\mathsf{b}_{2},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n})}.

Moreover, by (
5.F
)
\varphi_{10}\colon\mathcal{S}_{1}\to\mathcal{A}_{10}
is given by

\displaystyle x_{0}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{0},\quad x_{2}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-2}\mapsto\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\quad x_{2n}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n},

\displaystyle x_{1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{1},\quad x_{3}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{3},\quad\dots,\quad x_{2n-1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\quad u\mapsto\mathsf{a}_{2n},\quad v\mapsto\mathsf{v}.

Thus, for

, the images

are

\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{b}_{i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{b}_{i+1},\text{ for }i=1,3,\dots,2n-3\\
\mathsf{x}_{i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{b}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{i+1},\text{ for }i=2,4,\dots,2n-2\\
\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2n-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2n},\text{ for }i=2n-1\end{cases}

Introduce the notation obtained by successive elimination:

\mathsf{b}_{01}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\,\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\,j-1}-\mathsf{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{b}_{2}]\kern-1.0pt],

and, using

,

\mathsf{b}_{02}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\!\left(-\sum_{r=2}^{\infty}r\upkappa_{2r}\,\mathsf{b}_{2}^{\,r-1}-\mathsf{x}_{3}\right)^{\!j-1}-\mathsf{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{3}]\kern-1.0pt].

Continuing inductively, for
0\leq t\leq n-1
and

, define
\mathsf{b}_{2t,k}
with

\mathsf{b}_{2t,k}\in\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{k},\mathsf{b}_{k+1}]\kern-1.0pt],&k\text{ odd},\ k\neq 2n-1,\\
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{k},\mathsf{x}_{k+1}]\kern-1.0pt],&k\text{ even},\\
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt],&k=2n-1.\end{cases}

Each
\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{i}
has a linear term, hence eliminates one variable in (
5.I
). Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{20}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{1})\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathsf{b}_{0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\mathsf{x}_{1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{b}_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{4},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{01}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{30}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{1},\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2})\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathsf{b}_{0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\mathsf{x}_{1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2j}\mathsf{b}_{2}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{3})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{4},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{3},\mathsf{x}_{5},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{02}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n})},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2n-1,0}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{1},\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2n-2})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0,2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2,2n-2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-4,2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2n,0}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{10}/(\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{1},\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2n-2},\mathcal{A}_{10}h_{2n-1})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{b}_{2n}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\mathsf{b}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0,2n-1}\mathsf{b}_{2,2n-1}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-4,2n-1}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2,2n-1}\mathsf{b}_{2n})}.

Hence, for

, the first affine chart

is analogous to

\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2n-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{a}_{2n},\text{ for }i=2n-1\end{cases}

\mathsf{b}_{2t,k}

From the last equation, set

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{2n-2,2n-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2n-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}^{j-1}-\mathsf{a}_{2n}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{a}_{2n}]\kern-1.0pt].

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{4},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{1},\mathsf{x}_{3},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{a}_{2n-2},\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{01}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-2})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{31}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{11}/(\mathcal{A}_{11}h_{1},\mathcal{A}_{11}h_{2})\cong\mathcal{A}_{11}/(\mathsf{b}_{0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\mathsf{x}_{1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{x}_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2j}\mathsf{b}_{2}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{3})

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{b}_{2},\mathsf{b}_{4},\dots,\mathsf{b}_{2n-4},\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{3},\mathsf{x}_{5},\dots,\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{a}_{2n-2},\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{02}\mathsf{b}_{2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-2})},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{x}_{2n-1},\mathsf{x}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{v}]\kern-1.0pt][\mathsf{a}_{2n-2},\mathsf{b}_{2n-2}]}{(\mathsf{x}_{2n-2}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2},\mathsf{u}-\mathsf{a}_{2n-2}\mathsf{a}_{2n},\mathsf{v}-\mathsf{b}_{0,2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2,2n-2}\dots\mathsf{b}_{2n-4,2n-2}\mathsf{b}_{2n-2})},

Since

, the affine charts
