are analogous to
\mathcal{U}_{i1}
(for each fixed
i
), hence smooth as well.
Therefore
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is smooth for all
2\leq i\leq 2n
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

With notation in
5.3
,
\End_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(N_{i})\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 2n
.

Proof.

Recall from
5.3
the commutative diagram

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{2}
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{2n}
\mathcal{X}_{1}
\mathcal{X}_{2}
\mathcal{X}_{2n}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{1}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{2}
\scriptstyle\uppi_{2n}
\dots
\dots

together with the
\mathcal{S}_{i}
-module
N_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 2n
.

By
[
IW1
, §5]
,
N_{1}
is the tilting bundle for
\uppi_{1}
, and by (
5.J
) we have

\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(N_{1})\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{1},

(5.J)

where
Q
and
R_{1}
are given in (
5.C
).

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is an integral domain and normal, and
\mathcal{X}_{1}
is connected and smooth.
By
5.4
,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is also an integral domain and normal.
By
5.6
and
5.7
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}
is connected and smooth.
Since
N_{1}
is the tilting bundle for
\uppi_{1}
, we can apply
[
V3
, 2.11]
to deduce that
N_{2}\cong N_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{2}
is the tilting bundle for
\uppi_{2}
and

\displaystyle\End_{\mathcal{S}_{2}}(N_{2})

\displaystyle\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}/h_{1}}(N_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{1}/h_{1})

(since
\mathcal{S}_{2}\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}/h_{1},\ N_{2}\cong N_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{2}
)

\displaystyle\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(N_{1})/(h_{1})

(by
[
V3
, 2.11]
)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{2}.

(by (
5.J
))

Here
R_{2}
is obtained from
R_{1}
by adding the relation (
5.D
) with
i=1
, namely

l_{t,0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}l_{t,1}^{j-1}+l_{t,2}=0,\quad\text{ for }t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1),

which corresponds to

h_{1}=x_{0}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\,x_{1}^{\,j-1}+x_{2}.

Iterating this argument, for any
2\leq i\leq 2n
, we have
N_{i}\cong N_{i-1}\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{i-1}}\mathcal{S}_{i}
is the tilting bundle for
\uppi_{i}
, and

\displaystyle\End_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(N_{i})

\displaystyle\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{i-1}}(N_{i-1})/(h_{i-1})

\displaystyle\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{i-2}}(N_{i-2})/(h_{i-1},h_{i-2})

\displaystyle\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(N_{1})/(h_{i-1},h_{i-2},\dots,h_{1})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{i}.\qed

The following theorem shows that any monomialized Type
A
potential on
Q_{n}
, not necessarily reduced, can be realised by a crepant resolution of a
cA_{n}
singularity.

Theorem 5.9
.

With the monomialized Type
A
potential
f
in (
5.A
) on
Q_{n}
, the
cA_{n}
singularity
\mathcal{R}
, and the

\mathrm{CM}

\mathcal{R}
-module
M
as in
5.3
, one has

\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},f)
.

Proof.

By
5.4
,
\mathcal{R}\cong\mathcal{S}_{2n}
and
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\End_{\mathcal{S}_{2n}}(N_{2n})
. By
5.8
,
\End_{\mathcal{S}_{2n}}(N_{2n})\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{2n}
.
Thus
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{2n}
, where
Q
and
R_{2n}
are as in
5.3
(
5.D
).

Similar to
Q_{n}
, we also define
\mathsf{x}_{i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}
on
Q
as follows:
for any
0\leq i\leq n
, set
\mathsf{x}_{2i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a_{2i}b_{2i}
and
\mathsf{x}_{2i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b_{2i}a_{2i}
, and for any
1\leq i\leq n
, set
\mathsf{x}_{2i-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}l_{i,2i-1}=:\mathsf{x}_{2i-1}^{\prime}
.

Next, we consider the following relations induced by
R_{2n}
.
For any
1\leq t\leq n-1
, left multiplying the
i=2t
case of
(
5.D
)
by
b_{2t}
gives

\displaystyle\quad b_{2t}l_{t,2t-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}b_{2t}l_{t,2t}^{j-1}+b_{2t}l_{t,2t+1}

\displaystyle=b_{2t}l_{t,2t-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}b_{2t}l_{t,2t}^{j-1}+l_{t+1,2t+1}b_{2t}

(since
b_{2t}l_{t,2t+1}=l_{t+1,2t+1}b_{2t}
by (
5.C
))

\displaystyle=b_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}b_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}b_{2t}.

(since
l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}
,
l_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}
and
l_{t+1,2t+1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}
by (
5.C
))

Similarly, for any
1\leq t\leq n-1
, right multiplying the
i=2t
case of
(
5.D
)
by
a_{2t}
gives

\displaystyle\quad l_{t,2t-1}a_{2t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}l_{t,2t}^{j-1}a_{2t}+l_{t,2t+1}a_{2t}

\displaystyle=l_{t,2t-1}a_{2t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}l_{t,2t}^{j-1}a_{2t}+a_{2t}l_{t+1,2t+1}

(since
l_{t,2t+1}a_{2t}=a_{2t}l_{t+1,2t+1}
by (
5.C
))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}a_{2t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j-1}a_{2t}+a_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}.

(since
l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}
,
l_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}
and
l_{t+1,2t+1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}
by (
5.C
))

For any
1\leq t\leq n
, the
i=2t-1
case of (
5.D
) is

\displaystyle l_{t,2t-2}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}l_{t,2t-1}^{j-1}+l_{t,2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-2}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2t}.

(since
l_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
,
l_{t,2t-2}=b_{2t-2}a_{2t-2}=\mathsf{x}^{\prime}_{2t-2}
and
l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}
by notation and (
5.C
))

Combining the above three types of relations gives the following,

T\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}b_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}b_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{i+1}b_{i}=0\text{, for }i=2,4,\dots,2n-2.\\
\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}a_{i}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}a_{i}+a_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}=0\text{, for }i=2,4,\dots,2n-2.\\
\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{i+1}=0\text{, for }i=1,3,\dots,2n-1.\end{cases}

(5.K)

Then we define the quiver
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
by deleting loops on
Q
as follows.
For each vertex
t
on
Q
with
1\leq t\leq n
, we delete all loops
l_{tj}
except
l_{t,2t-1}
(namely
\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
). Note that
Q_{n}
is
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
by removing the vertex
0
and loops on it.

In
5.10
below we will show that
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n},e_{0}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T,e_{0}\rangle
.
Together with the isomorphism
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/R_{2n}
at the start of the proof, this gives

\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n},e_{0}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T,e_{0}\rangle.

Thus
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
factored by the relations
T
, which after deleting paths that factor through vertex
0
, become

\displaystyle b_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}b_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{i+1}b_{i}=0\text{, for }i=2,4,\dots,2n-2.

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}a_{i}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}a_{i}+a_{i}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}=0\text{, for }i=2,4,\dots,2n-2.

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{i-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{i+1}=0\text{, for }i=3,\dots,2n-2.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{1j}\mathsf{x}_{1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2}=0,\ \mathsf{x}_{2n-2}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2n-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2n-1}^{j-1}=0.

These are exactly the relations generated by the derivatives of
f
.
Thus
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},f)
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

With notation in
5.3
and
5.9
,
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n},e_{0}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T,e_{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

We first divide the relations
R_{2n}
in
5.3
(
5.D
) into three parts. The following are the relations in
R_{2n}
that factor through the vertex
0
.

T_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}l_{00}=a_{0}b_{0},\text{ }l_{0,2n}=b_{2n}a_{2n}.\\
l_{0i}a_{0}=a_{0}l_{1i},\ l_{ni}a_{2n}=a_{2n}l_{0i},\ l_{0i}b_{2n}=b_{2n}l_{ni},\ l_{1i}b_{0}=b_{0}l_{0i},\\
l_{0i}l_{0j}=l_{0j}l_{0i}\text{, for }0\leq i,j\leq 2n.\\
l_{0,i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}l_{0,i}^{j-1}+l_{0,i+1}=0\text{, for }1\leq i\leq 2n-1.\end{cases}

Then we divide the remaining relations of
R_{2n}
into the following two parts.

T_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}l_{t,2t-2}=b_{2t-2}a_{2t-2},\text{ for }1\leq t\leq n.\\
l_{ti}l_{tj}=l_{tj}l_{ti},\text{ for }1\leq t\leq n\text{ and }0\leq i,j\leq 2n.\\
l_{ti}a_{2t}=a_{2t}l_{t+1,i},\ l_{t+1,i}b_{2t}=b_{2t}l_{ti},\text{ for }1\leq t\leq n-1\text{ and }0\leq i\leq 2n.\end{cases}

T_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t},\text{ for any }1\leq t\leq n.\\
l_{t,i-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}l_{t,i}^{j-1}+l_{t,i+1}=0\text{ for any }1\leq t\leq n\text{ and }1\leq i\leq 2n-1.\end{cases}

Since
T
in (
5.K
) is induced by
R_{2n}
, necessarily

\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n},T\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T_{0},T_{1},T_{2},T\rangle.

(5.L)

We next use
T_{2}
to eliminate some loops at vertex
1,2,\dots,n
of
Q
, as follows.

Fix some vertex
t
with
1\leq t\leq n
and consider the loops
l_{ti}
on it.
Since
l_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
in
T_{2}
, we can eliminate
l_{t,2t}
.
From our notation,
l_{t,2t-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
and
\mathsf{x}_{2t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a_{2t}b_{2t}
. Thus we can write
l_{t,2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}
.
Since
l_{ti}l_{tj}=l_{tj}l_{ti}
in
T_{1}
for
0\leq i,j\leq 2n
, we can consider
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle l_{t,2t-1},l_{t,2t}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
as the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[l_{t,2t-1},l_{t,2t}]\kern-1.0pt]
. By the relation

l_{t,2t-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}l_{t,2t}^{j-1}+l_{t,2t+1}=0,

(5.M)

in
T_{2}
, we can express
l_{t,2t+1}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[l_{t,2t-1},l_{t,2t}]\kern-1.0pt]=\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{2t-1},\mathsf{x}_{2t}]\kern-1.0pt]
.
Thus we can eliminate
l_{t,2t+1}
. Similar to the argument in
5.2
, for each
i\neq 2t-1
we can express
l_{ti}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bar{l}_{ti}(x_{2t-1},\mathsf{x}_{2t})\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{2t-1},\mathsf{x}_{2t}]\kern-1.0pt]
and eliminate it.
So we only leave one loop
l_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
on vertex
t
.

Thus we can use all the relations in
T_{2}
to eliminate all such loops at vertices
1,2,\dots,n
.
For
0\leq k\leq 2
, write
\overline{T}_{k}
for the the relations where we have substituted
l_{ti}
in
T_{k}
by the polynomial
\bar{l}_{ti}
for
1\leq t\leq n
and
0\leq i\leq 2n
. So we have

\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T_{0},T_{1},T_{2},T\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},\overline{T}_{1},T\rangle.

(5.N)

Now during the above substitution process, the following expressions in
\overline{T}_{2}

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}

(since
\mathsf{x}_{2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
)

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{ij}\bar{l}_{t,2t}^{j-1}+\bar{l}_{t,2t+1}=0

(by (
5.M
))

hold in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
tautologically. Similarly, tautologically, all the other expressions in
\overline{T}_{2}
also hold in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
.

We next prove that
T
in (
5.K
) induces
\overline{T}_{1}
.

(1) Firstly, we prove that
T
induces
\bar{l}_{t,2t-2}=b_{2t-2}a_{2t-2}
for
1\leq t\leq n
. Since

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{2t-2}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t-1,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2t}=0,

(by the
i=2t-1
case of the third line in (
5.K
))

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t-2}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t-1,j}\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}^{j-1}+\bar{l}_{t,2t}=0.

(since
\overline{T}_{2}
holds in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
)

and by notation
\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
and
\bar{l}_{t,2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}
, then
\bar{l}_{t,2t-2}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-2}^{\prime}=b_{2t-2}a_{2t-2}
.

(2) Secondly, we prove that
T
induces
\bar{l}_{ti}\bar{l}_{tj}=\bar{l}_{tj}\bar{l}_{ti}
for
1\leq t\leq n
and
0\leq i,j\leq 2n
.

Left multiplying the
i=2t
case of the first line in (
5.K
) by
a_{2t}
gives

\displaystyle 0=a_{2t}(b_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}b_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j-1}+\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}b_{2t})=\mathsf{x}_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j}+a_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}b_{2t}.

(since
\mathsf{x}_{2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
)

Right multiplying the
i=2t
case of the second line in (
5.K
) by
b_{2t}
gives

\displaystyle 0=(\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}a_{2t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j-1}a_{2t}+a_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t+1})b_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{2t}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j}+a_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}b_{2t}.

(since
\mathsf{x}_{2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
)

Thus
\mathsf{x}_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{2t}
. Since
\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
is the loop at vertex
t
, then by definition
\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
, and so
\mathsf{x}_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}\mathsf{x}_{2t}
.
Together with the fact that each
\bar{l}_{ti}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[\mathsf{x}_{2t-1},\mathsf{x}_{2t}]\kern-1.0pt]
gives
\bar{l}_{ti}\bar{l}_{tj}=\bar{l}_{tj}\bar{l}_{ti}
for
0\leq i,j\leq 2n
.

(3) Finally, we prove that
T
induces
\bar{l}_{ti}a_{2t}=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,i},\ \bar{l}_{t+1,i}b_{2t}=b_{2t}\bar{l}_{ti}
for
1\leq t\leq n-1
and
0\leq i\leq 2n
.
For each vertex
t
with
1\leq t\leq n-1
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t},

(since
\overline{T}_{2}
holds in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
)

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}=b_{2t}a_{2t}=\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{\prime},

(by (1))

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime},\ \bar{l}_{t+1,2t+1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}^{\prime}.

(by the definition of
\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}
and
\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}
)

Thus

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t}a_{2t}

\displaystyle=a_{2t}b_{2t}a_{2t}

(since
\bar{l}_{t,2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
)

\displaystyle=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t},

(since
\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}=b_{2t}a_{2t}
)

and

\displaystyle\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}a_{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}a_{2t}

(since
\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}=\mathsf{x}_{2t-1}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\mathsf{x}_{2t}^{j-1}a_{2t}-a_{2t}\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}

(by the
i=2t
case of the second line in (
5.K
))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}^{j-1}-a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t+1}

(since
\mathsf{x}_{2t}=a_{2t}b_{2t}
,
\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}=b_{2t}a_{2t}
and
\mathsf{x}_{2t+1}=\bar{l}_{t+1,2t+1}
)

\displaystyle=-a_{2t}(\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}j\upkappa_{2t,j}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}^{j-1}+\bar{l}_{t+1,2t+1})

\displaystyle=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t-1}.

(since
\overline{T}_{2}
holds in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
)

Since
\overline{T}_{2}
holds in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
, then similar to the argument in
5.2
, each
\bar{l}_{ti}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\bar{l}_{t,2t-1},\bar{l}_{t,2t}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
and
\bar{l}_{t+1,i}\in\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\bar{l}_{t+1,2t-1},\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
. Furthermore,

\bar{l}_{ti}=H_{i}(\bar{l}_{t,2t-1},\bar{l}_{t,2t}),\ \bar{l}_{t+1,i}=H_{i}(\bar{l}_{t+1,2t-1},\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}).

for the same
H_{i}
.
Together with the above
\bar{l}_{t,2t}a_{2t}=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t}
and
\bar{l}_{t,2t-1}a_{2t}=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,2t-1}
, this gives
\bar{l}_{ti}a_{2t}=a_{2t}\bar{l}_{t+1,i}
for each
i
.

Similarly,
T
(
5.K
) also induces
\bar{l}_{t+1,i}b_{2t}=b_{2t}\bar{l}_{ti}
for each
i
.

Combining (1), (2) and (3), it follows that
T
induces
\overline{T}_{1}
, and so
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},\overline{T}_{1},T\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},T\rangle
.
Together with (
5.L
), this gives

\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T_{0},T_{1},T_{2},T\rangle\mathrel{{\mathop{\cong}\limits}}\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},\overline{T}_{1},T\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},T\rangle,

and so
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle R_{2n},e_{0}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle\overline{T}_{0},T,e_{0}\rangle\cong\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle\mathcal{Q}_{n}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\langle T,e_{0}\rangle
.
∎

We now consider the quiver
Q_{n,I}
for some
I\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and prove that any Type
A
potential on it can be realized by a crepant resolution of a
cA_{n}
singularity as follows.

Definition 5.11
.

We say that
\uppi
is
Type
A_{n}
if
\uppi
is a crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
where
\mathcal{R}
is
cA_{n}
. Moreover, we say that
\uppi
is
Type
A_{n,I}
if the normal bundle of the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
is
\mathcal{O}(-1)\bigoplus\mathcal{O}(-1)
if and only if
i\in I
, else the normal bundle is
\mathcal{O}(-2)\bigoplus\mathcal{O}
.

Theorem 5.12
.

For any Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, there exists a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
. If furthermore
f
is reduced, then
\uppi
is Type
A_{n,I}
.

Proof.

By the Splitting Theorem (
[
DWZ
, 4.6]
) and
4.4
, there is a reduced Type A potential
f_{\textnormal{red}}
on
Q_{n,I^{\prime}}
for some
I\subseteq I^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I^{\prime}},f_{\textnormal{red}})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
. Then, by
4.20
, there exists a reduced monomialized Type
A
potential
g
on
Q_{n,I^{\prime}}
such that
f_{\textnormal{red}}\cong g
. By
4.23
, there exists a monomialized Type
A
potential
h
on
Q_{n}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I^{\prime}},g)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},h)
. Thus we have

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I^{\prime}},f_{\textnormal{red}})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I^{\prime}},g)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},h).

By
5.9
, there exists a
cA_{n}
singularity
\mathcal{R}
and a maximal
\mathrm{CM}

\mathcal{R}
-module
M
such that
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},h)
. Denote
\uppi
to be the crepant resolution of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
, which corresponds to
M
in
3.9
. Thus
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n},h)
, and so
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
.

If furthermore
f
is reduced, then
I^{\prime}=I
,
f_{\textnormal{red}}=f
and
g
is a reduced monomialized Type
A
potential on
Q_{n,I}
. Then write

h=\sum_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n-1}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\upkappa_{ij}\mathsf{x}_{i}^{j},

for some
\upkappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}
. Since
g
is reduced on
Q_{n,I}
, then by
4.23

\upkappa_{2i-1,2}\neq 0
when
i\in I
, and
\upkappa_{2i-1,2}=0
when
i\notin I
. Write
\mathcal{R}
and
M
as follows,

\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-g_{0}g_{2}\dots g_{{2n}}}

and
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,g_{0})\oplus(u,g_{0}g_{2})\oplus\dots\oplus(u,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}g_{2i})
. We next prove that
\uppi
is Type
A_{n,I}
.

(1)

For any vertex
i\in I
, since
\upkappa_{2i-1,2}\neq 0
, then
(g_{2i-2},g_{2i})=(x,y)
by
5.2
, and so the normal bundle of the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
of
\uppi
is
\mathcal{O}(-1)\bigoplus\mathcal{O}(-1)
by
3.10
.

(2)

For any vertex
i\notin I
, since
\upkappa_{2i-1,2}=0
, then
(g_{2i-2},g_{2i})\subsetneq(x,y)
by
5.2
, and so the normal bundle of the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
of
\uppi
is
\mathcal{O}(-2)\bigoplus\mathcal{O}
by
3.10
. ∎

The Brown–Wemyss Realisation Conjecture
[
BW
]
states that if
f
is any potential which satisfies
\textnormal{Jdim}(f)\leq 1
(see
[
BW
, 3.4]
for the definition), then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is isomorphic to the contraction algebra of some crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
with
\mathcal{R}

cDV
.
Therefore,
5.12
verifies the Brown–Wemyss Realisation Conjecture for all Type
A
potentials
on
Q_{n,I}
, for arbitrary
n\geq 1
and
I\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

5.2.
Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolutions and potentials

In this subsection we prove the converse to
5.12
; namely, given any Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi
, there exists a reduced Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
(see
5.15
).
Together with
5.12
, this yields a correspondence between Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions and monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
,
made precise in
5.18
and
5.19
.

The following
5.13
and
5.14
show that commutativity of the algebras
e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}
and
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
provides the key link between Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions and Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
; this will be crucial in the proof of
5.15
.

Lemma 5.13
.

If
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
is a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution, then
e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}
is commutative for any
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

Since
\uppi
is a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution, we have
\Lambda(\uppi)\cong\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
for some maximal Cohen–Macaulay (
\mathrm{CM}
)
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
where
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus M_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus M_{n}
and each
M_{i}
is an indecomposable rank one CM
\mathcal{R}
-module. Thus
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})\cong\mathcal{R}
for
1\leq i\leq n
from e.g.
[
IW2
, 5.4]
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
denote the stable category
\underline{\mathrm{CM}}\,\mathcal{R}
of
\mathrm{CM}\,\mathcal{R}
-modules.
Then
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\End_{\mathscr{C}}(M)
and
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})\cong\End_{\mathscr{C}}(M_{i})
.
Thus for any
1\leq i\leq n
,

e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}\cong e_{i}\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)e_{i}\cong e_{i}\End_{\mathscr{C}}(M)e_{i}\cong\End_{\mathscr{C}}(M_{i})\cong\underline{\operatorname{End}}_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i}).

Since
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})\cong\mathcal{R}
is commutative and
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})
is a quotient of
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})
, then
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M_{i})
is also commutative, and so
e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}
is commutative.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Suppose that
f
is a reduced potential on
Q_{n,I}
. If there is some integer
j
where
1\leq j\leq m-1
such that
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
, then there exists some integer
i
(depending on
j
) where
1\leq i\leq n
such that
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is not commutative.

Proof.

(1) When
\mathsf{x}_{j}
and
\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
are not loops, then there exists a vertex
i\in I
such that
Q_{n,I}
at vertex
i
locally looks like the following.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}

We denote the above quiver with only the three vertices shown, as
Q^{\prime}
. Then consider the noncommutative algebra
J
, defined as
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
quotiented by the ideal generated by the following paths:

•

\mathfrak{m}^{5}_{Q_{n,I}}
where
\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{n,I}}
is the ideal generated by all the arrows of
Q_{n,I}
(see
2.1
).

•

e_{k}
for all
1\leq k<i-1
and
i+1<k\leq n
.

•

possible loops
\mathsf{x}_{j-1}
on vertex
i-1
and
\mathsf{x}_{j+2}
on vertex
i+1
.

It is clear that
J\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q^{\prime},g)
where
g\sim\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{2}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}+\uplambda_{3}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}
for some
\uplambda_{1},\ \uplambda_{2},\ \uplambda_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then we suppose that
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative and
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
, and aim for a contradiction.

Since
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative and
e_{i}Je_{i}
is a factor of
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
, then
e_{i}Je_{i}
is also commutative, and furthermore
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}=\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}
in
e_{i}Je_{i}
. Since
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
,
g\sim\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{2}+\uplambda_{3}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}
. It is clear that the four relations induced by differentiating
\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{2}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}
can not induce the relation
(b_{j}a_{j})(a_{j+1}b_{j+1})=(a_{j+1}b_{j+1})(b_{j}a_{j})
.
Thus
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\neq\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}
in
e_{i}Je_{i}
, a contradiction.

(2) When
\mathsf{x}_{j}
is not a loop and
\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
is a loop, then there exists a vertex
i\notin I
such that
Q_{n,I}
at vertex
i
locally looks like the following.

We again denote the above quiver with the only two vertices shown, as
Q^{\prime}
. Then consider the noncommutative algebra
J
, defined as
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
quotiented by the ideal generated by the following paths:

•

\mathfrak{m}^{4}_{Q_{n,I}}
where
\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{n,I}}
is the ideal generated by all the arrows of
Q_{n,I}
(see
2.1
).

•

e_{k}
for all
1\leq k<i-1
and
i<k\leq n
.

•

the possible loop
\mathsf{x}_{j-1}
on vertex
i-1
.

•

\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}
.

It is clear that
J\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q^{\prime},g)/(\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2})
where
g\sim\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{2}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}+\uplambda_{3}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}+\uplambda_{4}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2}+\uplambda_{5}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{3}+\uplambda_{6}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{4}
for some
\uplambda_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
.
We suppose that
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative and
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
, and aim for a contradiction.

Since
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative and
e_{i}Je_{i}
is a factor of
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
, then
e_{i}Je_{i}
is also commutative, and furthermore
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}=\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}
in
e_{i}Je_{i}
. Since
f
does not contain
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
,
\uplambda_{2}=0
. Since
f
is reduced,
\uplambda_{4}=0
. Thus

J\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q^{\prime}\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle}{(\uplambda_{3}(b_{j}a_{j})\mathsf{x}_{j+1}+\uplambda_{3}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}(b_{j}a_{j}),\uplambda_{1}b_{j}a_{j}b_{j},\uplambda_{1}a_{j}b_{j}a_{j},\mathsf{x}_{j+1}^{2})}.

Again, it is clear that the above relations can not induce
(b_{j}a_{j})\mathsf{x}_{j+1}=\mathsf{x}_{j+1}(b_{j}a_{j})
. Thus
\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\neq\mathsf{x}_{j+1}\mathsf{x}_{j}^{\prime}
in
e_{i}Je_{i}
, a contradiction.

(3) When
\mathsf{x}_{j}
is a loop and
\mathsf{x}_{j+1}
is not a loop, the proof is analogous to that of (2).
∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Given any Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
, there exists a reduced Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

Proof.

By
3.10
, the NCCR
\Lambda(\uppi)
can be presented as the quiver in
3.10
with some relations. Since
\mathcal{R}
is complete local,
\Lambda(\uppi)
is also complete local by e.g.
[
BW
, 8.4]
. Moreover,
\Lambda(\uppi)
is
3
-CY
[
IW1
, 2.8]
.
Since any complete local
3
–CY algebra is a Jacobi algebra, as shown in
[
V2
, §10]
, the relations of
\Lambda(\uppi)
are generated by some reduced potential
g
.
Since
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\Lambda(\uppi)/\langle e_{0}\rangle
,
it follows that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f)
for some reduced potential
f
, obtained from
g
by deleting all terms involving arrows with source or target at the vertex
0
.

It remains to show that
f
is of Type
A
, that is, that
f
contains
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
for every
1\leq i\leq m-1
.
Since
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
and
e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}
is commutative for each
1\leq i\leq n
by
5.13
,
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is also commutative for each
i
.
Thus
f
contains
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
for each
i
, by
5.14
.
∎

We are now in a position to show that our definition of Type A potential
4.4
is intrinsic.

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
f
be a reduced potential on
Q_{n,I}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is Type A.

(2)

There exists a Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
.

(3)

e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Since
f
is a reduced Type A potential on
Q_{n,I}
, it is immediate by
5.12
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3): Since
\uppi
is a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution, then
e_{i}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)e_{i}
is commutative by
5.13
, and so
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative for any
1\leq i\leq n
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1): Since
f
is a reduced potential on
Q_{n,I}
and
e_{i}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)e_{i}
is commutative for any
1\leq i\leq n
, then
f
contains
\mathsf{x}_{i}^{\prime}\mathsf{x}_{i+1}
for any
1\leq i\leq m-1
by
5.14
, and so
f
is Type A.
∎

Definition 5.17
.

We say two crepant resolutions
\uppi_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
for
i=1,2
have the same
noncommutative deformation type (NC deformation type)
if
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
.

The name NC deformation type comes from the fact that the contraction algebra represents the noncommutative deformation functor of the exceptional curves
[
DW1
]
.

Together with
4.20
, the above
5.15
induces a map
\varphi
from Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolutions to the isomorphism classes of reduced monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n,I}
. More precisely, for any Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi
, we define
\varphi(\uppi)
to be the reduced monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
that satisfies
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
by
5.15
and
4.20
. Moreover,
\varphi
is well-defined since if there are two such
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, then
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})
.

Theorem 5.18
.

The above
\varphi
induces a one-to-one correspondence as follows.

isomorphism classes of reduced monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n,I}
Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolutions up to NC deformation type

Proof.

Firstly, we prove the map from top to bottom is surjective, namely that for any reduced monomialized Type
A_{n,I}
potential
f
, there is a Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
. This is immediate from
5.12
.

Then we prove that the map from top to bottom is injective. Let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}_{k}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{k}
be two Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolutions for
k=1,2
. If
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
for some reduced monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, then
\uppi_{1}
and
\uppi_{2}
have the same NC deformation type.
∎

The following asserts that Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
describe the contraction algebra of all Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions.

Corollary 5.19
.

The set of isomorphism classes of contraction algebras associated to Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions is equal to the set of isomorphism classes of Jacobi algebras of monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
.

Proof.

We first define a map
\phi
from the isomorphism classes of contraction algebra associated with Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions to the isomorphism classes of Jacobi algebra of monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
.

Given any contraction algebra
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
where
\uppi
is a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution, then
\uppi
belongs to Type
A_{n,I}
crepant resolution for some
I
. Thus
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{n,I},f^{\prime})
for some reduced monomialized Type
A
potential
f^{\prime}
on
Q_{n,I}
by
5.18
. Moreover,
f^{\prime}
is isormphic to some monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
by
4.23
. We define
\phi(\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

Secondly, we prove that
\phi
is well-defined. If there are two Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions
\uppi_{1}
and
\uppi_{2}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, then
\phi(\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1}))=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})
and
\phi(\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2}))=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})
, so
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})
from the above definition of
\phi
.

Thirdly, we prove that
\phi
is injective. If there are two Type
A_{n}
crepant resolutions
\uppi_{1}
and
\uppi_{2}
such that
\phi(\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1}))\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\phi(\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2}))
for some monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
, then
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
from the above definition of
\phi
.

Finally, by
5.12

\phi
is surjective.
∎

Notation 5.20
.

Let
f
and
g
be potentials on a quiver
Q
.
We say that
f
is
derived equivalent to

g
(written
f\simeq g
) if the derived categories
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f))
and
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g))
are triangle equivalent.

Given any isolated
cA_{n}
singularity
\mathcal{R}
which admits a crepant resolution, let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be one of the crepant resolutions. Then, by
5.19
, there exists some monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, so it induces a map
\Phi
from isolated
cA_{n}
singularities, which admit a crepant resolution to monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
.

Theorem 5.21
.

The above
\Phi
induces a one-to-one correspondence as follows.

derived equivalence classes of monomialized Type
A
potentials on
Q_{n}
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra
isomorphism classes of isolated
cA_{n}
singularities
which admit a crepant resolution

Proof.

Firstly, we prove that the map from top to bottom is well-defined. Given any isolated
cA_{n}

\mathcal{R}
which admits a crepant resolution, let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be one of the crepant resolutions. Then there exists some monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
by
5.19
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{R}
is isolated,
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite-dimensional by
3.5
. Let
\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be another crepant resolution such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{\prime})
for some monomialized Type
A
potential
f^{\prime}
on
Q_{n}
. Since
\uppi^{\prime}
is a flop of
\uppi
and
\mathcal{R}
is isolated,
f
is derived equivalent to
f^{\prime}
by
3.7
.

Secondly, we prove that the map from top to bottom is surjective. Given any monomialized Type
A
potential
f
on
Q_{n}
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra, there exists a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
such that
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
by
5.9
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite-dimensional,
\mathcal{R}
is isolated by
3.5
.

Finally, we prove the map from top to bottom is injective.
This uses the proof of the Donovan-Wemyss conjecture in
3.6
.
Let
\uppi_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
be two crepant resolutions of isolated
cA_{n}

\mathcal{R}_{i}
with
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{i})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{i})
for
i=1,2
. If
f_{1}
is derived equivalent to
f_{2}
, together with
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
isolated, then
R_{1}\cong R_{2}
by
3.6
.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

Since both
3.6
and
3.7
need the assumption of isolated cDVs, we only prove the correspondence in
5.21
for isolated
cA_{n}
singularities.
Moreover, by testing contraction algebras of crepant resolutions of the non-isolated
cA_{2}
singularity
\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]/(uv-x^{2}y)
, it seems that
5.21
can not be generalised directly to the non-isolated cases.

6.
Special cases:
A_{3}

Q_{3,\{1,2,3\}}
, namely

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{x}_{1}=a_{1}b_{1},\ \mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime}=b_{1}a_{1}\\
\mathsf{x}_{2}=a_{2}b_{2},\ \mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime}=b_{2}a_{2}\end{array}\end{array}

We classify Type
A
potentials on
Q_{3,\{1,2,3\}}
up to isomorphism, and determine the derived equivalence classes among those with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebras. This generalises results in
[
DWZ
,
E2
,
H2
]
.

Notation 6.1
.

In this section, for simplicity, we will adopt the following notation.
Recall the notation
f_{d}
,
f_{\geq d}
in
4.11
.

(1)

Write
Q
for
Q_{3,\{1,2,3\}}
,
\mathsf{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{y}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}_{2}
, whereas
\mathsf{x}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}_{1}
and
\mathsf{y}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}_{2}^{\prime}
.

(2)

Suppose that
f
is a Type
A
potential on
Q
.
Since the aim of this section is to classify Type
A
potentials up to isomorphism,
we may assume that
f
is reduced.
Define the
base part
of
f
by

f_{b}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q},

where
\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}
and
\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
are the lowest degree (nonzero) pure powers of
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}

appearing in
f
, respectively.
Since
f
is reduced, we necessarily have
p,q\geq 2
.
If no pure power of
\mathsf{x}
(respectively,
\mathsf{y}
) appears in
f
, we set
\upkappa_{1}=0
(respectively,
\upkappa_{2}=0
).
The
redundant part
of
f
is then defined by
f_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f-f_{b}
.

(3)

Given any Type A potential
f
on
Q
with
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
, we give a new definition of degree as follows, which differs from
4.3
. For any
t\geq 0
, define

\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{x}^{p+t})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t+2,\quad\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{y}^{q+t})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t+2.

The degree of binomials in
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
is the same as
4.3
.
We also write
f_{d}
for the degree
d
piece of
f
with respect to this new grading (overwriting
4.11
). Similar for
f_{ij,d}
,
\mathcal{O}_{d}
and
\mathcal{O}_{ij,d}
.
This new definition of degree is natural since now
f_{b}=f_{2}
and
f_{r}=f_{\geq 3}
, which will unify the proof below.

(4)

Given any Type A potential
f
on
Q
with
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
, consider the matrices

A_{11}(f)=\begin{bmatrix}p\upkappa_{1}\end{bmatrix},\qquad A_{22}(f)=\begin{bmatrix}q\upkappa_{2}\end{bmatrix},\qquad A_{12}(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{bmatrix}\upvarepsilon_{12}(f)&1\\
1&\upvarepsilon_{22}(f)\end{bmatrix},

where
\upvarepsilon_{12}(f)=\begin{cases}2\upkappa_{1}\text{ if }p=2\\
0\ \ \ \text{ if }p>2\end{cases}

and

\upvarepsilon_{22}(f)=\begin{cases}2\upkappa_{2}\text{ if }q=2\\
0\ \ \ \text{ if }q>2\end{cases}
.

6.1.
Normalization

The purpose of this subsection is to prove
6.19
, which gives the isomorphism classes of Type A potentials on
Q
.

Notation 6.2
.

In this section, we assume
f=f_{b}+f_{r}
is a Type A potential on
Q
with
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
, and will freely use the notations
f_{d}
,
f_{\geq d}
,
f_{ij,d}
,
\mathcal{O}_{d}
and
\mathcal{O}_{ij,d}
in
6.1
(3).

The following results show that, up to right-equivalence, we can freely commute occurrences of
\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}
into
\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
in the redundant part
f_{r}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that
f_{r}=g+\uplambda\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}c
where
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{deg}(\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}c)
and
c
is a cycle with
\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 1
. Then there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\upvartheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\uplambda\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}c+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Proof.

We apply the unitriangular automorphism
\upvartheta
of depth
d-1
given by

\upvartheta:a_{1}\mapsto a_{1}-\uplambda a_{1}c
,
b_{1}\mapsto b_{1}+\uplambda cb_{1}
.
Equivalently, since
\mathsf{x}=b_{1}a_{1}
, the induced action is

\upvartheta(\mathsf{x})=\mathsf{x}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}c+\uplambda c\mathsf{x}-\uplambda^{2}c\mathsf{x}c.

Applying
\upvartheta
to
f
gives

\displaystyle\upvartheta\colon f

\displaystyle\mapsto\upkappa_{1}(\mathsf{x}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}c+\uplambda c\mathsf{x}-\uplambda^{2}c\mathsf{x}c)^{p}+(\mathsf{x}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}c+\uplambda c\mathsf{x}-\uplambda^{2}c\mathsf{x}c)\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}c+g+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle=f_{b}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}c\mathsf{y}+\uplambda c\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}c+g+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(since
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
)

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\uplambda\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}c+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

The above equality holds as follows.
First, as in the proof of
4.15
, all terms produced from

f_{r}=g+\uplambda\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}c
under
\upvartheta
have degree at least
d+1
, and
may therefore be absorbed into
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
in the first line.
Second, since
\mathrm{deg}(c)\geq 1
by assumption and
p\geq 2
as specified in
6.1
(2), every term in

\upkappa_{1}\big(\mathsf{x}-\uplambda\mathsf{x}c+\uplambda c\mathsf{x}-\uplambda^{2}c\mathsf{x}c\big)^{p}

other than
\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}
has degree at least
d+1
; similarly,

\mathrm{deg}(\uplambda^{2}c\mathsf{x}c\mathsf{y})\geq d+1
.
Hence all such terms are again absorbed in
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
in the second line.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose that
f_{r}=g+\uplambda c
where
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
d\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathrm{deg}(c)
and
c
is a cycle with
\mathbf{T}(c)_{1}=i
and
\mathbf{T}(c)_{2}=j
. Then there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\uptheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{i}\mathsf{y}^{j}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Proof.

If
j=0
, then
c\sim\mathsf{x}^{i}
, so there is nothing to prove. The case of
i=0
is similar. Thus we assume
i
,
j>0
. Firstly, note that
c\sim\mathsf{x}^{i_{1}}\mathsf{y}^{j_{1}}\mathsf{x}^{i_{2}}\mathsf{y}^{j_{2}}\dots\mathsf{x}^{i_{k}}\mathsf{y}^{j_{k}}
where
\sum_{t=1}^{k}i_{t}=i
and
\sum_{t=1}^{k}j_{t}=j
.
Since lemma
6.3
allows us to commute each occurrence of
\mathsf{y}\mathsf{x}
into
\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
(up to
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
),
iterating it yields a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence

\uptheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{i}\mathsf{y}^{j}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.\qed

Remark 6.5
.

With notation as in
6.4
, any term
\uplambda c
of degree
d=\mathrm{deg}(c)
can be rewritten, up to
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
, in the form
\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{i}\mathsf{y}^{j}
.
Henceforth, since we normalise the potential degree by degree, we may assume throughout this section that
c=\mathsf{x}^{i}\mathsf{y}^{j}
.

We now normalise
f
degree by degree: the base part
f_{b}
is used to eliminate higher-degree terms in
f_{r}
via unitriangular automorphisms.

For any integer
s\geq 1
, we define the following depth
s+1
unitriangular automorphisms.

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{11,s}\colon a_{1}\mapsto a_{1}+\uplambda a_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s},

(6.A)

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{22,s}\colon a_{2}\mapsto a_{2}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{s}a_{2},

(6.B)

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{12,s}\colon a_{1}\mapsto a_{1}+\uplambda_{1}a_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s-1}\mathsf{y},\ a_{2}\mapsto a_{2}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s}a_{2},

(6.C)

where
\uplambda
,
\uplambda_{1}
,
\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The
\upvarphi_{11,s}
(
6.A
) induces a degree
s+1
right-equivalence,

\upvarphi_{11,s}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f+\uplambda p\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+s}+\mathcal{O}_{12,s+2}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}.

Proof.

Applying
\upvarphi_{11,s}\colon a_{1}\mapsto a_{1}+\uplambda a_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s}
to
f
gives

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{11,s}\colon f

\displaystyle\mapsto\upkappa_{1}(b_{1}(a_{1}+\uplambda a_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s}))^{p}+b_{1}(a_{1}+\uplambda a_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s})\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}+f_{r}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f+\uplambda p\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+s}+\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{s+1}\mathsf{y}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}

\displaystyle=f+\uplambda p\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+s}+\mathcal{O}_{12,s+2}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}.\qed

Lemma 6.7
.

The
\upvarphi_{22,s}
(
6.B
) induces a degree
s+1
right-equivalence,

\upvarphi_{22,s}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f+\uplambda q\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q+s}+\mathcal{O}_{12,s+2}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
6.6
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Lemma
6.7
can be deduced from
6.6
by applying
the automorphism of
Q
which interchanges
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
and fixes
e_{2}
.
Explicitly, this automorphism is given by

a_{1}\mapsto b_{2},\quad b_{1}\mapsto a_{2},\quad a_{2}\mapsto b_{1},\quad b_{2}\mapsto a_{1},

or equivalently, it interchanges
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

The
\upvarphi_{12,s}
(
6.C
) induces a degree
s+1
right-equivalence,

\upvarphi_{12,s}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f+\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{x}^{s+1}\mathsf{y}&\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}A_{12}(f)\begin{bmatrix}\uplambda_{1}\\
\uplambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}.

Proof.

Applying
\upvarphi_{12,s}
to
f
gives

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{12,s}\colon f

\displaystyle\mapsto\upkappa_{1}(\mathsf{x}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y})^{p}+(\mathsf{x}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y})(\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y})+\upkappa_{2}(\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y})^{q}+f_{r}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}f+\uplambda_{1}p\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+s-1}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s+1}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\uplambda_{2}q\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y}^{q}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}

\displaystyle=f+\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{x}^{s+1}\mathsf{y}&\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}A_{12}(f)\begin{bmatrix}\uplambda_{1}\\
\uplambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{s+3}.

Recall the definition of
A_{12}(f)
in
6.1
(4).
In the last line, the term
\uplambda_{1}p\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+s-1}\mathsf{y}
contributes to the displayed matrix expression
only when
p=2
(and is absorbed into
\mathcal{O}_{s+3}
when
p>2
); similarly,

\uplambda_{2}q\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{x}^{s}\mathsf{y}^{q}
contributes only when
q=2
.
∎

Proposition 6.10
.

With notation in
6.2
,
for any
d\geq 3
, there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence

\upphi_{d}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{<d}+c_{d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

where the
c_{d}
is defined to be

c_{d}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{if }\det(A_{12}(f))\neq 0\\
\upmu\mathsf{x}^{p+d-2}&\text{if }\det(A_{12}(f))=0\end{array}\right.

for some
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

We first rewrite
f_{r}=f_{d}+g
and
f_{d}=f_{11,d}+f_{12,d}+f_{22,d}
where
f_{11,d}=\upalpha_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+d-2}
and
f_{22,d}=\upalpha_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q+d-2}
for some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Recall that
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
in
6.2
.
If
\upkappa_{2}=0
, then there is no monomial of
\mathsf{y}
in
f
, and so
\alpha_{2}=0
. Otherwise,
\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
, so set
\uplambda=-\upalpha_{2}/(q\upkappa_{2})
and applying
6.7
to obtain,

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{22,d-2}\colon f

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f+\uplambda q\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q+d-2}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle=f_{b}+g+f_{d}+\uplambda q\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q+d-2}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f=f_{b}+f_{r},\ f_{r}=f_{d}+g
)

\displaystyle=f_{b}+g+f_{11,d}+f_{12,d}+(\upalpha_{2}+\uplambda q\upkappa_{2})\mathsf{y}^{q+d-2}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(
f_{d}=f_{11,d}+f_{12,d}+f_{22,d},\ f_{22,d}=\upalpha_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q+d-2}
)

\displaystyle=f_{b}+g+f_{11,d}+f_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

(since
\uplambda=-\upalpha_{2}/(q\upkappa_{2})
)

\displaystyle=f_{b}+g+f_{11,d}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

(6.D)

Set
\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+g+f_{11,d}+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
.
The proof splits into cases.

(1)
\det(A_{12}(f))=0
.

By
4.19
, when
\det(A_{12}(f))=0
we may absorb the degree
d
binomial terms
\mathcal{O}_{12,d}
into monomial terms in
\mathsf{x}
(up to
\mathcal{O}_{d+1}
). More precisely, there exists a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence,

\displaystyle\rho_{d}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{p+d-2}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1},

(
f_{11,d}=\upalpha_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p+d-2}
)

\displaystyle=f_{<d}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{p+d-2}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

(
f_{b}+g=f_{<d}+f_{>d}
)

for some
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}
. Set
\upphi_{d}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rho_{d}\circ\upvarphi_{22,d-2}
, we are done.

(2)
\det(A_{12}(f))\neq 0
.

Similar to (
6.D
), applying
6.6
to
\mathsf{f}_{1}
gives

\upvarphi_{11,d-2}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+g+\mathcal{O}_{12,d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Then we continue to normalize the
\mathcal{O}_{12,d}
in
\mathsf{f}_{2}
.
By construction,
\mathsf{f}_{2}
satisfies the hypotheses of
4.15
, hence we may apply
4.15
repeatedly to obtain

\displaystyle\upvartheta\colon\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+g+\upbeta\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

for some
\upbeta\in\mathbb{C}
. Then by
6.9
,

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{12,d-2}\colon\mathsf{f}_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{3}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}&\mathsf{x}^{d-2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}A_{12}(f)\begin{bmatrix}\uplambda_{1}\\
\uplambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle=f_{b}+g+\upbeta\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}&\mathsf{x}^{d-2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}A_{12}(f)\begin{bmatrix}\uplambda_{1}\\
\uplambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

Since
\det A_{12}(f)\neq 0
, the linear system in
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
is solvable, so we may choose
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}
such that

\upbeta\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{x}^{d-1}\mathsf{y}&\mathsf{x}^{d-2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\end{bmatrix}A_{12}(f)\begin{bmatrix}\uplambda_{1}\\
\uplambda_{2}\end{bmatrix}=0.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{12,d-2}\colon\mathsf{f}_{3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+g+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}

\displaystyle=f_{<d}+\mathcal{O}_{d+1}.

(
f_{b}+g=f_{<d}+f_{>d}
)

Set
\upphi_{d}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upvarphi_{12,d-2}\circ\upvartheta\circ\upvarphi_{11,d-2}\circ\upvarphi_{22,d-2}
, we are done.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

With notation in
6.2
, there exists a right-equivalence,

\Phi\colon f\rightsquigarrow f_{b}+c

where
c
is given by

c=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{if }\det A_{12}(f)\neq 0\\
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\upmu_{i}\mathsf{x}^{p+i}&\text{if }\det A_{12}(f)=0\end{array}\right.

for some
\upmu_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

We first apply the
\upphi_{3}
in
6.10
,

\upphi_{3}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{<3}+c_{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4},

where the
c_{3}
is the same as in
6.10
. Then we continue to apply the
\upphi_{4}
in
6.10
to
\mathsf{f}_{1}
,

\upphi_{4}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathsf{f}_{1})_{<4}+c_{4}+\mathcal{O}_{5}=f_{<3}+\sum_{d=3}^{4}c_{d}+\mathcal{O}_{5}.

where the
c_{4}
is the same as in
6.10
. For
1\leq s\leq 3
, repeating this process
s-2
times gives

\upphi_{s}\circ\dots\circ\upphi_{4}\circ\upphi_{3}\colon f\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{f}_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{<3}+\sum_{d=3}^{s}c_{d}+\mathcal{O}_{s+1}.

Since
\upphi_{d}
is a path degree
d-1
right-equivalence for each
d\geq 3
by
6.10
, by
2.9

\Phi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\upphi_{s}\circ\dots\circ\upphi_{4}\circ\upphi_{3}
exists, and further

\Phi\colon f\rightsquigarrow f_{<3}+\sum_{d=3}^{\infty}c_{d}.

where each
c_{d}
is the same as in
6.10
.
By the grading in
6.1
(3), we have
f_{<3}=f_{2}=f_{b}
, hence

\Phi\colon f\rightsquigarrow f_{b}+\sum_{d=3}^{\infty}c_{d}.

Thus set
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{d=3}^{\infty}c_{d}
, we are done.
∎

Proposition
6.11
shows that if
\det A_{12}(f)\neq 0
, then all terms of
f_{r}
can be eliminated.
We therefore restrict to the case
\det A_{12}(f)=0
.
The following lemma is immediate from the definition of
A_{12}(f)
in
6.1
(4).

Lemma 6.12
.

\det A_{12}(f)=0
if and only if
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}
with
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
.

Lemma 6.13
.

With notation in
6.2
, suppose that
f
satisfies
\det A_{12}(f)=0
, and
f_{r}=\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+\mathcal{O}_{t}
where
t>s\geq 3
and
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Then there exists a path degree
t-s+1
right-equivalence
\uppsi_{t}
such that

\uppsi_{t}\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}.

Proof.

By
6.12
, we have
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}
with
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
.
If the degree
t
part of
\mathcal{O}_{t}
vanishes, there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise, applying
4.15
repeatedly yields a right-equivalence

\upvartheta\colon f\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+\upbeta\mathsf{x}^{t-1}\mathsf{y}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1},

for some
\upbeta\in\mathbb{C}
. If
\upbeta=0
, we are done. Otherwise, we next apply
\upvarphi_{12,t-s}
in
6.9
which gives

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{12,t-s}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mapsto

\displaystyle\upkappa_{1}(\mathsf{x}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y})^{2}+(\mathsf{x}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y})(\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y})+\upkappa_{2}(\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y})^{2}

\displaystyle+\upmu(\mathsf{x}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y})^{s}+\upbeta\mathsf{x}^{t-1}\mathsf{y}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}

\displaystyle f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+(2\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}+\uplambda_{2})\mathsf{x}^{t-s+1}\mathsf{y}+(\uplambda_{1}+2\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{2})\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y}^{2}+(s\upmu\uplambda_{1}+\upbeta)\mathsf{x}^{t-1}\mathsf{y}

\displaystyle+(\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}^{2}+\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{2}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2})\mathsf{x}^{2(t-s)}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathcal{O}_{t+1}.

Note that in the above simplification we rewrite mixed monomials into the normal form
\mathsf{x}^{i}\mathsf{y}^{j}
(up to higher degree terms) using
6.5
, and then collect like terms.

Since
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
, set
\uplambda_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-2\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}
.
Then

2\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}+\uplambda_{2}=0,\qquad\uplambda_{1}+2\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{2}=\uplambda_{1}-4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{1}=0,

and moreover

\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}^{2}+\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{2}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}=\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}^{2}+4\upkappa_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{2}\uplambda_{1}^{2}-2\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}^{2}=\upkappa_{1}\uplambda_{1}^{2}(4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}-1)=0.

Finally, choose
\uplambda_{1}
so that
s\upmu\uplambda_{1}+\upbeta=0
.
This makes the coefficients of
\mathsf{x}^{t-s+1}\mathsf{y}
,
\mathsf{x}^{t-s}\mathsf{y}^{2}
,
\mathsf{x}^{t-1}\mathsf{y}
and
\mathsf{x}^{2(t-s)}\mathsf{y}^{2}
equal to zero in the above potential. Set
\uppsi_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upvarphi_{12,t-s}\circ\upvartheta
, we are done.
∎

The following shows that when
\det A_{12}(f)=0
, the leading term of
f_{r}
can eliminate all the other terms.

Proposition 6.14
.

With notation in
6.2
, suppose that
f
satisfies
\det A_{12}(f)=0
. Then there exists a right-equivalence
\Psi
such that

\Psi\colon f\rightsquigarrow f_{b}\textnormal{ or }f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s},

where
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
s\geq 3
.

Proof.

Since
\det A_{12}(f)=0
, then by
6.11

\Phi\colon f\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\upmu_{i}\mathsf{x}^{i+2}.

If all
\upmu_{i}=0
, then
f\rightsquigarrow f_{b}
.
Otherwise, let
s\geq 3
be minimal such that the coefficient of
\mathsf{x}^{s}
in
\mathsf{f}_{1}
is nonzero,
and write this coefficient as
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
Applying
6.13
to
\mathsf{f}_{1}
, there exists a right-equivalence

\uppsi_{s+1}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightsquigarrow}\limits}}\mathsf{f}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+\mathcal{O}_{s+2}.

Thus, repeating this process
k
times gives

\uppsi_{s+k}\circ\dots\circ\uppsi_{s+2}\circ\uppsi_{s+1}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{f}_{k+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}+\mathcal{O}_{s+k+1}.

Since by
6.13
each
\uppsi_{t}
is a degree
t-s+1
right-equivalence for
t>s
,
2.9
implies that

\Psi^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\uppsi_{s+k}\circ\dots\circ\uppsi_{s+2}\circ\uppsi_{s+1}
exists, and further

\Psi^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{f}_{1}\rightsquigarrow f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}.

Set
\Psi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Psi^{\prime}\circ\Phi
, we are done.
∎

Combining
6.11
,
6.12
and
6.14
gives the following result.

Proposition 6.15
.

Any Type A potential on
Q
must be right-equivalent to one of the following potentials:

(1)

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}
where
\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\neq 1
.

(2)

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}
where
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
,
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
s\geq 3
.

(3)

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
where
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
and
\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(4)

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}
where
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
.

(5)

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
where
p\geq 2
and
\upkappa_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(6)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
where
q\geq 2
and
\upkappa_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(7)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
.

Proof.

Recall in
6.1
and
6.2
, any Type A potential on
Q
has the form of
f=f_{b}+f_{r}
where
f_{b}=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
.

When
\det A_{12}(f)=0
, namely
p=q=2
and
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
by
6.12
, then by
6.11

f\cong f_{b}\textnormal{ or }f_{b}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}
where
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
s\geq 3
. This gives exactly the cases (4) and (2).

When
\det A_{12}(f)\neq 0
, by
6.11

f\cong f_{b}
. Again by
6.12
, we have
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
or
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\neq 1
, so
f
must belong to one of the following cases.

a)

\upkappa_{1}\neq 0
and
\upkappa_{2}=0
.

b)

\upkappa_{1}=0
and
\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
.

c)

\upkappa_{1}=0
and
\upkappa_{2}=0
.

d)

\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
,
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\neq 1
and
p=q=2
.

e)

\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

The a), b), c), d) and e) are items (5), (6), (7), (1) and (3) in the statement.
∎

Then we continue to normalize the coefficients of the potentials in
6.15
.

Corollary 6.16
.

Any Type A potential on
Q
must be isomorphic to one of the following potentials:

(1)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
where
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
.

(2)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{s}
where
s\geq 3
.

(3)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}
where
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

(4)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

(5)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
where
p\geq 2
.

(6)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}
where
q\geq 2
.

(7)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
.

Proof.

(1) Applying
a_{1}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}a_{1},\ a_{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{2}a_{2}
where
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
to (
1
) gives

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}.

Since
\upkappa_{1}\neq 0
, we can solve for
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
such that

\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}=\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}=1
. Moreover, since
\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\neq 1
,
\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}=\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}\neq 0,\frac{1}{4}
. Set
\uplambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}
.
Thus
\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\mapsto\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
where
\uplambda\neq 0,\frac{1}{4}
.

(2) Applying
\varphi\colon a_{1}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}a_{1},\ a_{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{2}a_{2}
where
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
to (
2
) gives

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}^{s}\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}.

We next claim that we can find some
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
which satisfies

\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}:\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}:\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}:\uplambda_{1}^{s}\upmu=1:1:\frac{1}{4}:1.

Once the claim is certified, it follows at once that

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\upmu\mathsf{x}^{s}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{s}
.

To prove the claim, since
s\geq 3
and
\upmu\neq 0
, we can solve for
\uplambda_{1}
such that
\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}:\uplambda_{1}^{s}\upmu=1:1
holds. Then we solve
\uplambda_{2}
from
\uplambda_{1}
and
\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}:\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}=1:1
. Moreover, this choice of
\uplambda_{1}
and
\uplambda_{2}
also satisfies
\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}:\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}=1:\frac{1}{4}
since
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
. Combining these together,
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
satisfies the claim.

(3) Applying
\varphi\colon a_{1}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}a_{1},\ a_{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{2}a_{2}
where
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
to (
3
) gives

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}^{p}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}^{q}\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}.

Similar to (2), the statement follows once we find some
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
which satisfies

\uplambda_{1}^{p}\upkappa_{1}:\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}:\uplambda_{2}^{q}\upkappa_{2}=1:1:1.

The above equations induce
\uplambda_{2}=\uplambda_{1}^{p-1}\upkappa_{1}
and
\uplambda_{1}=\uplambda_{2}^{q-1}\upkappa_{2}
, and so
\uplambda_{1}^{(p-1)(q-1)-1}\upkappa_{1}^{q-1}\upkappa_{2}=1
. Since
\upkappa_{1}
,
\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
, we can solve
\uplambda_{1}
, and then
\uplambda_{2}
such that the above equations hold.

(4) Applying
a_{1}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}a_{1},\ a_{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{2}a_{2}
where
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
to (
4
) gives

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}.

Similar to (1), we can solve for
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
such that

\uplambda_{1}^{2}\upkappa_{1}=\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}=1
holds, and then
\uplambda_{2}^{2}\upkappa_{2}=\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=\frac{1}{4}
since
4\upkappa_{1}\upkappa_{2}=1
. Thus
\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}\mapsto\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

(5) Applying
a_{1}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}a_{1},\ a_{2}\mapsto\uplambda_{2}a_{2}
where
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
to (
5
) gives

\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mapsto\uplambda_{1}^{p}\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}.

Since
\upkappa_{1}\neq 0
, we can solve for
(\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2})
such that

\uplambda_{1}^{p}\upkappa_{1}=\uplambda_{1}\uplambda_{2}=1
holds. Thus
\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mapsto\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
.

The proof of (6) and (7) is similar to (5).
∎

We now simplify the previous geometric realization in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{GR}
for the potentials in
6.16
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Each Jacobi algebra of potentials in
6.16
is realized by a crepant resolution of a
cA_{3}
singularity
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]/(uv-h_{0}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3})
, which corresponds to the
\mathcal{R}
-module
M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1}h_{2})
in
3.9
as follows.

h_{0}

h_{1}

h_{2}

h_{3}

(1)

2x+y

x

y

x+2\uplambda y

(2)

2x+y+sx^{s-1}

x

y

x+\frac{1}{2}y

(3)

px^{p-1}+y

x

y

x+qy^{q-1}

(4)

2x+y

x

y

x+\frac{1}{2}y

(5)

px^{p-1}+y

x

y

x

(6)

y

x

y

x+qy^{q-1}

(7)

y

x

y

x

Proof.

In order to construct the geometric realization by
5.9
and (
5.B
), we first transform the potentials in
6.16
to some potentials in
Q_{3}
, which has a single loop at each vertex, as illustrated below (see also
4.2
).

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle a_{1}
\scriptstyle b_{1}
\scriptstyle a_{2}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle l_{1}
\scriptstyle l_{2}
\scriptstyle l_{3}
Q_{3}=

Consider a potential
f=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}+\upkappa_{3}\mathsf{x}^{s}
on
Q
where
\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2},\upkappa_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
. By applying
4.22
three times, each of which adds a loop
l_{i}
at vertex
i
of
Q
for
1\leq i\leq 3
, we have
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{3},f^{\prime})
where

f^{\prime}=l_{1}\mathsf{x}^{\prime}+\mathsf{x}l_{2}+l_{2}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{\prime}l_{3}-\frac{1}{2}l_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}l_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}l_{3}^{2}-\mathsf{x}^{2}-\mathsf{y}^{2}+\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}+\upkappa_{3}\mathsf{x}^{s}.

Then by
5.9
,
5.2
and (
5.B
), we can realize
f^{\prime}
by setting
g_{2}=x
,
g_{3}=x+y
and then solving the following system of equations where each
g_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[x,y]\kern-1.0pt]

\displaystyle g_{0}-g_{1}+g_{2}=0

\displaystyle g_{1}-2g_{2}+\upkappa_{1}pg_{2}^{p-1}+\upkappa_{3}sg_{2}^{s-1}+g_{3}=0

\displaystyle g_{2}-g_{3}+g_{4}=0

\displaystyle g_{3}-g_{4}+\upkappa_{2}g_{4}^{q-1}+g_{5}=0

\displaystyle g_{4}-g_{5}+g_{6}=0.

Thus
(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6})=(-\upkappa_{1}px^{p-1}-\upkappa_{3}sx^{s-1}-y,x-\upkappa_{1}px^{p-1}-\upkappa_{3}sx^{s-1}-y,x,x+y,y,-x+y-\upkappa_{2}qy^{q-1},-x-\upkappa_{2}qy^{q-1})
. Set
(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-g_{0},g_{2},g_{4},-g_{6})
and
consider

\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-h_{0}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-(\upkappa_{1}px^{p-1}+\upkappa_{3}sx^{s-1}+y)xy(x+\upkappa_{2}qy^{q-1})}

and
\mathcal{R}
-module
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1}h_{2})
. Write
\uppi
for the crepant resolution of
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
, which corresponds to
M
in
3.9
. Then
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{3},f^{\prime})
by
5.9
, and so
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)
.
Specialising
(\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2},\upkappa_{3},p,q,s)
to match the seven families in
6.16

yields the table, and hence the claim.
∎

We now classify Type A potentials on
Q
up to isomorphism; this is the main result of the section.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
\uplambda_{1},\uplambda_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
with
\uplambda_{1}\neq\uplambda_{2}
. Then we have
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{y}^{2})\not\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{y}^{2})
.

Proof.

Write
f_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda_{i}\mathsf{y}^{2}
for
i=1,2
.
We argue by contradiction, assuming that there exists an algebra isomorphism

\phi\colon\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})
.

By
6.17
, each
f_{i}
is realised by a crepant resolution
\uppi_{i}
of a
cA_{3}
singularity with

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{i})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{i})
.
Hence
3.11
implies that
\phi
either fixes each idempotent
e_{i}
, or sends
e_{i}
to
e_{4-i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
. We consider only the first case; the second is similar.
Recall that the doubled
A_{3}
quiver
Q
on which
f_{i}
is defined:

\begin{array}[]{cl}\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to124.46pt{\vbox to40.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 62.23158pt\lower-21.4175pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}
}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
