{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.29 3.05 C 26.01 15.02 52.73 15.02 72.25 3.75}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.86603}{-0.5}{0.5}{0.86603}{52.2166pt}{2.70715pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.86603 -0.5 0.5 0.86603 72.25 3.75)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.11 0 C -0.28 0.28 -1.66 0.83 -3.32 1.8 C -1.66 0.55 -1.66 -0.55 -3.32 -1.8 C -1.66 -0.83 -0.28 -0.28 1.11 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{24.33249pt}{13.2234pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 33.67 18.3)} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{x}=b_{1}a_{1}\\
\mathsf{y}=a_{2}b_{2}\end{array}\end{array}

Write
\mathfrak{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\kern-2.5pt(a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2})\kern-2.5pt)
for the arrow ideal.
Since
\phi(e_{i})=e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
,
\phi
preserves sources and targets, so

\phi(a_{1})\in e_{1}\mathfrak{m}e_{2},\quad\phi(b_{1})\in e_{2}\mathfrak{m}e_{1},\quad\phi(a_{2})\in e_{2}\mathfrak{m}e_{3},\quad\phi(b_{2})\in e_{3}\mathfrak{m}e_{2}.

Moreover, the induced map on
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
is invertible, hence

\phi\colon a_{1}\mapsto c_{1}a_{1}+r_{1},\quad b_{1}\mapsto c_{2}b_{1}+r_{2},\quad a_{2}\mapsto c_{3}a_{2}+r_{3},\quad b_{2}\mapsto c_{4}b_{2}+r_{4},

where each
c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
r_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}^{2}
.
To eliminate the higher-order terms
r_{i}
, we pass to the quotient modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{4}
.
Since
\phi(\mathfrak{m})=\mathfrak{m}
, it induces an isomorphism
\bar{\phi}\colon\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})/\mathfrak{m}^{4}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})/\mathfrak{m}^{4}
. Since for each arrow
a
the relation
\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}f_{1}
is homogeneous of arrow-length
3
,
any term in
\phi(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}f_{1})
involving at least one of the
r_{i}

has arrow-length at least
4
, and hence lies in
\mathfrak{m}^{4}
. It follows that
\bar{\phi}
depends only on the linear part of
\phi
, that is

\bar{\phi}\colon a_{1}\mapsto c_{1}a_{1},\quad b_{1}\mapsto c_{2}b_{1},\quad a_{2}\mapsto c_{3}a_{2},\quad b_{2}\mapsto c_{4}b_{2}.

By a slight abuse of notation, we regard
\bar{\phi}
as a homomorphism on
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
and write

\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1}))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{1}}f_{1}),\,\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{1}}f_{1}),\,\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{2}}f_{1}),\,\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{2}}f_{1}))\kern-2.5pt)

for the ideal of
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle
generated by these elements.
Since
\bar{\phi}
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{1})/\mathfrak{m}^{4}\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{2})/\mathfrak{m}^{4}
,
it follows that the ideals
\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1}))
and
J(f_{2})

generate the same ideal in
\mathbb{C}\langle\kern-2.5pt\langle Q\rangle\kern-2.5pt\rangle/\mathfrak{m}^{4}
.
Therefore,

(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),\,J(f_{2}),\,\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)=(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\,\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt).

The Jacobi ideal
J(f_{1})
is generated by:

\displaystyle\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{1}}f_{1}

\displaystyle=2b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}=2\mathsf{x}b_{1}+\mathsf{y}b_{1},

\displaystyle\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{1}}f_{1}

\displaystyle=2a_{1}b_{1}a_{1}+a_{1}a_{2}b_{2}=2a_{1}\mathsf{x}+a_{1}\mathsf{y},

\displaystyle\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{2}}f_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{2}b_{1}a_{1}+2\uplambda_{1}b_{2}a_{2}b_{2}=b_{2}\mathsf{x}+2\uplambda_{1}b_{2}\mathsf{y},

\displaystyle\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{2}}f_{1}

\displaystyle=b_{1}a_{1}a_{2}+2\uplambda_{1}a_{2}b_{2}a_{2}=\mathsf{x}a_{2}+2\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{y}a_{2}.

Thus
\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{1}}f_{1})=2c_{1}c_{2}^{2}b_{1}a_{1}b_{1}+c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}\propto 2\mathsf{x}b_{1}+\uplambda^{\prime}\mathsf{y}b_{1}
where
\uplambda^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}c_{3}c_{4}/(c_{1}c_{2})\neq 0
, and
\propto
denotes equality up to multiplication by a non-zero scalar.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{1}}f_{1})\propto 2a_{1}\mathsf{x}+\uplambda^{\prime}a_{1}\mathsf{y},\quad\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{a_{2}}f_{1})\propto b_{2}\mathsf{x}+2\uplambda^{\prime}\uplambda_{1}b_{2}\mathsf{y},\quad\bar{\phi}(\textnormal{\reflectbox{6}}_{b_{2}}f_{1})\propto\mathsf{x}a_{2}+2\uplambda^{\prime}\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{y}a_{2}.

Compare the generators of
\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1}))
(left column) and
J(f_{2})
(right column):

\displaystyle 2\mathsf{x}b_{1}+\uplambda^{\prime}\mathsf{y}b_{1},

(6.E)

\displaystyle 2a_{1}\mathsf{x}+\uplambda^{\prime}a_{1}\mathsf{y},

(6.F)

\displaystyle b_{2}\mathsf{x}+2\uplambda^{\prime}\uplambda_{1}b_{2}\mathsf{y},

(6.G)

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}a_{2}+2\uplambda^{\prime}\uplambda_{1}\mathsf{y}a_{2}.

(6.H)

\displaystyle 2\mathsf{x}b_{1}+\mathsf{y}b_{1},

(6.I)

\displaystyle 2a_{1}\mathsf{x}+a_{1}\mathsf{y},

(6.J)

\displaystyle b_{2}\mathsf{x}+2\uplambda_{2}b_{2}\mathsf{y},

(6.K)

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}a_{2}+2\uplambda_{2}\mathsf{y}a_{2}.

(6.L)

Recall that
\uplambda^{\prime}\neq 0
and
\uplambda_{1}\neq\uplambda_{2}
.
We split the proof into three cases.

(1)
\uplambda^{\prime}\neq 1
.

Our strategy is to show that the four paths

\mathsf{x}b_{1},\ \mathsf{y}b_{1},\ a_{1}\mathsf{x},\ a_{1}\mathsf{y}

lie in the ideal
(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
, while none of them lies in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
. This yields a contradiction, since
(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)=(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

Since
\mathfrak{m}^{4}
has arrow-length at least
4
, any arrow-length
3
element of
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
must lie in the
\mathbb{C}
-span of (
6.I
)–(
6.L
).
But none of
\mathsf{x}b_{1},\ \mathsf{y}b_{1},\ a_{1}\mathsf{x},\ a_{1}\mathsf{y}
lies in this span.
Hence none of them lies in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

Since
\uplambda^{\prime}\neq 1
, comparing (
6.E
) with (
6.I
) yields
\mathsf{x}b_{1},\mathsf{y}b_{1}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}))\kern-2.5pt)
, and similarly, comparing (
6.F
) with (
6.J
) yields
a_{1}\mathsf{x},a_{1}\mathsf{y}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}))\kern-2.5pt)
. Consequently,

\mathsf{x}b_{1},\mathsf{y}b_{1},a_{1}\mathsf{x},a_{1}\mathsf{y}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt),

which contradicts the fact that none of these elements lies in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

(2)
\uplambda^{\prime}=1
and
\uplambda_{2}\neq 0
.

In this case the argument is analogous, but uses the relations involving
b_{2}
and
a_{2}
.
Since
\mathfrak{m}^{4}
has arrow-length at least
4
, the arrow-length
3
component of
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
coincides with the
\mathbb{C}
-span of the generators
(
6.I
)–(
6.L
). As
\uplambda_{2}\neq 0
, none of the paths

b_{2}\mathsf{x},\ b_{2}\mathsf{y},\ \mathsf{x}a_{2},\ \mathsf{y}a_{2}

lies in this span. Hence none of them lies in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

Since
\uplambda^{\prime}=1
, the comparisons used in case (1) are no longer available. However, since
\uplambda_{1}\neq\uplambda_{2}
by assumption, comparing (
6.G
) with (
6.K
)
and (
6.H
) with (
6.L
) yields

b_{2}\mathsf{x},b_{2}\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x}a_{2},\mathsf{y}a_{2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}))\kern-2.5pt)\subset(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt),

which contradicts the fact that none of these elements lies in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

(3)
\uplambda_{2}=0
.

This case differs from case (2) only in that when
\uplambda_{2}=0
,
the relations (
6.K
) and (
6.L
) imply

b_{2}\mathsf{x},\ \mathsf{x}a_{2}\in J(f_{2})
.
However, since
\mathfrak{m}^{4}
has arrow-length at least
4
,
the arrow-length
3
component of
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)

is again the
\mathbb{C}
-span of (
6.I
)–(
6.L
).
But the paths
b_{2}\mathsf{y},\mathsf{y}a_{2}
do not lie in this span, and hence do not lie in
(\kern-2.5pt(J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
.

Since
\uplambda_{2}=0
and
\uplambda_{1}\neq\uplambda_{2}
, we have
\uplambda_{1}\neq 0
.
Thus comparing (
6.G
) with (
6.K
) and (
6.H
) with (
6.L
) gives
b_{2}\mathsf{y},\mathsf{y}a_{2}\in(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}))\kern-2.5pt)\subset(\kern-2.5pt(\bar{\phi}(J(f_{1})),J(f_{2}),\mathfrak{m}^{4})\kern-2.5pt)
, again a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 6.19
.

Any Type A potential on
Q
must be isomorphic to one of the following isomorphism classes of potentials:

(1)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
for any
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
.

(2)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{s}
for any
s\geq 3
.

(3)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\cong\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
for any
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

(4)

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

(5)

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\cong\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
for any
p\geq 2
.

(6)

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
.

The Jacobi algebras of these potentials are all mutually non-isomorphic (except for those isomorphisms stated), and in particular, the Jacobi algebras with different parameters in the same item are non-isomorphic.

The Jacobi algebras in
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
are finite-dimensional and realized by crepant resolutions of isolated
cA_{3}
singularities, and those in
(4)
,
(5)
,
(6)
are infinite-dimensional and realized by crepant resolutions of non-isolated
cA_{3}
singularities.

Proof.

We first prove the isomorphisms in the statement. Applying
a_{1}\mapsto b_{2},b_{1}\mapsto a_{2},a_{2}\mapsto b_{1},b_{2}\mapsto a_{1}
gives

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p},\ \mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}.

Then we prove the non-isomorphisms in the statement by using the following fact. If Type A potentials
f
and
g
on
Q
are isomorphic, then
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)=\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)
, and further by
3.11
there is an equality of sets

\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{3}\}=\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(g)/e_{3}\}.

(6.M)

The following table lists
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
,
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{1}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{3}
for each
f
in each item, using Toda’s formula (see
[
T
, §4.4]
).

\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)

\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{1}

\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)/e_{3}

(1)

20

6

6

(2)

9s+2

6

6

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}

4p+4q+4

4q-2

4p-2

(4)

\infty

6

6

\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}

\infty

\infty

4p-2

(6)

\infty

\infty

\infty

Now, all Jacobi algebras in (1) have dimension
20
, but are mutually non-isomorphic by
6.18
.
All Jacobi algebras in (2) are mutually non-isomorphic since they all have different dimensions.

For (3), we only need to prove that
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\not\cong\mathsf{x}^{r}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{s}
for any
(p,q)\neq(r,s)
and
(p,q)\neq(s,r)
. From the above table,

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q})/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q})/e_{3}\}=\{4q-2,4p-2\},

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{r}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{s})/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{r}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{s})/e_{3}\}=\{4r-2,4s-2\}.

Since
(p,q)\neq(r,s)
and
(p,q)\neq(s,r)
, then the above two sets are not equal, and so
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\not\cong\mathsf{x}^{r}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{s}
by (
6.M
).

For (5), since
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\cong\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
, we only need to prove that
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\not\cong\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
for any
p\neq q
. From the above table,

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})/e_{3}\}=\{\infty,4p-2\},

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})/e_{1},\ \operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y})/e_{3}\}=\{\infty,4q-2\}.

Since
p\neq q
, the above two sets are not equal, so
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\not\cong\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}
by (
6.M
).

The above shows that potentials in the same item are mutually non-isomorphic.
We finally prove that the potentials in different items are mutually non-isomorphic.

Since Jacobi algebras in (1), (2) and (3) have finite dimension, while those in (4), (5) and (6) have infinite dimension, we only need to prove that the potentials in (1), (2) and (3) are mutually non-isomorphic, and the potentials in (4), (5) and (6) are mutually non-isomorphic, respectively.

From the above table, the Jacobi algebras in (1), (2), and (3) have dimensions
20
,
9s+2
and
4p+4q+4
, respectively. Since
s\geq 3
and
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
, then
9s+2>20
and
4p+4q+4>20
, and so the potentials in (1) are not isomorphic to those in (2) and (3).
To compare the potentials in (2) and (3), since
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
, then
\{4q-2,4p-2\}\neq\{6,6\}
, then the potentials in (2) are not isomorphic to those in (3), by (
6.M
) and the table.

To compare the potentials in (4), (5) and (6), since
\{6,6\}
,
\{\infty,4p-2\}
and
\{\infty,\infty\}
are mutually not equal, then the potentials in (4), (5) and (6) are mutually non-isomorphic by (
6.M
) and the table.

By the geometric realizations in
6.17
, the Jacobi algebras in (1), (2), (3) are realized by crepant resolutions of isolated
cA_{3}
singularities, and those in (4), (5), (6) are realized by crepant resolutions of non-isolated
cA_{3}
singularities.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

In
6.19
, the class
(4)
can be viewed as a limit of
(2)
as
s\to\infty

or of
(1)
as
\uplambda\to\frac{1}{4}
.
Similarly, the classes
(5)
and
(6)
arise as limits of
(3)

as
p\to\infty
and
q\to\infty
, respectively.
This parallels the general phenomenon that divisor-to-curve contractions often occur as limits of flops; see also
[
BW
]
.
In the proof of
6.19
, we establish separately the finiteness of the dimension of the Jacobi algebra and the realisation as a crepant resolution of an isolated
cA_{3}
singularity.
However, by
3.5
, either of these conditions implies the other.

Remark 6.21
.

In this section, for a Type A potential
f
on the doubled
A_{3}
quiver without loops,
we normalise
f
using the matrix
A_{12}(f)
(see
6.1
(4)),
which also appears in
[
Z
, §4.2]
.
For Type A potentials on the doubled
A_{3}
quiver with loops,
or more generally on the doubled
A_{n}
quiver
Q_{n}
with
n\geq 4
,
one would instead need to employ matrices of the form
A_{ij}(f)
with
j-i\geq 2
in order to carry out an analogous normalisation procedure.
At present, it is not clear how to extend the normalisation method developed here to Type A potentials on
Q_{n}
for arbitrary
n
.

6.2.
Derived equivalence classes

The purpose of this subsection is to prove
6.28
, which describes the derived equivalence classes of Type A potentials on
Q
whose Jacobi algebras are finite-dimensional.
Throughout this subsection we restrict to this finite-dimensional case, since
3.7
applies only to isolated singularities.

Given a Type A potential
f
on
Q
, by
6.16
and
6.17
we can realize
f
by a
cA_{3}
singularity

\mathcal{R}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-h_{0}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}}

together with the
\mathcal{R}
-module
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1}h_{2})
. Let
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be the corresponding crepant resolution. Then
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
.

Notation 6.22
.

We adopt the following notation. We first recall
\uppi^{i}
,
\mathcal{X}^{i}
,
M^{i}
and
\uppi^{\mathbf{r}}
,
\mathcal{X}^{\mathbf{r}}
,
M^{\mathbf{r}}
in
3.8
. By
5.15
, there is a Type A potential
g
on
Q_{3,I}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{\mathbf{r}})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{3,I},g)
for some
I\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
, and we set
f^{\mathbf{r}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}g
, which is well defined up to the isomorphism of Jacobi algebras. For
1\leq i\leq 3
, write
f^{i}
for
f^{(i)}
.

Since we are interested only in derived equivalence classes, working with
f^{\mathbf{r}}
and
f^{i}
up to isomorphism of Jacobi algebras causes no ambiguity.
By
3.9
, we have

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{\mathbf{r}})\cong\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{\mathbf{r}})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{\mathbf{r}}).

If, in addition,
\mathcal{R}
is isolated, then
3.7
yields
f^{\mathbf{r}}\simeq f

(in the sense of
2.8
).
Moreover, by
3.5
,
\mathcal{R}
is isolated if and only if
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)<\infty
,
equivalently, if and only if
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite-dimensional.

Consequently, to determine derived equivalence classes of Type A potentials on
Q
with
\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)<\infty
, it suffices to study iterated flops of crepant resolutions of isolated
cA_{3}
singularities.
The finiteness assumption is essential here, since
3.7
requires the base singularity to be isolated.

In order to present the NCCRs
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M)
and
\End_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{\mathbf{r}})
, we adopt the following.

Definition 6.23
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the quiver obtained from
Q
by adding a new vertex
0
, arrows
a_{0},b_{0}
between
0
and
1
, arrows
a_{3},b_{3}
between
0
and
3
, and possibly a loop at
0
, as illustrated below.

Since
f^{i}
might be a potential in
Q_{3,I}
for some
I\neq\emptyset
, and we aim to classify the derived equivalence classes of Type A potentials on
Q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q_{3,\emptyset}
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.24
.

Given a Type A potential
f=\upkappa_{1}\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\upkappa_{2}\mathsf{y}^{q}
in
Q
, the following statements hold.

(1)

\upkappa_{1}\neq 0
and
p=2

\iff

f^{1}
is a potential on
Q
.

(2)

\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
q=2

\iff

f^{3}
is a potential on
Q
.

(3)

\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
p=q=2

\iff

f^{2}
is a potential on
Q
.

Proof.

By
6.17
, the potential
f
can be realised by

\mathcal{R}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-h_{0}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}}

with associated
\mathcal{R}
-module
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1}h_{2})
where

h_{0}=\upkappa_{1}px^{p-1}+y,\quad h_{1}=x,\quad h_{2}=y,\quad h_{3}=x+\upkappa_{2}qy^{q-1}.

Recall from notation
6.22
that for each
1\leq i\leq 3
, the
f^{i}
is realised by
\uppi^{i}\colon\mathcal{X}^{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{i})\cong\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{i})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q_{3,I_{i}},f^{i})
for some subset
I_{i}\subseteq\{1,2,3\}
.

(1) By
3.9
,
M^{1}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{1}h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{1}h_{0}h_{2})
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}
is given pictorially by

Thus
I_{3}=\emptyset
if and only if
\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
q=2
.

(3) Similarly, by
3.9

M^{2}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{2})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{2}h_{1})
and
\mathcal{X}^{2}
is depicted as

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{X}^{2}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to117.68pt{\vbox to29.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.62529pt\lower-13.09216pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Thus
I_{2}=\emptyset
if and only if
\upkappa_{1},\upkappa_{2}\neq 0
and
p=q=2
.
∎

Lemma 6.25
.

Suppose that
f
is a Type A potential on
Q
. Then the following holds.

(1)

If
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
with
\uplambda\neq 0
, then
f^{1}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+(\frac{1}{4}-\uplambda)\mathsf{y}^{2}\cong f^{3}
and
f^{2}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{16\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

(2)

If
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}
with
p\geq 3
, then
f^{1}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
and
f^{3}\cong\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{2}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{p}
. By
6.17
, we can realize
f
by

\mathcal{R}\cong\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-h_{0}h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}}

and
\mathcal{R}
-module
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{1}h_{2})
where
h_{0}=2\mathsf{x}+y
,
h_{1}=x
,
h_{2}=y
and
h_{3}=x+\uplambda p\mathsf{y}^{p-1}
. Since
M^{1}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{1})\oplus(u,h_{1}h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{1}h_{0}h_{2})
, then by
3.10

{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{1})
can be presented by
\mathcal{Q}
with relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}b_{1}-\mathsf{y}b_{1}=2b_{1}b_{0}a_{0},\quad b_{2}\mathsf{x}-b_{2}\mathsf{y}=2(a_{3}b_{3}b_{2}-\uplambda pb_{2}\mathsf{y}^{p-1}),

\displaystyle a_{1}\mathsf{x}-a_{1}\mathsf{y}=2b_{0}a_{0}a_{1},\quad\mathsf{x}a_{2}-\mathsf{y}a_{2}=2(a_{2}a_{3}b_{3}-\uplambda p\mathsf{y}^{p-1}a_{2}),

together with additional relations factoring through the vertex
0
,
which will not play a role in the following.
Hence
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{1})
can be presented by
Q
with relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}b_{1}-\mathsf{y}b_{1}=0,\quad b_{2}\mathsf{x}-b_{2}\mathsf{y}=-2\uplambda pb_{2}\mathsf{y}^{p-1},

\displaystyle a_{1}\mathsf{x}-a_{1}\mathsf{y}=0,\quad\mathsf{x}a_{2}-\mathsf{y}a_{2}=-2\uplambda p\mathsf{y}^{p-1}a_{2}.

Thus
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f^{1})
where
f^{1}\cong\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}^{2}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{y}^{2}-2\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{p}
. Normalizing by applying
a_{1}\mapsto-\sqrt{2}a_{1}
and
a_{2}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}a_{2}
to
f^{1}
gives

f^{1}\mapsto\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}-2^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{p}.

Setting
p=2
in the above potential proves the statement about
f^{1}
in (1). The proof of the statement about
f^{3}
in (1) is similar.

For
p\geq 3
and
\uplambda\neq 0
, applying
a_{1}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}b_{2},b_{1}\mapsto a_{2},a_{2}\mapsto 2b_{1},b_{2}\mapsto a_{1}
gives

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}-2^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{p}\rightsquigarrow\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}-2^{1+\frac{p}{2}}\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{p}.

Then since
p\geq 3
and
\uplambda\neq 0
, by
6.16
(2),

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}-2^{1+\frac{p}{2}}\uplambda\mathsf{x}^{p}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}.

Therefore, for
p\geq 3
we obtain
(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p})^{1}=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}
.
Since flopping is an involution, this is equivalent to the statement in (2). The proof of the statement about
f^{3}
in (2) is similar.

Then we finally prove the statement about
f^{2}
in (1). In this case,
g_{0}=2\mathsf{x}+y
,
g_{1}=x
,
g_{2}=y
and
g_{3}=x+2\uplambda\mathsf{y}
. Since
M^{2}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,h_{0})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{2})\oplus(u,h_{0}h_{2}h_{1})
, then by
3.10

{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{2})
can be presented by
\mathcal{Q}
with relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}b_{1}+2\mathsf{y}b_{1}=b_{1}b_{0}a_{0},\quad 2\uplambda b_{2}\mathsf{x}+b_{2}\mathsf{y}=a_{3}b_{3}b_{2},

\displaystyle a_{1}\mathsf{x}+2a_{1}\mathsf{y}=b_{0}a_{0}a_{1},\quad 2\uplambda\mathsf{x}a_{2}+\mathsf{y}a_{2}=a_{2}a_{3}b_{3},

plus some other relations that factor through the vertex
0
(and so will not be relevant below).
Hence
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{2})
can be presented by
Q
with relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{x}b_{1}+2\mathsf{y}b_{1}=0,\quad 2\uplambda b_{2}\mathsf{x}+b_{2}\mathsf{y}=0,

\displaystyle a_{1}\mathsf{x}+2a_{1}\mathsf{y}=0,\quad 2\uplambda\mathsf{x}a_{2}+\mathsf{y}a_{2}=0.

Thus
\underline{\End}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{2})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f^{2})
where
f^{2}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{16\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}
.
∎

Now let
\uppi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
be a crepant resolution where
\mathcal{R}
is cDV, with exceptional curves
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{C}_{i}
. For any curve class
\upbeta\in\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}\left\langle\mathrm{C}_{i}\right\rangle
, there exists a integer-valued number
\textnormal{GV}_{\upbeta}(\uppi)
called the Gopakumar–Vafa (GV for short) invariant of class
\upbeta

[
BKL
,
MT
]
.

Definition 6.26
.

Define the
GV set
of
\uppi
as
\textnormal{GV}(\uppi)=\{\textnormal{GV}_{\upbeta}(\uppi)|\text{ all }\upbeta\text{ with }\textnormal{GV}_{\upbeta}(\uppi)\neq 0\}
.

Lemma 6.27
.

Let
\uppi_{k}\colon\mathcal{X}_{k}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{k}
be two crepant resolutions of isolated
cDV
singularities
\mathcal{R}_{k}
for
k=1,2
. If
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is derived equivalent to
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
, then
\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{1})=\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{2})
.

Proof.

Since
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is derived equivalent to
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
and each
\mathcal{R}_{k}
is isolated, then
\mathcal{R}_{1}\cong\mathcal{R}_{2}
by
3.6
, so
\uppi_{1}
and
\uppi_{2}
are two crepant resolutions of a same cDV singularity and connected by a sequence of flops. Thus
\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{1})=\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{2})
by
[
NW
, 5.4]
,
[
V4
, 5.10]
.
∎

For
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
, set

\operatorname{Orbit}(\uplambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\uplambda,\frac{1-4\uplambda}{4},\frac{1}{4(1-4\uplambda)},\frac{\uplambda}{4\uplambda-1},\frac{4\uplambda-1}{16\uplambda},\frac{1}{16\uplambda}\right\}.

This notation is purely definitional at this stage and records the parameters arising from the classification in
6.28
.

Theorem 6.28
.

The following groups the Type
A
potentials on
Q
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra into sets, where all the Jacobi algebras in a given set are derived equivalent.

(1)

\{\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda^{\prime}\mathsf{y}^{2}\mid\uplambda^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Orbit}(\uplambda)\}
for
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,\tfrac{1}{4}\}
.

(2)

\{\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{2}
,
\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
,
\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}\}
for
p\geq 3
.

(3)

\{\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}
,
\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}\}
for
p\geq 3
and
q\geq 3
.

Moreover, the Jacobi algebras of the sets in
(1)–(3)
are all mutually not derived equivalent, and in particular the Jacobi algebras of different sets in the same item are not derived equivalent.
In
(1)
there are no further basic algebras in the derived equivalence class, whereas in
(2)–(3)
there are an additional finite number of basic algebras in the derived equivalence class.

Proof.

By
6.19
and
3.5
, the potentials in the statement are precisely the Type
A
potentials on
Q
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra, thus they exhaust all possibilities.

Firstly, we prove that the Jacobi algebras in each given set are derived equivalent. By
3.7
, given a Type A potential
f
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra and
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
, if we want to obtain all the basic algebras that are derived equivalent to
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, we only need to calculate all iterated flops from
\uppi
. So we consider
f^{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
in the following.

(1) Suppose that
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
where
\uplambda\neq 0,\frac{1}{4}
.

By
6.25
,
f^{1}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+(\frac{1}{4}-\uplambda)\mathsf{y}^{2}\cong f^{3}
and
f^{2}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{16\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}
. Repeating the same argument, we have
f^{(12)}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4(1-4\uplambda)}\mathsf{y}^{2}
,
f^{(21)}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{4\uplambda-1}{16\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}
and
f^{(121)}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{\uplambda}{4\uplambda-1}\mathsf{y}^{2}
. Repeating this process, only six numbers appear, so by
3.7
there are no further basic algebras in this derived equivalence class.

(2) Suppose that
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}+\mathsf{x}^{p}
where
p\geq 3
.

By
6.25
,
f^{1}\cong\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
and
f^{3}\cong\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{2}
, and thus the three potentials in the statement are derived equivalent.
Since
p\geq 3
, then
f^{(12)}
,
f^{(13)}
,
f^{(31)}
and
f^{(32)}
are not potentials on
Q
by
6.24
, and so there are additional basic algebras in this derived equivalence class.

(3) By
6.19
,
\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}\cong\mathsf{x}^{q}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{p}
, and thus the two potentials in the statement are derived equivalent. Suppose that
f=\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\mathsf{y}^{q}
. Since
p\geq 3
and
q\geq 3
, then
f^{1}
,
f^{2}
and
f^{3}
are not on
Q
by
6.24
, and so there are additional basic algebras in this derived equivalence class.

The wall–chamber decomposition of the movable cone for a
cA_{3}
crepant resolution is governed by the type
A_{3}
root system (see e.g.
[
W2
, 5.24, §7]
). These chambers are precisely the Weyl chambers, so their number equals the order of the Weyl group, namely
\#W(A_{3})=|S_{4}|=24
.
Each chamber corresponds to a crepant resolution.

Moreover, the double
A_{3}
quiver
Q
admits a natural involution that sends

e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}
,
e_{2}\mapsto e_{2}
, and
e_{3}\mapsto e_{1}

(equivalently, exchanging
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
in the potentials).
This symmetry identifies certain Jacobi algebras, so that there are at most

12
distinct isomorphism classes. Consequently, the number of additional basic algebras appearing in the derived equivalence classes in cases (2) and (3) of
6.28
is finite.

Secondly, we prove that the Jacobi algebras in different sets in (1)–(3) are all mutually not derived equivalent.

Given any potential
f
in the statement, by
6.17
we can find a Type

A_{3}
crepant resolution
\uppi
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
. By Toda’s formula (see
[
T
, §4.4]
), the GV set of each set in (1)–(3) is:

➀

{
1,1,1,1,1,1
},

➁

{
1,1,1,p-1,1,1
},

➂

{
1,1,1,p-1,q-1,1
}.

Suppose that
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are potentials in the statement with
f_{1}\simeq f_{2}
and each
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{i})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{i})
, where
\uppi_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
is a Type
A_{3}
crepant resolution. Then
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})
is derived equivalent to
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})
. Since each
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f_{i})
is finite-dimensional, then each
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is isolated by
3.5
, and so
\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{1})=\textnormal{GV}(\uppi_{2})
by
6.27
. So if we want to prove that two potentials are not derived equivalent, we only need to prove that their corresponding GV sets are not equal.

Since
p\geq 3
, then any GV set in ① is different from that in ②, and so any set of potentials in (1) is not derived equivalent to that in (2). Since
q\geq 3
, then any GV set in ② is different from that in ③, and so any set of potentials in (2) is not derived equivalent to that in (3). Similar for (1) and (3).

Next, consider two sets of potentials in the same item. Given a potential
f
in (1), we have already exhausted all
6
potentials that are derived equivalent to
f
in the above proof. Thus by
3.7
, different sets of potentials in (1) are not derived equivalent. Since different GV sets in ② are not equal, different sets of potentials in (2) are not derived equivalent. Similar for (3).
∎

Remark 6.29
.

It is usually hard to give the derived equivalence class of an algebra
A
.
But when
A
is
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
for a Type A potential
f
on
Q_{n,I}
, there is a Type
A_{n}
crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
such that
A\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi)
by
5.12
. If further
A
is finite-dimensional over
\mathbb{C}
, then
\mathcal{R}
is isolated by
3.5
. So we can apply
3.7
to get the
full
derived equivalence class of
A
by calculating all iterated flops from
\uppi
.
This is why we restrict this subsection to Type A potentials on
Q
with finite-dimensional Jacobi algebra.

Remark 6.30
.

In cases
(2)
and
(3)
of
6.28
, there are additional basic algebras in the derived equivalence class. These algebras are isomorphic to the Jacobi algebras of some potentials on
Q_{3,I}
where
I\neq\emptyset
(see the proof in
6.28
).

Recall the quaternion type quiver algebra
A_{p,q}(\upmu)
from
[
E2
,
H2
]
.
It is defined as the completion of the path algebra of the quiver
Q

\scriptstyle 1
\scriptstyle 2
\scriptstyle 3
\scriptstyle b_{1}
\scriptstyle a_{1}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle a_{2}

subject to the relations

a_{1}a_{2}b_{2}-(a_{1}b_{1})^{p-1}a_{1},b_{2}b_{1}a_{1}-\upmu(b_{2}a_{2})^{q-1}b_{2},a_{2}b_{2}b_{1}-(b_{1}a_{1})^{p-1}b_{1},b_{1}a_{1}a_{2}-\upmu(a_{2}b_{2})^{q-1}a_{2},

where
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}
and
p,q\geq 2
.
Note that we impose fewer relations than in
[
E2
]
, since we work in the completed path algebra.
Equivalently,
A_{p,q}(\upmu)\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)
, where

f=\frac{1}{p}\mathsf{x}^{p}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{\upmu}{q}\mathsf{y}^{q}\;\cong\;\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+(-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\upmu\,\mathsf{y}^{q}.

For convenience, set
B_{p,q}(\uplambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)
where
f=\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{q}
.
Then
A_{p,q}(\upmu)\cong B_{p,q}\!\big((-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\upmu\big)
.
Denote
B_{p,q}(\uplambda)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(Q,f)
where
f=\mathsf{x}^{p}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{q}
. Thus
A_{p,q}(\upmu)\cong B_{p,q}((-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\upmu)
.

The following refines and extends the results of Erdmann and Holm
[
E2
,
H2
]
.

Similar to
6.28
, for
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
we set a different orbit

\operatorname{Orbit}(\upmu)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\upmu,1-\upmu,\frac{1}{1-\upmu},\frac{\upmu}{\upmu-1},\frac{\upmu-1}{\upmu},\frac{1}{\upmu}\right\},

which again serves as a convenient notation for the parameters occurring in the classification
6.31
below.

Corollary 6.31
.

The following groups those algebras
A_{p,q}(\upmu)
which are finite-dimensional into sets, where all the algebras in a given set are derived equivalent.

(1)

\{A_{2,2}(\upmu^{\prime})\mid

\upmu^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Orbit}(\upmu)\}
for
\upmu\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

(2)

\{A_{p,q}(1),A_{q,p}(1)\}
for
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
.

Moreover, the algebras of the sets in
(1)–(2)
are all mutually not derived equivalent.
In
(1)
there are no further basic algebras in the derived equivalence class, whereas in
(2)
there are an additional finite number of basic algebras in the derived equivalence class.

Proof.

Using
A_{p,q}(\upmu)\cong B_{p,q}\!\big((-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\upmu\big)
, in particular
A_{2,2}(\upmu)\cong B_{2,2}(\upmu/4)
,
it follows from
6.19
that the algebras listed in the statement are precisely the finite-dimensional ones, up to isomorphism.
We now show that the algebras within each set are derived equivalent.

(1) Since

A_{2,2}(\upmu)\cong B_{2,2}(\frac{\upmu}{4})=\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}\!\left(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{\upmu}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2}\right),

the claim follows from
6.28
(1). The same reference also shows that there are no further basic algebras in this derived equivalence class.

(2)
If
(p,q)\neq(2,2)
, then by the proof of
6.16
(3) we have

B_{p,q}\!\big((-1)^{q}p^{-\frac{q}{p}}q^{-1}\big)\cong B_{p,q}(1)
, hence
A_{p,q}(1)\cong B_{p,q}(1)
, and similarly
A_{q,p}(1)\cong B_{q,p}(1)
.
Therefore the stated pair is derived equivalent by
6.28
(2)(3), which also implies that only finitely many further basic algebras occur in this derived equivalence class.

Finally, the algebras of the sets in (1)–(2) are all mutually not derived equivalent by
6.28
.
∎

Geometric interpretation of the
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-symmetry.

We now explain that the orbit structures appearing in
6.28
and
6.31
,
which were introduced there in a purely ad hoc manner,
admit a uniform conceptual interpretation in terms of the
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-action
arising from elliptic curves in Legendre form.
Conceptually, this reflects the fact that both the flopping geometry of
cA_{3}
singularities and the Legendre form of elliptic curves are governed by configurations of four points on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, up to projective transformation. We now make this connection precise.

We begin by recalling some standard facts about elliptic curves; see
[
H1
, §4.4]
. Any elliptic curve
E
over
\mathbb{C}
can be written in Legendre form

E\colon y^{2}=x(x-1)(x-\uplambda),

where
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
. This realises
E
as a double cover
E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
branched over the four points
0,\;1,\;\uplambda,\;\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The
j
-invariant of
E
is defined by

\displaystyle j(\uplambda)=2^{8}\frac{(\uplambda^{2}-\uplambda+1)^{3}}{\uplambda^{2}(\uplambda-1)^{2}}.

(6.N)

Two elliptic curves over
\mathbb{C}
are isomorphic if and only if they have the same
j
-invariant, and every element of
\mathbb{C}
arises as the
j
-invariant of some elliptic curve.

There is a natural action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
, defined as follows. Given
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
, we permute the three points
0,1,\uplambda
according to an element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}
, and then apply a linear fractional transformation of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
sending the first two back to
0
and
1
.
The image of the third point defines
\sigma(\uplambda)
.
The resulting
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-orbit of
\uplambda
consists of

\uplambda,\ 1-\uplambda,\ \frac{1}{1-\uplambda},\ \frac{\uplambda}{\uplambda-1},\ \frac{\uplambda-1}{\uplambda},\ \frac{1}{\uplambda}.

The group
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
is generated by the transformations

\uplambda\mapsto 1-\uplambda\quad\text{and}\quad\uplambda\mapsto\frac{1}{\uplambda}.

Elements lying in the same orbit determine isomorphic elliptic curves, and each orbit coincides with a fibre of the
j
-invariant.

To relate the elliptic curve parameter
\uplambda
to our setting, we consider the Type
A
potential

f\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{x}^{2}-\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{2}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\cong\;\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2},\quad\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\},

which is precisely the potential
A_{2,2}(\uplambda)
appearing in
6.31
.
The restriction
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
agrees with the parameter range for elliptic curves in Legendre form.

By
6.17
the potential
f
is realised by the crepant resolution
\uppi\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
where

\mathcal{R}=\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-(x-y)xy(x-\uplambda y)},

with associated
\mathcal{R}
-module
M=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,x-y)\oplus(u,(x-y)x)\oplus(u,(x-y)xy)
. Since
\uplambda\neq 0,1
, the singularity
\mathcal{R}
is isolated by
6.19
.
By
3.9
,
\mathcal{X}
is given pictorially by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{X}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to127.45pt{\vbox to29.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.32314pt\lower-13.27272pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

If we evaluate the ratios
x/y
at the defining linear factors
x-y,\ x,\ y,\ x-\uplambda y
, we obtain the values
1,\ 0,\ \infty,\ \uplambda
, which coincide exactly with the branch points of the elliptic curve in Legendre form.

We now follow the notation of
6.22
and analyse how flops of
\mathcal{X}
act on the parameter
\uplambda
.
For
1\leq i\leq 3
, we write
f^{i}
for the Type
A
potential whose Jacobi algebra is isomorphic to the contraction algebra
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{i})
of the crepant resolution
\uppi^{i}
obtained by flopping the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{i}
.
When
\mathcal{R}
is isolated (equivalently,
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
is finite-dimensional), by
3.7
we have the derived equivalence of potentials
f^{i}\simeq f
.

Consider the crepant resolution
\uppi^{1}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
obtained by flopping the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}
. The associated
\mathcal{R}
-module is
M^{1}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,x)\oplus(u,x(x-y))\oplus(u,x(x-y)y)
, and the corresponding contraction algebra satisfies
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{1})
and
f\simeq f^{1}
.
By
3.9
,
\mathcal{X}^{1}
is given pictorially by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{X}^{1}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to121.73pt{\vbox to29.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.60037pt\lower-13.27272pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}

Applying the linear transformation of variables
\varphi_{1}\colon x\mapsto x-y,\ y\mapsto-y,\ u\mapsto-u
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{1}
, where

\mathcal{S}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-(x-y)xy(x-(1-\uplambda)y)}.

Let
N_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}M^{1}
. Then
N_{1}\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}\oplus(u,x-y)\oplus(u,(x-y)x)\oplus(u,(x-y)xy)
, and the corresponding crepant resolution
\uppi_{1}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{1}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{1}
is identified with
\uppi^{1}
under the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}
. In particular,

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{1})
. By
3.9
,
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
is given pictorially by

Thus, by
6.17
, we have

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}\big(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}(1-\uplambda)\mathsf{y}^{2}\big)
.
Since
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{1})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{1})
, it follows that

f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\simeq\;\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}(1-\uplambda)\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\cong\;f^{1}.

Thus, flopping the curve
\mathrm{C}_{1}
induces the transformation
\uplambda\mapsto 1-\uplambda
on the parameter.

By the symmetry of the exceptional curves
\mathrm{C}_{1}
and
\mathrm{C}_{3}
, an entirely analogous argument applies to the flop of
\mathrm{C}_{3}
.
Consequently, we obtain

f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\simeq\;\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}(1-\uplambda)\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\cong\;f^{3}.

At the level of parameters, this corresponds to the transformation
\uplambda\mapsto 1-\uplambda
.

Consider the crepant resolution
\uppi^{2}\colon\mathcal{X}^{2}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{R}
obtained by flopping the exceptional curve
\mathrm{C}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}
. The associated
\mathcal{R}
-module is
M^{2}=\mathcal{R}\oplus(u,x-y)\oplus(u,(x-y)y)\oplus(u,(x-y)yx)
, and the corresponding contraction algebra satisfies
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{2})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{2})
and
f\simeq f^{2}
.
By
3.9
,
\mathcal{X}^{2}
is given pictorially by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{X}^{2}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to127.45pt{\vbox to29.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.32314pt\lower-13.27272pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Since
\uplambda\neq 0
, applying the linear transformation of variables
\varphi_{2}\colon x\mapsto y,\ y\mapsto x,\ u\mapsto\uplambda u
induces an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{2}
, where

\mathcal{S}_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{\mathbb{C}[\kern-1.0pt[u,v,x,y]\kern-1.0pt]}{uv-(x-y)xy(x-\frac{1}{\uplambda}y)}.

Let
N_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}M^{2}
.
Then
N_{2}\cong\mathcal{S}_{2}\oplus(u,x-y)\oplus(u,(x-y)x)\oplus(u,(x-y)xy)
,
and the corresponding crepant resolution

\uppi_{2}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{2}\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathcal{S}_{2}

is identified with
\uppi^{2}
under the induced isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\cong\mathcal{S}_{2}
.
In particular,
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})\cong\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{2})
. By
3.9
,
\mathcal{Y}_{2}
is given pictorially by

Thus, by
6.17
, we obtain

\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi_{2})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}\big(\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}\big)
.
Since
\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}(\uppi^{2})\cong\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f^{2})
, it follows that

f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\simeq\;\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{\uplambda}\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\cong\;f^{2}.

At the level of parameters, this corresponds to the transformation

\uplambda\mapsto\frac{1}{\uplambda}
.

Note that the above provides an alternative proof of
6.25
(1).
Recall from the proof of
6.28
that, in order to obtain all basic algebras derived equivalent to
\mathcal{J}\mathrm{ac}(f)
, it suffices to consider all iterated flops of the crepant resolution
\uppi
.

As shown above, flopping the exceptional curves
\mathrm{C}_{1}
,
\mathrm{C}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{C}_{3}
induces the transformations

\uplambda\mapsto 1-\uplambda

and
\uplambda\mapsto\frac{1}{\uplambda}
, which generate the full
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-action on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
. Consequently, the derived equivalence class of the potential
f=\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}
consists precisely of the six-element orbit

\left\{\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda^{\prime}\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\middle|\;\uplambda^{\prime}\in\{\uplambda,\;1-\uplambda,\;\frac{1}{1-\uplambda},\;\frac{\uplambda}{\uplambda-1},\;\frac{\uplambda-1}{\uplambda},\;\frac{1}{\uplambda}\}\right\},

where
\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

Therefore, in both the elliptic curve setting and the potential-theoretic setting, the parameter
\uplambda
is acted on by a permutation of the distinguished points followed by a linear transformation of coordinates.
In each case this induces the same group action on
\uplambda
, generated by

\uplambda\mapsto 1-\uplambda\quad\text{and}\quad\uplambda\mapsto\frac{1}{\uplambda}.

Consequently, elliptic curves and Type
A
potentials have identical orbits in the parameter space.
Therefore we give a result parallel to the classical description for elliptic curves; see
[
H1
, §4.4]
.

Corollary 6.32
.

Let

F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\;\middle|\;\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}\right\},

and for
f\in F
define

j(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}j(\uplambda)
,
where
j(\uplambda)
is given by (
6.N
).
Then:

(1)

the value
j(f)
depends only on the derived equivalence class of
f
;

(2)

two potentials
f,f^{\prime}\in F
are derived equivalent if and only if
j(f)=j(f^{\prime})
;

(3)

every element of
\mathbb{C}
occurs as the
j
-invariant of some potential in
F
.

Consequently, there is a one-to-one correspondence between derived equivalence classes of potentials in
F
and elements of
\mathbb{C}
, given by
f\mapsto j(f)
.
Moreover, the fibre over
j(\uplambda)
consists precisely of the
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-orbit

\{\uplambda,\;1-\uplambda,\;\frac{1}{1-\uplambda},\;\frac{\uplambda}{\uplambda-1},\;\frac{\uplambda-1}{\uplambda},\;\frac{1}{\uplambda}\}.

It is natural to ask whether the function

F\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad f\mapsto j(f),

admits a natural extension to the boundary values
\uplambda\in\{0,1\}
.
Equivalently, one may ask whether this correspondence extends to a map

\bar{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\uplambda\mathsf{y}^{2}\mid\uplambda\in\mathbb{C}\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}.

From the geometric perspective developed above, this question reduces to understanding whether the potentials

\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\ \text{and }\mathsf{x}^{2}+\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{y}^{2},

which arise from crepant resolutions of non-isolated
cA_{3}
singularities (see
6.19
), are derived equivalent.

7.
Appendix

The purpose of this section is to prove
7.17
, which gives a quiver presentation (
7.A
) of
\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
.
This is used to prove the geometric realization in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{GR}
.

We first introduce the reduction system and Diamond Lemma.
For a quiver
Q
, we denote the set of paths of degree
i
by
Q_{i}
where the degree is with respect to the path length, and write
Q_{\geq i}=\bigcup_{j\geq i}Q_{j}
for the set of paths of degree
\geq i
.

Definition 7.1
.

[
B1
, §1]

Given a field
k
, a
reduction system

R
for the path algebra
kQ
is a set of pairs

R=\left\{\left(s,\varphi_{s}\right)\mid s\in S\text{ and }\varphi_{s}\in kQ\right\}

where

(1)

S
is a subset of
Q_{\geq 2}
such that
s
is not a sub-path of
s^{\prime}
when
s\neq s^{\prime}\in S
.

(2)

For all
s\in S
,
s
and
\varphi_{s}
have the same head and tail.

(3)

For each pair
(s,\varphi_{s})\in R
,
\varphi_{s}
is
irreducible
, meaning we can write
\varphi_{s}=\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}p_{i}
where each
\uplambda\in k^{\times}
, and each
p_{i}
does not contain elements in
S
as a sub-path.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
(s,\varphi_{s})\in R
and let
q,\ r
be two paths such that
qsr\neq 0
in
kQ
. Following
[
CS
, §2]
a
basic reduction

\mathfrak{r}_{q,s,r}:kQ\rightarrow kQ
is defined as the
k
-linear map uniquely determined by the following: for any path
p

\mathfrak{r}_{q,s,r}(p)=\begin{cases}q\varphi_{s}r&\text{ if }p=qsr\\
p&\text{ if }p\neq qsr\end{cases}

Sometimes we write
p\rightarrow q\varphi_{s}r
instead of
\mathfrak{r}_{q,s,r}(p)=q\varphi_{s}r
for simplicity.

Definition 7.3
.

A
reduction

\mathfrak{r}
is defined as a composition
\mathfrak{r}_{q_{n},s_{n},r_{n}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{q_{2},s_{2},r_{2}}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{q_{1},s_{1},r_{1}}
of basic reductions for some
n\geq 1
. We say a path
p
is
reduction-finite
if for any infinite sequence of reductions
(\mathfrak{r}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, we have
\mathfrak{r}_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{2}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{1}(p)=\mathfrak{r}_{n_{0}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{2}\circ\mathfrak{r}_{1}(p)
.

A path may contain many sub-paths in
S
, so one may obtain different elements in
kQ
after performing different reductions.

Definition 7.4
.

[
B1
, §1]

Let
R
be a reduction system for
kQ
. A path
pqr\in Q_{\geq 3}
for
p,q,r\in Q_{\geq 1}
is an
overlap ambiguity
of
R
if
pq,\ qr\in S
.
We say that an overlap ambiguity
pqr
with
pq=s
and
qr=s^{\prime}
is
resolvable
if
\varphi_{s}r
and
p\varphi_{s^{\prime}}
are reduction-finite and
\mathfrak{r}(\varphi_{s}t)=\mathfrak{r^{\prime}}(p\varphi_{s^{\prime}})
for some reductions
\mathfrak{r},\ \mathfrak{r^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 7.5
.

(Diamond Lemma)

[
B1
, 1.2]

Let
R=\{(s,\varphi_{s})\}_{s\in S}
be a reduction system for
kQ
. Let
I=\left\langle s-\varphi_{s}\right\rangle_{s\in S}\subset kQ
be the corresponding two-sided ideal and write
A=kQ/I
for the quotient algebra. If
R
is reduction-finite, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

All overlap ambiguities of
R
are resolvable.

(2)

The image of the set of irreducible paths under the projection
kQ\rightarrow A
forms a
k
-basis of
A
.

Consider the following quiver
Q
with relations
I
.

1
2
3
\ldots\ldots
n-1
n
0
\scriptstyle b_{0}
\scriptstyle a_{0}
\scriptstyle b_{1}
\scriptstyle a_{1}
\scriptstyle b_{2}
\scriptstyle a_{2}
\scriptstyle b_{n-1}
\scriptstyle a_{n-1}
\scriptstyle a_{n}
\scriptstyle b_{n}
\scriptstyle l_{0,0},l_{0,1},\dots,l_{0,n}
\scriptstyle l_{1,0},l_{1,1},\dots,l_{1,n}
\scriptstyle l_{2,0},l_{2,1},\dots,l_{2,n}
\scriptstyle l_{3,0},l_{3,1},\dots,l_{3,n}
\scriptstyle l_{n-1,0},l_{n-1,1},\dots,l_{n-1,n}
\scriptstyle l_{n,0},l_{n,1},\dots,l_{n,n}

I\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}l_{t,i}a_{t}=a_{t}l_{t+1,i},\ l_{t+1,i}b_{t}=b_{t}l_{t,i},\ l_{t,i}l_{t,j}=l_{t,j}l_{t,i},\\
l_{t,t}=a_{t}b_{t},\ l_{t+1,t}=b_{t}a_{t}\text{ for any }t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)\text{ and }0\leq i,j\leq n.\end{cases}

(7.A)

Then define the reduction system
R
for the path algebra
kQ
to be

\displaystyle R\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(l_{t,i}a_{t},a_{t}l_{t+1,i}),(l_{t+1,i}b_{t},b_{t}l_{t,i}),(a_{t}b_{t},l_{t,t}),(b_{t}a_{t},l_{t+1,t}),(l_{tj}l_{ti},l_{ti}l_{tj})\mid

\displaystyle\textnormal{ for any }0\leq i\leq n,\ t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)\text{ and }j>i\}.

(7.B)

We next prove that
R
is reduction-finite and all overlap ambiguities of
R
are resolvable.

Lemma 7.6
.

The reduction system
R
(
7.B
) is reduction-finite.

Proof.

For any path
p
and any infinite sequence of reductions
(\mathfrak{r}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, if there does not exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have
\mathfrak{r}_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{1}(p)=\mathfrak{r}_{n_{0}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{r}_{1}(p)
, then there must exist infinite basic reductions that can be applied to
p
consecutively. We prove that this is impossible. There are three types of path pairs in
R
:

(1)

(a_{t}b_{t},l_{t,t}),(b_{t}a_{t},l_{t+1,t})
.

(2)

(l_{t,i}a_{t},a_{t}l_{t+1,i}),(l_{t+1,i}b_{t},b_{t}l_{t,i})
.

(3)

(l_{t,j}l_{t,i},l_{t,i}l_{t,j})
for
j>i
.

The type
(1)
basic reduction decreases the path degree by one.
The type
(2)
basic reduction moves
a_{t}
or
b_{t}
one step left, and
l_{t,i}
or
l_{t+1,i}
one step right in the path.
Similarly, the type
(3)
basic reduction moves
l_{t,i}
one step left, and
l_{t,j}
one step right in the path for
j>i
.

Thus, any composition of these three types either decreases the path degree or moves
a_{t}
,
b_{t}
to the left,
l_{t,j}
with the larger
j
to the right. Since the path degree of
p
is finite, we can only apply the basic reductions of these three types to
p
finitely many times.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

All overlap ambiguities of the reduction system
R
(
7.B
) are resolvable.

Proof.

There are seven types of overlap ambiguities in the reduction system
R
(
7.B
), namely

l_{t,i}a_{t}b_{t},\quad l_{t+1,i}b_{t}a_{t},\quad l_{t,j}l_{t,i}a_{t},\quad l_{t+1,j}l_{t+1,i}b_{t},\quad a_{t}b_{t}a_{t},\quad b_{t}a_{t}b_{t},\quad l_{t,j}l_{t,i}l_{t,k},

for
0\leq i\leq n
,
t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)
and
j>i>k
.
We now verify that each of these ambiguities is resolvable.

(1)

When
t<i
,

(l_{t,i}a_{t})b_{t}\rightarrow a_{t}(l_{t+1,i}b_{t})\rightarrow(a_{t}b_{t})l_{t,i}\rightarrow l_{t,t}l_{t,i}
, and

l_{t,i}(a_{t}b_{t})\rightarrow l_{t,i}l_{t,t}\rightarrow l_{t,t}l_{t,i}
.
The case of
t\geq i
is similar.

(2)

When
t<i
,

(l_{t+1,i}b_{t})a_{t}\rightarrow b_{t}(l_{t,i}a_{t})\rightarrow(b_{t}a_{t})l_{t+1,i}\rightarrow l_{t+1,t}l_{t+1,i}
, and

l_{t+1,i}(b_{t}a_{t})\rightarrow l_{t+1,i}l_{t+1,t}\rightarrow l_{t+1,t}l_{t+1,i}.

The case of
t\geq i
is similar.

(3)

(l_{t,j}l_{t,i})a_{t}\rightarrow l_{t,i}(l_{t,j}a_{t})\rightarrow(l_{t,i}a_{t})l_{t+1,j}\rightarrow a_{t}l_{t+1,i}l_{t+1,j}
,

l_{tj}(l_{t,i}a_{t})\rightarrow(l_{t,j}a_{t})l_{t+1,i}\rightarrow a_{t}(l_{t+1,j}l_{t+1,i})\rightarrow a_{t}l_{t+1,i}l_{t+1,j}
.

(4)

(l_{t+1,j}l_{t+1,i})b_{t}\rightarrow l_{t+1,i}(l_{t+1,j}b_{t})\rightarrow(l_{t+1,i}b_{t})l_{t,j}\rightarrow b_{t}l_{t,i}l_{t,j}
,

l_{t+1,j}(l_{t+1,i}b_{t})\rightarrow(l_{t+1,j}b_{t})l_{t,i}\rightarrow b_{t}(l_{t,j}l_{t,i})\rightarrow b_{t}l_{t,i}l_{t,j}
.

(5)

(a_{t}b_{t})a_{t}\rightarrow l_{t,t}a_{t}\rightarrow a_{t}l_{t+1,t}
, and
a_{t}(b_{t}a_{t})\rightarrow a_{t}l_{t+1,t}
.

(6)

(b_{t}a_{t})b_{t}\rightarrow l_{t+1,t}b_{t}\rightarrow b_{t}l_{t,t}
, and
b_{t}(a_{t}b_{t})\rightarrow b_{t}l_{t,t}
.

(7)

(l_{t,j}l_{t,i})l_{t,k}\rightarrow l_{t,i}(l_{t,j}l_{t,k})\rightarrow(l_{t,i}l_{t,k})l_{t,j}\rightarrow l_{t,k}l_{t,i}l_{t,j}
,

l_{t,j}(l_{t,i}l_{t,k})\rightarrow(l_{t,j}l_{t,k})l_{t,i}\rightarrow l_{t,k}(l_{t,j}l_{t,i})\rightarrow l_{t,k}l_{t,i}l_{t,j}
. ∎

Proposition 7.8
.

Consider the quiver
Q
with relations
I
(
7.A
) and its reduction system
R
in (
7.B
). Then, the set of irreducible paths (with respect to
R
) of
kQ
under the projection
kQ\rightarrow kQ/I
forms a
k
-basis of
kQ/I
.

Proof.

It is clear that the two-sided ideal generated by
R
(see
7.5
) coincides with
I
(
7.A
). Since
R
is reduction-finite and all overlap ambiguities of
R
are resolvable by
7.6
and
7.7
, the statement holds by
7.5
.
∎

Notation 7.9
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)
, consider the following subsets of the set of paths on
Q
with head
t
.

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{a_{t-i}\dots a_{t-2}a_{t-1}\mid\text{ all }i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

(2)

\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{b_{t+i-1}\dots b_{t+1}b_{t}\mid\text{ all }i\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

(3)

\mathcal{L}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{l_{t,0}^{i_{1}}l_{t,1}^{i_{2}}\dots l_{t,n}^{i_{n}}\mid\text{ all }i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\}
.

(4)

\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{pq\mid\text{ all }p\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\text{ and }q\in\mathcal{L}_{t}\}
.

(5)

\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{pq\mid\text{ all }p\in\mathcal{B}_{t}\text{ and }q\in\mathcal{L}_{t}\}
.

(6)

Then write
k\mathcal{A}_{t}
,
k\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
k\mathcal{L}_{t}
for the
k
-span of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}
and
\mathcal{L}_{t}
respectively.

(7)

For any
A\in k\mathcal{A}_{t}
, write
(A)_{t-1}
for the unique element in
k\mathcal{A}_{t-1}\oplus ke_{t-1}
such that
A=(A)_{t-1}a_{t-1}
. Here the summand
ke_{t-1}
accounts for the case where
A=a_{t-1}
.

(8)

For any
B\in k\mathcal{B}_{t}
, write
(B)_{t+1}
for the unique element in
k\mathcal{B}_{t+1}\oplus ke_{t+1}
such that
B=(B)_{t+1}b_{t}
. Here the summand
ke_{t+1}
accounts for the case where
B=b_{t}
.

(9)

For any
L\in k\mathcal{L}_{t}
and
0\leq s\leq n
, write
(L)_{s}
for the unique element in
k\mathcal{L}_{s}
, which is obtained by replacing
l_{t,0},l_{t,1},\dots,l_{t,n}
in
L
by
l_{s,0},l_{s,1},\dots,l_{s,n}
.

We next describe all irreducible paths in
Q
, with respect to the reduction system
R
(
7.B
).

Proposition 7.10
.

For any path
p
with head
t
in
Q
,

p\text{ is irreducible }\iff p\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\cup\mathcal{B}_{t}\cup\mathcal{L}_{t}\cup\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}\cup\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}.

Proof.

By the reduction system
R
(
7.B
), it is clear that each path in
\mathcal{A}_{t},\mathcal{B}_{t},\mathcal{L}_{t},\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t},\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}
is irreducible. We next prove the other direction.
Since the head of
p
is
t
,
p
either ends with
a_{t-1}
,
b_{t}
or
l_{t,i}
for some
i
. The proof splits into cases.

(1)
p
ends with
a_{t-1}
.

Write
p=qa_{t-1}
for some
q
with head
t-1
. Then
q
either ends with
a_{t-2}
,
b_{t-1}
or
l_{t,i}
for some
i
. However, if
q
either ends with
b_{t-1}
or
l_{t,i}
, then
qa_{t-1}
is reducible by
R
(
7.B
). Thus
q
can only end with
a_{t-2}
. Repeating the same process gives
p\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
.

(2)
p
ends with
b_{t}
.

Similar to (1), we can prove that
p\in\mathcal{B}_{t}
.

(3)
p
ends with
l_{t,i}
.

Write
p=ql_{t,i}
for some
q
with head
t
. Then
q
either ends with
a_{t-1}
,
b_{t}
or
l_{t,j}
for some
j
. If
q
ends with
a_{t-1}
, then
q\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
by (1), and so
p\in\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}
. Similarly, if
q
ends with
b_{t}
, then
p\in\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}
. If
q
ends
l_{t,j}
, then
j\leq i
; otherwise, it will contradict the irreducibility of
ql_{t,i}
. Repeating the same process gives
p\in\mathcal{L}_{t},\ \mathcal{A}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}
or
\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathcal{L}_{t}
.
∎

We next apply
7.8
and
7.10
to prove the exactness of a particular complex in
7.12
.
In the following, we write
P_{t}
for the
k
-span of the paths with head
t
in
kQ/I
(
7.A
).

Lemma 7.11
.

The
k
-linear maps

\displaystyle m_{l_{t,n}}\colon P_{t}\rightarrow P_{t},\quad m_{a_{t}}\colon P_{t}\rightarrow P_{t+1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad f\mapsto fl_{t,n}\quad\qquad f\mapsto fa_{t}

are injective for any
t\in\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)
.

Proof.

We only prove
m_{l_{0,n}}
and
m_{a_{0}}
and are injective, the other cases are similar.
Since the reduction system
R
(
7.B
) is reduction-finite by
7.6
, we can assume
f\in P_{0}
is irreducible.

(1)
m_{l_{0,n}}
is injective.

We first write
f=\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}p_{i}
as a linear combination of irreducible paths where each
\uplambda_{i}\in k
.
Since
p_{i}
is irreducible and there are no paths in
S
(
7.B
) that end with
l_{0,n}
,
p_{i}l_{0,n}
is also irreducible. Thus if
fl_{0,n}=\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}p_{i}l_{0,n}=0
, then each
\uplambda_{i}=0
by
7.8
, and so
f=0
.

(2)
m_{a_{0}}
is injective.

Since
f\in P_{0}
, by
7.10
we can write
f
as a linear combination of irreducible terms

f=\uplambda A+\upmu B+\upbeta L+\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}A_{i}L_{i}+\sum_{j}\upmu_{j}B_{j}L_{j},

where each
\uplambda,\upmu,\upbeta,\uplambda_{i},\upmu_{j}\in k
, and
A,A_{i}\in k\mathcal{A}_{0}
, and
B,B_{j}\in k\mathcal{B}_{0}
, and
L,L_{i},L_{j}\in k\mathcal{L}_{0}
. Thus

\displaystyle fa_{0}

\displaystyle=\uplambda Aa_{0}+\upmu Ba_{0}+\upbeta La_{0}+\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}A_{i}L_{i}a_{0}+\sum_{j}\upmu_{j}B_{j}L_{j}a_{0}

\displaystyle=\uplambda Aa_{0}+\upmu(B)_{1}b_{0}a_{0}+\upbeta La_{0}+\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}A_{i}L_{i}a_{0}+\sum_{j}\upmu_{j}(B_{j})_{1}b_{0}L_{j}a_{0}

(since
B=(B)_{1}b_{0}
and
B_{j}=(B_{j})_{1}b_{0}
)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\uplambda Aa_{0}+\upmu(B)_{1}l_{1,0}+\upbeta a_{0}(L)_{1}+\sum_{i}\uplambda_{i}A_{i}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}+\sum_{j}\upmu_{j}(B_{j})_{1}l_{1,0}(L_{j})_{1}.

(7.C)

By
7.10
, each term in (
7.C
) is irreducible. We next claim that each term in (
7.C
) differs from the others.

Since
A_{i}L_{i}
are different for different
i
,
A_{i}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}
are different for different
i
. Similarly,
(B_{j})_{1}l_{1,0}(L_{j})_{1}
are different for different
j
.
Since
\mathrm{deg}(A_{i})\geq 1
,
A_{i}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}
is different from
a_{0}(L)_{1}
for each
i
. Similarly,
(B_{j})_{1}l_{1,0}(L_{j})_{1}
is different from
(B)_{1}l_{1,0}
for each
j
. Thus we proved the claim.

So by
7.8
the terms in (
7.C
) descend to give basis elements of
kQ/I
. Thus if
fa_{0}=0
, then each
\uplambda,\upmu,\upbeta,\uplambda_{i},\upmu_{j}
is zero, and so
f=0
. Thus
m_{a_{0}}
is injective.
∎

Proposition 7.12
.

0\rightarrow P_{0}\xrightarrow[d_{4}]{(a_{0},b_{n})}P_{1}\oplus P_{n}\xrightarrow[d_{3}]{\begin{pmatrix}l_{1,n}&-b_{0}b_{n}\\
-a_{n}a_{0}&l_{n,0}\end{pmatrix}}P_{1}\oplus P_{n}\xrightarrow[d_{2}]{\begin{pmatrix}b_{0}\\
a_{n}\end{pmatrix}}P_{0}\xrightarrow[d_{1}]{}k[l_{0,1},l_{0,2},\dots,l_{0,n-1}]\rightarrow 0

is an exact sequence of
k
-linear maps in
\mathrm{k}Q/I
(
7.A
).

Proof.

This sequence is a chain complex from the relations
I
(
7.A
). The exactness at the last three indices is from
[
W1
, §6]
. By
7.11
, we have
d_{4}
is injective, and thus this complex is exact at the first index.
So we only need to prove that
\ker d_{3}\subseteq\operatorname{im}d_{4}
. It suffices to prove that, for any
(f,g)\in P_{1}\oplus P_{n}
,

fl_{1,n}=ga_{n}a_{0}\Rightarrow(f,g)=(ha_{0},hb_{n})\text{ for some }h\in P_{0}.

Since the reduction system
R
(
7.B
) is reduction-finite by
7.6
, we can assume that
f
and
g
are irreducible. Since
f
is irreducible and there are no paths in
S
(
7.B
) that end with
l_{1,n}
, then
fl_{1,n}
is also irreducible. Since

g\in P_{n}
, by
7.10
we can write
g
as a linear combination of irreducible terms

g=A+B+\sum_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}l_{n,i}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j\geq 1}A_{ij}K_{ij}l_{n,i}+\sum_{s\geq 1}B_{s}J_{s},

where each
A,A_{ij}\in k\mathcal{A}_{n}
, and
B,B_{s}\in k\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and
L_{i},K_{ij},J_{s}\in k\mathcal{L}_{n}
. Since
L_{i}l_{n,i}
is irreducible,
L_{i}\in k\langle l_{n,0},\dots,l_{n,i}\rangle
for each
i
. Similarly,
K_{ij}\in k\langle l_{n,0},l_{n,1},\dots,l_{n,i}\rangle
for each
i
and
j
.

Write

B=\uplambda b_{n}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{s}=\upmu_{s}b_{n}+B_{s,1}b_{0}b_{n}

for some
\uplambda,\upmu_{s}\in k
and

B_{0,1},B_{s,1}\in k\mathcal{B}_{1}\oplus ke_{1}
(see
7.9
(8)), where
s\geq 1
. Thus

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=A+\uplambda b_{n}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}l_{n,i}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j\geq 1}A_{ij}K_{ij}l_{n,i}+\sum_{s\geq 1}(\upmu_{s}b_{n}+B_{s,1}b_{0}b_{n})J_{s}

\displaystyle=A+\uplambda b_{n}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}l_{n,i}+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j\geq 1}A_{ij}K_{ij}l_{n,i}+\sum_{s\geq 1}\upmu_{s}b_{n}J_{s}+\sum_{s\geq 1}B_{s,1}b_{0}b_{n}J_{s}.

Multiplying the
g
above on the right by
a_{n}a_{0}
,
ga_{n}a_{0}
equals

\displaystyle Aa_{n}a_{0}+\uplambda b_{n}a_{n}a_{0}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}a_{n}a_{0}+\sum_{i}L_{i}l_{n,i}a_{n}a_{0}+\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}K_{ij}l_{n,i}a_{n}a_{0}

\displaystyle+\sum_{s}\upmu_{s}b_{n}J_{s}a_{n}a_{0}+\sum_{s}B_{s,1}b_{0}b_{n}J_{s}a_{n}a_{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow Aa_{n}a_{0}+\uplambda a_{0}l_{1,n}+B_{0,1}l_{1,0}l_{1,n}+\sum_{i}a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}+\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{s}\upmu_{s}a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}+\sum_{s}B_{s,1}l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}

(7.D)

(since
b_{0}b_{n}J_{s}a_{n}a_{0}\rightarrow b_{0}b_{n}a_{n}a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}\rightarrow b_{0}l_{0,n}a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}\rightarrow b_{0}a_{0}l_{1,n}(J_{s})_{1}\rightarrow l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}
)

\displaystyle=Aa_{n}a_{0}+\uplambda a_{0}l_{1,n}+B_{0,1}l_{1,0}l_{1,n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}+a_{n}a_{0}(L_{n})_{1}l_{1,n}+\sum_{j}A_{nj}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{nj})_{1}l_{1,n}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{j}A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}+\sum_{s}\upmu_{s}a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}+\sum_{s}B_{s,1}l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}

\displaystyle=Aa_{n}a_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{j}A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}+\mathsf{f}_{1}l_{1,n},

(7.E)

where we set
\mathsf{f}_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\uplambda a_{0}+B_{0,1}l_{1,0}+a_{n}a_{0}(L_{n})_{1}+\sum_{j}A_{nj}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{nj})_{1}+\sum_{s}\upmu_{s}a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}+\sum_{s}B_{s,1}l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}
.

We claim that each term in (
7.D
) is irreducible. To see this, we consider the terms in (
7.D
) separately.

(1)

By the reduction system
R
(
7.B
)
Aa_{n}a_{0}
is irreducible.

(2)

Since
l_{1,n},l_{1,0}l_{1,n}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, by
7.10

a_{0}l_{1,n}
and
B_{0,1}l_{1,0}l_{1,n}
are irreducible.

(3)

Since
L_{i}\in k\langle l_{n,0},\dots,l_{n,i}\rangle
,
(L_{i})_{1}\in k\langle l_{1,0},\dots,l_{1,i}\rangle
, so
(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}\in k\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Thus
a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}
is irreducible by
7.10
.

(4)

Since
K_{ij}\in k\langle l_{n,0},\dots,l_{n,i}\rangle
,
(K_{ij})_{1}\in k\langle l_{1,0},\dots,l_{1,i}\rangle
, so
(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}\in k\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Thus
A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}
is irreducible by
7.10
.

(5)

Since
(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n},l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}\in k\mathcal{L}_{1}
, by
7.10

a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}
and
B_{s,1}l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}
are irreducible.

We next claim that all terms in (
7.D
) are pairwise distinct.

First, each term of the form
a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}
ends with
l_{1,i}
, hence these terms are distinct for different values of
i
.
Similarly, since the paths
A_{ij}K_{ij}l_{n,i}
are distinct for different pairs
(i,j)
, the corresponding terms

A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}
are also distinct.
Likewise, the terms
a_{0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}
and
B_{s,1}l_{1,0}(J_{s})_{1}l_{1,n}
are distinct for different
s
.
Therefore, the terms appearing within each individual sum are pairwise different.

Next, we compare terms coming from different sums.
Since
\mathrm{deg}(A_{ij})\geq 1
, the path
A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}
differs from
a_{n}a_{0}
, and hence

A_{ij}a_{n}a_{0}(K_{ij})_{1}l_{1,i}
cannot coincide with
a_{n}a_{0}(L_{i})_{1}l_{1,i}
.
By the same reasoning, terms arising from different summands in (
7.D
) are mutually distinct.
This proves the claim.

Since (
7.E
) is obtained by combining the terms in (
7.D
) that end with
l_{1,n}
, each term in (
7.E
) is also irreducible and differs from the others. So the terms in (
7.E
) descend to give different basis elements of
kQ/I
by
7.8
.

Recall that
fl_{1,n}=ga_{n}a_{0}
and
fl_{1,n}
is irreducible. Since only
\mathsf{f}_{1}l_{1,n}
ends with
l_{1,n}
in
ga_{n}a_{0}
(
7.E
), then all terms in
ga_{n}a_{0}
except
\mathsf{f}_{1}l_{1,n}
are zero. So

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\uplambda b_{n}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}+L_{n}l_{n,n}+\sum_{j}A_{nj}K_{nj}l_{n,n}+\sum_{s}(\upmu_{s}b_{n}+B_{s,1}b_{0}b_{n})J_{s}

\displaystyle=\uplambda b_{n}+B_{0,1}b_{0}b_{n}+L_{n}a_{n}b_{n}+\sum_{j}A_{nj}K_{nj}a_{n}b_{n}+\sum_{s}(\upmu_{s}+B_{s,1}b_{0})(J_{s})_{0}b_{n}

(since
l_{n,n}=a_{n}b_{n}
and
b_{n}J_{s}=(J_{s})_{0}b_{n}
)

\displaystyle=hb_{n}.

(set
h\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\uplambda+B_{0,1}b_{0}+L_{n}a_{n}+\sum_{j}\uplambda_{nj}A_{nj}K_{nj}a_{n}+\sum_{s}(\upmu_{s}+B_{s,1}b_{0})(J_{s})_{0}
)

Thus
ga_{n}a_{0}=hb_{n}a_{n}a_{0}=ha_{0}l_{1,n}
. Together with
fl_{1,n}=ga_{n}a_{0}
gives
fl_{1,n}=ha_{0}l_{1,n}
, and so
f=ha_{0}
by
7.11
. Thus
(f,g)=(ha_{0},hb_{n})
, proving the claim.
∎

With the exact sequence in
7.12
, we can calculate the vector space dimension of each graded degree piece of
P_{t}
in (
7.F
), which will be used to prove the isomorphism in
7.17
.

Notation 7.13
.

In the following, we adopt a new definition of degree of
Q
(
7.A
), which differs from path length in
2.1
(4).

(1)

Define
\mathrm{deg}(a_{i})=\mathrm{deg}(b_{i})=1
and
\mathrm{deg}(l_{t,i})=2
for each
i
and
t
.

(2)

With respect to this degree, write
P_{t,d}
for the graded piece of degree
d
of
P_{t}
.

(3)

Write
D_{d}
for the vector space dimension of
P_{0,d}
.

By the symmetry of the quiver
Q
and relations
I
(
7.A
),
D_{d}
is also the vector space dimension of
P_{t,d}
for
1\leq t\leq n
.
By
7.12
, for any integer
d
, there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow P_{0,d}\rightarrow P_{1,d+1}\oplus P_{n,d+1}\rightarrow P_{1,d+3}\oplus P_{n,d+3}\rightarrow P_{0,d+4}\rightarrow T_{d+4}\rightarrow 0,

where
T_{d+4}
denotes the degree
d+4
piece of
k[l_{0,1},l_{0,2},\dots,l_{0,n-1}]
. Thus

D_{d}-2D_{d+1}+2D_{d+3}-D_{d+4}+E_{d+4}=0

(7.F)

where
E_{d+4}=\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{k}T_{d+4}
.

By definition of the grading,
P_{t,d}=0
for all
t
and all
d<0
, hence
D_{d}=0
for
d<0
.
Moreover, the exact sequence above and (
7.F
) both hold for
d<0
.

Notation 7.14
.

We next define

\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{k[u,v,x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]}{uv-x_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n}},

(7.G)

and consider the
\mathcal{S}
-module
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}N_{i}
where
N_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}
and
N_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(u,\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}x_{j})
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

We will show that
kQ/I
(
7.A
) presents
\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
.
By
[
IW2
]
, every morphism in
\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
can be obtained as a linear combination of compositions of the following maps.

(7.H)

Thus there exists an obvious surjective homomorphism
kQ\twoheadrightarrow\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
. Since
I
gets sent to zero by inspection, this induces a surjective homomorphism
\psi\colon kQ/I\twoheadrightarrow\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
. We will show that
\psi
is an isomorphism, by counting graded pieces.

Notation 7.15
.

Grade
\mathcal{S}
via
\mathrm{deg}(u)=\mathrm{deg}(v)=n+1
and
\mathrm{deg}(x_{0})=\dots=\mathrm{deg}(x_{n})=2
. The particular choice of the graded shift of
N
given by
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}N_{i}(-i)
induces a grading in
\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
, which explicitly grades each arrow in (
7.H
) as follows.

(7.I)

Notation 7.16
.

Parallel to the notation
7.13
, we adopt the following notation.

(1)

Set
Q_{t}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Hom_{\mathcal{S}}(N,N_{t}(-t))
for
0\leq t\leq n
.

(2)

With respect to (
7.I
), write
Q_{t,d}
for the degree
d
graded piece of
Q_{t}
.

(3)

Write
D^{\prime}_{d}
for the vector space dimension of
Q_{0,d}
.

By the symmetry of (
7.H
),
D^{\prime}_{d}
is also the vector space dimension of
Q_{t,d}
for
1\leq t\leq n
.

By
[
B2
]
, we have the following exact sequence,

0\rightarrow N_{0}\xrightarrow{(x_{0},u)}N_{1}\oplus N_{n}\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}x_{n}&-u\\
-\frac{x_{0}x_{n}}{u}&x_{0}\end{pmatrix}}N_{1}\oplus N_{n}\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}\scriptstyle inc\\
\frac{x_{n}}{u}\end{pmatrix}}N_{0}\rightarrow 0

Using the grading in
7.16
, the above exact sequence becomes

0\rightarrow N_{0}\xrightarrow{d_{4}}N_{1}(-1)\oplus N_{n}(-n)\xrightarrow{d_{3}}N_{1}(-1)\oplus N_{n}(-n)\xrightarrow{d_{2}}N_{0}\rightarrow 0.

(7.J)

where each
d_{i}
is homogeneous, and further
\mathrm{deg}(d_{4})=1=\mathrm{deg}(d_{2})
and
\mathrm{deg}(d_{3})=2
.
Applying
\Hom_{\mathcal{S}}(N,-)
to (
7.J
) induces the following exact sequence,

0\rightarrow Q_{0}\xrightarrow{d_{4}}Q_{1}\oplus Q_{n}\xrightarrow{d_{3}}Q_{1}\oplus Q_{n}\xrightarrow{d_{2}}Q_{0}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{con}}\cong k[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n-1}]\rightarrow 0,

which is parallel to the one in
7.12
. Thus for any integer
d
, there is an exact sequence

0\rightarrow Q_{0,d}\rightarrow Q_{1,d+1}\oplus Q_{n,d+1}\rightarrow Q_{1,d+3}\oplus Q_{n,d+3}\rightarrow Q_{0,d+4}\rightarrow T_{d+4}^{\prime}\rightarrow 0,

where
T_{d+4}^{\prime}
denotes the degree
d+4
piece of
k[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n-1}]
. Thus

D^{\prime}_{d}-2D^{\prime}_{d+1}+2D^{\prime}_{d+3}-D^{\prime}_{d+4}+E^{\prime}_{d+4}=0

(7.K)

where
E_{d+4}^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{dim}}_{k}T_{d+4}^{\prime}
.

Similarly to (
7.F
), by definition of the grading we have
Q_{t,d}=0
for all
t
and all
d<0
, hence
D^{\prime}_{d}=0
for
d<0
.
Moreover, the exact sequence above and (
7.K
) both hold for
d<0
.

Proposition 7.17
.

With notation as above, the homomorphism
\psi
induces an isomorphism
kQ/I\xrightarrow{\sim}\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
.

Proof.

With the notation above,
\psi
is a graded surjective homomorphism, so it suffices to show that
D_{d}=D_{d}^{\prime}
for all
d\geq 0
.
Using (
7.F
), (
7.K
), and the equality
E_{d}=E^{\prime}_{d}
for all
d
, together with the fact that

D_{d}=0=D^{\prime}_{d}
for
d<0
, we deduce by induction that
D_{d}=D^{\prime}_{d}
for all
d\geq 0
.

For completeness, we also verify the cases
0\leq d\leq 3
explicitly.
By
7.8
and
7.10
, the vector space
P_{0,d}
admits a
\mathbb{C}
-basis given by irreducible paths as follows:

(1)

For
d=0
, a basis of
P_{0,0}
is
\{e_{0}\}
, hence
D_{0}=1
.

(2)

For
d=1
, a basis of
P_{0,1}
is
\{a_{n},b_{0}\}
, hence
D_{1}=2
.

(3)

For
d=2
, a basis of
P_{0,2}
is

\{a_{n-1}a_{n},\;b_{1}b_{0},\;l_{0,0},l_{0,1},\dots,l_{0,n}\},

hence
D_{2}=n+3
.

(4)

For
d=3
, a basis of
P_{0,3}
is

\{a_{n-2}a_{n-1}a_{n},\;b_{2}b_{1}b_{0},\;a_{n}l_{0,0},\dots,a_{n}l_{0,n},\;b_{0}l_{0,0},\dots,b_{0}l_{0,n}\},

hence
D_{3}=2n+4
.

For each
0\leq d\leq 3
, the elements listed above form a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
P_{0,d}
.
Their images under the correspondence in (
7.H
) lie in
Q_{0,d}
and are linearly independent.
Therefore,
D^{\prime}_{d}\geq D_{d}
for
0\leq d\leq 3
.
Since
\psi\colon kQ/I\twoheadrightarrow\End_{\mathcal{S}}(N)
is surjective, it follows that

D^{\prime}_{d}=D_{d}
for
0\leq d\leq 3
.
Combining the base cases with the inductive step completes the proof.
∎
